#5852. PSEG Power plans 200-MW nuclear uprate as company eyes data center deals

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

But one of the units at the Salem plant in New Jersey, which is jointly owned with Constellation Energy, faces an extended outage to deal with degraded equipment, according to federal filings.

PSEG Power and Constellation Energy are planning a 200-MW uprate at their jointly owned Salem nuclear power plant in southern New Jersey, according to an earnings presentation by Public Service Enterprise Group, PSEG Power’s parent company.

Salem’s current installed capacity is 2,280 MW. PSEG Power would have a 112-MW share of the uprate and Constellation would own the remainder, which would support a recently announced nuclear-focused power purchase agreement with Google.

PSEG Power’s roughly 3,760-MW nuclear fleet ran at a 95.5% capacity factor in the third quarter, up from 92.4% in the same period last year, according to the presentation.

However, there has been a snag at the Salem plant, PSEG said Monday in its quarterly filing with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission.

During a scheduled refueling outage at Salem 1, a visual inspection last month revealed that the thermal shield inside the reactor vessel is degraded, according to the filing.

“We have initiated further testing, which is ongoing and is expected to extend the refueling outage,” PSEG said. “The impact of the outage on Salem 1 will depend on the timing and results of the inspection and necessary repairs.”

Meanwhile, PSEG continues to explore potential deals with data center developers centered around PSEG Power’s roughly 3.8-GW nuclear fleet, according to Ralph LaRossa, PSEG chair, president and CEO.

“PSEG Power is continuing to develop terms with large load entities for contracting arrangements involving existing nuclear production, future nuclear upgrades and other generation opportunities, similar to recently announced power purchase agreements in the PJM region and specifically in New Jersey,” he said during the earnings call.

FERC’s recent decision to reject key portions of the PJM Interconnection proposed plan for interconnection data centers and other large loads “reinforces that the focus has shifted to the states to address retail curtailment and cost issues associated with large load customers,” LaRossa said.

Public Service Electric and Gas Co., a PSEG utility, is working with the administration of New Jersey Gov. Mikie Sherrill, D, and the state’s utility regulators to limit electric cost increases for consumers and consider new utility regulatory models, according to LaRossa.

PSE&G, for example, plans to ask the New Jersey Board of Public Utilities to approve a plan for recovering the costs for investments made since its last distribution rate case, LaRossa said.

“Our filing … is all about staying aligned with the governor's goals to keep rates flat for as long as possible,” he said. The utility expects to file the proposal by the end of the year, PSEG said in its SEC filing.

Meanwhile, the BPU’s study on modernizing New Jersey's electric utility business model is now in its second phase, LaRossa noted.

“This initiative is focusing on cost discipline, financing modernization, targeted incentives and shared savings and staged performance-based ratemaking,” he said.

Those and other options mentioned in the Phase 1 report, such as multi-year rate plans, performance-based rates, performance incentive metrics, earnings sharing mechanisms, decoupling and shared saving mechanisms, provide “constructive paths to strengthen the connection between customer value, cost accountability and timely recovery of prudent investment,” LaRossa said.

PSE&G may have opportunities to invest in solar and grid readiness for distributed resources, but the extent of it could hinge on the state allowing utilities to own generating assets, according to LaRossa.

“I think the policy is not yet certain here in New Jersey as to whether or not it will move in a regulated direction or stay solely competitive,” he said.

On the financial front, PSEG’s third-quarter income fell to $376 million, or 76 cents/share, down from $622 million, or $1.25/share a year ago, mainly on mark-to-market losses at PSEG Power, the Newark, New Jersey-based company said in its SEC filing.

Third-quarter revenue slipped to $3 billion from $3.2 billion in the same period last year.

#5849. The Future of Buildings in Illinois Is Electric

The Illinois Commerce Commission authorized a study of the most affordable way for the state to meet its building decarbonization goals. The report has fundamental flaws but critical insights.

Illinois’s landmark Climate and Equitable Jobs Act (CEJA) sets the goal of 100 percent clean energy across the state by 2050. For nonelectric sources of emissions and transportation—such as the 17 percent of emissions that occur when we burn gas to heat our homes and other buildings where we live, work, and play—CEJA provides little direction on how these sectors are expected to align with the state’s climate goals.

The Illinois Commerce Commission (ICC), which regulates the state’s electric and gas utilities, showed real leadership in 2023 when it found that “if the decarbonization goals of CEJA are to be met, the gas distribution system as currently operated will need to change.” To chart that change, the ICC launched a Future of Gas (FOG) open stakeholder process to identify the most efficient, cost-effective path to a gas system that fits the state’s climate goals.

As part of this FOG process, the ICC authorized a consultant to model five pathways to that CEJA-compliant system by 2050. In its recently released Gas Decarbonization Pathways Study, the consultant modeled a range of scenarios, from one where the state invests heavily in energy efficiency and helps customers replace outdated gas appliances with high-efficiency electric alternatives to others that rely on unproven biofuels with no clear evidence that they can be produced at anything close to the scale needed. For each scenario, the consultant was expected to assess technical feasibility, up-front and operating costs, impacts on the gas and electric systems, emissions reductions, and effects on environmental justice communities, all to help Illinois meet its gas decarbonization goals at the lowest cost and with the most benefits to residents.

Unfortunately, the consultant’s final report is biased by faulty (and at times, plainly unrealistic) assumptions that do not allow for accurate comparison across scenarios. Despite this, the study yields a clear finding: Gas use in Illinois’s buildings and industrial facilities must decline by at least 50 percent in order to reach the state’s climate target of net-zero emissions by 2050. Together with the state’s recently released Comprehensive Climate Action Plan, the study offers Illinois policymakers a clear agenda for initiating the transition to an affordable, equitable, and clean energy future.

The study offers some useful insights, but treating all of its findings as a road map for Illinois’s gas transition would be a mistake. Before getting into the no-regrets steps that Illinois can take now, we need to unpack the biggest problems with the report, including the flawed assumptions behind some of its conclusions.

Despite the study’s shortcomings detailed above, several key findings should influence the recommendations in the final FOG report:

The remaining technologies are incredibly early-stage, and to the extent that they are available at all over the next several decades, they are likely to be extremely expensive. The study’s assumptions around e-methane are particularly troubling. Modeling initially showed that the Continued Gas Use and Hybrid Gas cases could not meet the net-zero-by-2050 emissions requirement using only available technologies, like biomethane. As a result, in the final report, the model selected e-methane to backfill any resource gap—despite the fact that e-methane derived from green hydrogen and direct air carbon capture is still in “prototype and demonstration stage” today.

While the pathways study acknowledges that “[h]igher-gas scenarios present … greater challenges in alternative fuel procurement, technology readiness, long-term affordability, and air quality” compared to electrification-focused scenarios, this is a significant understatement. Scenarios relying on e-methane are infeasible and should not be considered on equal footing with scenarios that primarily rely on electric heat pumps, which already comprise 47 percent of air conditioner sales and have annually outsold gas furnaces since 2021.

The pathways study reinforces the need for Illinois to proactively plan for a future where gas demand declines, which occurs under all five scenarios despite flawed assumptions that heavily favor continued gas use scenarios. The state can and must act now to achieve the state’s climate goals in an affordable and equitable way. Illinois does not need to solve every aspect of the transition in the next few years. However, it can begin taking the following no-regrets steps now to prepare for a changing energy landscape.

Despite its numerous flaws, the study underscores the transformative role that clean, electric heat pumps will play in Illinois’s energy future and the need to plan for a smaller gas system. The ICC and the state legislature can and must begin advancing no-regrets building electrification policies today to ensure tomorrow’s energy system is healthy, clean, and affordable for all of the state’s residents.

By Madeline Semanisin, Policy Director, Illinois, Climate & Energy, NRDC; and Kiki Velez, Equitable Gas Transition Lead, Climate & Energy, NRDC

Featured photo by Diego Alberto Martínez Mendoza

#5848. Energy affordability tops voter concerns in election set to shake up Congress

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“History shows us that energy crises create policy openings, and we think the conditions are probably in place for a window of policymaking,” Hasan Nazar, head of policy at Crux, told Utility Dive.

The outcome of Tuesday’s midterm elections could have serious implications for utilities, as candidates in both parties seek to capitalize on widespread concerns about energy affordability.

Policy areas that could see the biggest impact include data centers, energy tax credits and permitting reform,  experts told Utility Dive.

Hasan Nazar, head of policy at Crux, a finance technology company that connects tax credit buyers and sellers, said he believes energy affordability will be “determinative” in this election.

Polls have given Democrats a strong chance of taking control of the House and possibly also flipping the Senate, but even if they do, Nazar noted that there would still be a split government with a Republican administration in place. He hopes broad support for reform will lead to bipartisan cooperation on energy policy.

“History shows us that energy crises create policy openings, and we think the conditions are probably in place for a window of policymaking,” Nazar said.

All U.S. House seats are up for grabs, along with one-third of the Senate and 36 gubernatorial seats.

An Oct. 8 poll from Pew Research Center found that 74% of U.S. adults surveyed were concerned about the price of gasoline and energy, the largest share of concern among all surveyed issues.

As candidates campaigned this year, energy issues were central in many races.

Dylan Reed, Advanced Energy United’s managing director overseeing federal and political affairs, said Pennsylvania’s race is also significant given the role that Gov. Shapiro has played in the ongoing debate over the PJM Interconnection’s surging prices.

In 2025, Shapiro was one of the PJM state governors who threatened to pull out of the grid operator’s markets.

“Affordability is the message that we see out here very clearly,” Reed said. “I think when you are looking at high prices when it comes to your utility bill, the number one office that can impact that right now is the governor.”

Nazar said he thinks that following the election, in Congress “there’s probably going to be at least a discussion about new kinds of tax credit incentives that could be deployed.”

In 2022, then-Vice President Kamala Harris cast the tie-breaking vote in the Senate to pass the Inflation Reduction Act, which received no Republican votes and established long-horizon tax credits for clean energy along with new tax credit mechanisms like transferability.

In 2025, a Republican-controlled Congress passed the OBBBA, which shortened the horizon for some of the IRA’s clean energy benefits. Now, if Democrats take back control, they could seek to revise the OBBBA’s impacts, observers say.

“A flipped Congress where both houses are with the Democrats could have a dramatic turn on the energy policy landscape, and particularly with federal energy tax credits,” said Heather Cooper, a partner at the law firm McDermott Will & Schulte. “We have seen pretty dramatic flip-flopping in the last five years as Congress has gone back and forth.”

However, Cooper — who advises clients on federal income tax issues related to the energy transition — said she thinks the clean energy industry is prepared for the possibility of future flip-flopping.

“Most of the players who are in this market … The reality is all of their business models are kind of used to a world where these tax credits have 1, 2, 3, 4-year horizons,” she said.

The IRA, which created a longer runway for the first time, resulted in a “phenomenal amount of money poured into this industry,” she added.

Carl Fleming, global co-head of McDermott Will & Schulte’s energy and project finance group, said, “A number of folks have already worked out scenarios where some of these projects pencil out regardless of tax credits.”

“I do think you'll probably see an extension of the solar credit,” he said. “Solar took a good hit under OB3, but with rising energy costs, I think all that stuff's on the table because if I'm running for office or trying to get back into office, and I can find a technology that can help bring those energy costs down … whatever it takes to kind of get these energy costs down, that’s going to be on the table.”

Some utility executives have suggested that the furor over data centers and affordability could die down after the elections.

Fleming said he thinks there may be a more fundamental shift happening. He pointed to Texas’ pause on data center grid interconnections, aimed at weeding out speculative requests, as a potential policy model.

“You could question whether [the pause was] driven by the midterms or just normal processes, but I think that's going to be the way folks will look at this at the state level going forward — taking a kind of investment-type approach to these projects,” he said.

“It's no longer a race to power; it's a race to bankable power,” he continued. “Data centers are coming along so fast, much faster than people had anticipated. It's tough for legislation to keep up with.”

Reed said that the question of how to achieve speed to power, while preserving grid reliability and affordability, is what “every policymaker should be asking.”

Nazar said that post-election, he sees the chance for “durable” bipartisan legislation.

“I think it is actually quite an interesting opportunity next year to have a necessarily bipartisan approach to these policies, where energy is top of mind for voters,” he said. “I think the [Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act] is a good signal of potentially a return to cross-party substantive, serious and impactful energy policymaking that I could foresee carrying into next year.”

The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act aims to speed federal permitting for energy and infrastructure projects, protect permits for approved projects and expand the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission’s power to eliminate obstacles to new transmission. It was announced in September, the result of careful negotiation between Republicans and Democrats.

But bill co-sponsor Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse, D-R.I., warned in October that the deal could be “[blown] up” by the Trump administration’s moves to revoke federal permits for offshore wind farms.

The bill has also been criticized by some consumer and clean energy advocates who say it would favor oil and gas projects, weaken environmental laws, undermine state and Tribal authority and limit the judiciary’s ability to intervene to prevent harm.

Nazar said he thinks the potential for BAAJA to be amended “seems like that's an ongoing conversation that appears to be in play.”

“That said, I think we will see how the dynamics shift after tomorrow, if they shift at all,” he added.

According to Crux’s conversations with developers, Nazar said, “Predictability is the most important thing they could ask for from the federal government.”

#5846. Donated solar project powers community center in unincorporated New Mexico village  Solar Power World’s Projects of Impact

A building that started as a one-room schoolhouse has through the decades expanded to become a lifeline for a New Mexico community far from any city infrastructure, and a recent solar installation has brought new resilience to this place.

Children run outside of Ojo Sarco, New Mexico’s one-room schoolhouse in a photo from January 1943. The structure still stands today as the Ojo Sarco Community Center. Credit: John Collier, Jr./Library of Congress

The structure housing the Ojo Sarco Community Center (OSCC) was founded in the early 20th century. Ojo Sarco is an unincorporated community home to about 300 people that is located about 43 miles northeast from Santa Fe.

“We have no political voice, really,” said Carol Miller, a grant writer and board member for the Ojo Sarco Community Center. “We’re pretty neglected. We’ve lost resources in the past. We were the first wave of people to lose our post office many years ago. Everybody has to commute pretty much for everything.”

That commute is about 40 minutes round trip for basic amenities, she said.

The community center has tried to fill in those resource gaps with services like a twice-monthly food bank, summer meal programs for kids, safety classes and outings for its residents, such as a night at the opera. The OSCC also recently met all the qualifications to be a state library.

PPC Solar, also known as Paradise Power Company, from Taos, built a 7.5-kW solar project paired with 10.6-kWh of LFP energy storage at the OSCC. The contractor built the array at no-cost to the community center. Every year PPC donates a solar array to a regional nonprofit organization, and after three years of applying, the OSCC was awarded the project.

CEO Daniel Weinman’s father founded Paradise Power Company in the 1970s, and the company has installed solar in off-the-grid communities since the start. Its PV donation program is built out of that tradition.

“We’ve always wanted to find a way to give back to the community,” Weinman said. “The best thing that we found — and we’ve been doing it for over a decade now — is doing an annual nonprofit donation system … We’ve had farming organizations, land conservation organizations, and it goes on and on. Really, it’s about finding the right fit. We wanted to make a good impact for this community center, and we felt resiliency was the best way to do that.”

Solar Power World spoke to both PPC Solar and organizers with the OSCC about what effect this solar project has had in the village since it was built in fall 2025.

Larry Miller, project manager and OSCC board member: I think it’s a transformative project for our small community. We like to talk about the history of the community center, and now this is part of it. If someone doesn’t notice the solar panels on the roof, we point it out to them. It’s integrated into our lives here.

Carol Miller: It’s lowered our electric costs, which is really important for us, because we’re such a shoe-string operation. We’ve had some fun talks with the community because they all know we’ll have the charging capacity. We still have internet available, even with the power out. All the kids that come to our library programs, they’re solar oriented. The benefit of this company and its staff just has ripple effects throughout our greater community.

PPC Solar donated a solar plus storage project to the Ojo Sarco Community Center. This array will keep power running in this facility that has become a haven for Ojo Sarco residents. Credit: PPC Solar

Preston Bastardo, sales and design, PPC Solar: The building itself had some challenges with mounting the array on the roof, but that just took some exploration in the attic and making sure that we could land things where we needed to. The building has two large areas, and so fo installation of batteries had some restrictions, there was a good mechanical closet that we were able to use for batteries. Then as far as backup circuits, we did a partial facility backup where we had a critical loads panel next to their main panel. I think everything went pretty smoothly.

Weinman: Well, it really goes back to our roots. In 1972, there was no technology supporting solar at the time. My dad was a pioneer in the industry and he really developed his own controls and safety protections and found used panels and used batteries and was bringing power to places where there was no power. Our house, for example, we started with kerosene lanterns. We didn’t have electricity until we had a solar power system, and it was really life changing.

Being able to build on that legacy and look at our community in that light and be able to offer systems like this to communities that need it — I think that really adds to that legacy and really is profound in a lot of ways. It feels great to be able to do this and be in a position where we can help others realize and experience the benefits of solar power. We want to continue to participate in any way we can.

Carol Miller: We’re a very old community and proud of it. And having worked with local businesses and our electrical co-op, we know it’s really the exception and not the rule because we own our power. Any technology that is shared equally in a low-income community has ripple effects beyond what I think some of us imagined a number of years ago. It’s for everyone.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5845. Congress Is Cutting Off Transportation Investments that Help Families Save Money

The loss of guaranteed federal funding could stall projects that make it easier and cheaper for Americans to get where they need to go.

UPDATE: On October 27, 2026, an update to the Senate Commerce Committee’s Chopping Block report provided a state-by-state assessment of the harm to transportation systems if Congress fails to renew the Bipartisan Infrastructure Law’s $36.8 billion in advance appropriations funding. Led by Ranking Member Cantwell, the report demonstrates that these cuts will only drive increased costs for families and business across the country.

At a time when Americans are struggling to pay staggering prices at the fuel pump and the Trump administration sows uncertainty for states and communities by clawing back committed transportation infrastructure funds, Congress is moving to scale back more critical federal funding for affordable and clean transportation.

The need for consistent funding is clear: Transportation costs are the second-largest household expense in the United States, and our roads and transit infrastructure remain structurally inadequate. Nevertheless, state transportation and transit authorities are facing a significant funding cliff due to the expiration of the Bipartisan Infrastructure Law (BIL) on September 30, 2026. Over the past five years, the infrastructure law has provided a guaranteed pot of funding worth $36.8 billion for transportation projects. These “advanced appropriations” cover a wide breadth of programs and include large amounts of funding for clean transportation, such as electric vehicle (EV) charging infrastructure projects, transit, and rail investments. This funding has helped state transit and transportation authorities plan for longer-term projects and has been critical to the infrastructure law’s support of almost 70,000 unique transportation projects across the country.

But transportation funding from the infrastructure law ran out on September 30, 2026, and while Congress passed a short-term extension until December 11, 2026, it does not include these guaranteed dollars. This means that, for the three-month duration of the extension, states will lack the continuity and certainty they need to plan for both large- and small-scale infrastructure projects.

To add insult to injury, Congress also seems to be contemplating leaving this guaranteed funding out of the next transportation bill altogether. The House BUILD America 250 Act proposal, released in May, proposes cutting all advanced appropriations funding. The proposal, in fact, slashes transportation funding from current infrastructure law levels by almost 50 percent, leaving Americans without access to travel options that help defray rising costs.

Historically, transportation bills have included authorizations of two types of funding: (1) contract authority, which is supported by revenues that flow into the Highway Trust Fund and (2) general funds, which Congress has to “appropriate” and assign annually. The current infrastructure law includes another category—advanced appropriations—which makes money available in the future for certain programs without the need for any congressional action in future years, guaranteeing states and communities secure funding over the five-year bill period. This certainty allows for states to plan in advance for future projects and grant opportunities.

Any cut to this pot of money takes a sledgehammer to clean transportation programs that reduce household costs and improve the health of our communities. Particular programs at risk include the National Electric Vehicle Infrastructure program, which helps states build a national network of EV chargers; the Capital Investment Grants, a transit-related discretionary grant program; and the Reconnecting Communities Pilot, which aims to address harms caused by past transportation decisions. Also of concern are the BUILD and MEGA grants, which help support the build-out of transit, bicycle, and pedestrian infrastructure.

Congress is unlikely to agree on a new bill before the current extension comes to an end in December 2026, meaning that there is an opportunity to include advanced appropriations in any future extension of the current infrastructure law and the next transportation bill so that states and local governments can avoid having to grapple with temporary or potentially even permanent funding interruptions. This guaranteed funding is even more important now as transportation decision-makers contend with the Trump administration interfering with implementation and funding guidance. Additional funding clawbacks will only serve to stall progress. The sustained funding will also allow states and local governments to continue to build on the progress that the current infrastructure law made on clean transportation investments.

Even more important, American households are currently struggling to afford their basic daily necessities, and soaring gas prices have left many searching for ways to save on their transportation costs. In 2024, when gas prices averaged below $3.50 a gallon (a far cry from the current average price of $4.35 a gallon), transportation was the second-largest cost for American households, averaging 17 percent of household budgets. Cutting advanced appropriations funding for transit, EV charging, and rail will only serve to penalize the very households struggling the most, at a time when those investments would give Americans access to a wide range of affordable, reliable, and safe transportation options.

By Shruti Vaidyanathan, Director, Transportation Advocacy, Climate & Energy, NRDC

NRDC is the nation's most effective environmental action group, combining the grassroots power of 1.3 million members and online activists with the courtroom clout and expertise of more than 350 lawyers, scientists, and other professionals.

#5844. Can ‘BattMan’ secure the US battery manufacturing supply chain?

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Energy Department’s Battery Manufacturing Accelerator aims to plug gaps in domestic material and component supplies over the next few years as U.S. battery demand looks set to quadruple.

A secure, scaled-up domestic battery supply chain is critical for U.S. economic growth and security in the years ahead, DOE said in the white paper, “A National Strategy for American Leadership in Batteries.”

For utilities, batteries are increasingly indispensable for improving grid reliability and shifting load, particularly in regions where data center loads threaten to disrupt power flows, DOE said in the paper. Data centers themselves could account for one-quarter of total U.S. battery demand by 2030, according to a Benchmark Mineral Intelligence forecast cited in the paper.

The U.S. today is heavily dependent on external trade partners — particularly China — for battery materials and components, which “poses substantial supply chain risks,” DOE said in the paper.

Recent investments in U.S. battery cell and pack manufacturing have not been matched by investments in U.S. production of precursor components, creating gaps in the supply chain, DOE said.

“We have substantial work to do to secure the materials and manufacturing capabilities [lithium-ion batteries] require,” Robertson said in an email. “China currently controls between 60% and 100% of global production capacity across several critical stages of battery materials and component manufacturing … [which] represents a serious vulnerability for American industry and national security.”

China and other Asian countries host nearly all global lithium-ion battery cathode, anode and separator manufacturing and nearly 93% of electrolyte manufacturing, DOE said in 2024.

Robertson said the BattMan consortium, which has support from DOE’s Office of Critical Minerals and Energy Innovation, would expand the Trump administration’s efforts to boost domestic sources of battery materials and components.

The Pacific Northwest National Laboratory leads the consortium, with participation from Argonne National Laboratory, Oak Ridge National Laboratory and the National Laboratory of the Rockies, formerly known as the National Renewable Energy Laboratory. BattMan’s research university partners include the University of Alabama, Arizona State University, Binghamton University, the University of Michigan–Dearborn and the University of Washington.

DOE says the consortium will advance three strategic objectives: establish a “mine-to-market” domestic supply chain by 2030; develop new battery technologies that are “more affordable, longer lasting, higher performing, and less reliant on critical minerals;” and scale deployment of advanced battery technologies in sectors critical to national security.

“We’re excited about technologies that use more abundant domestic materials, reduce dependence on foreign supply chains, and can be manufactured competitively here in the United States,” Robertson said in an email.

In August, DOE announced $500 million in funding for seven projects to expand U.S. critical minerals and materials processing, battery manufacturing and recycling projects. Award recipients include a California-based company expanding lithium-ion battery anode manufacturing with U.S.-sourced materials and another firm piloting battery production using ultra‑thin lithium‑metal films and “prelithiated” material.

The latter “could enable higher-energy, faster-charging batteries,” Robertson said.

According to PNNL, BattMan will work to overcome barriers that have kept promising, U.S.-developed energy storage technologies like these from reaching commercial scale.

The consortium will use a “cost-focused” research and development strategy that “[takes] performance, manufacturability and scalability into account up front;” broaden innovation beyond lithium-ion technologies using “platform technologies that are applicable to all energy storage chemistries;” modernize energy storage manufacturing using digital twins and other AI-enabled tools; and develop reliable, data-driven frameworks for validating emerging energy storage technologies, PNNL said.

Get the free daily newsletter read by industry experts

Major work by regional planners is focused on flexible access to existing transmission. Some say greater emphasis on advanced transmission technologies can quickly and cost-effectively create room for new large loads.

Shortages of skilled labor and equipment, as well as local opposition, are threatening even high-profile projects backed by big tech companies. Developers are responding by embracing flexibility and alternative technologies once considered too unwieldy.

#5843. Buy A New Mazda Miata Today, You Will Not Regret Buying A Mazda Miata

Sometimes you order something online, only for it to arrive in a different size and color than you were expecting. The Mazda MX-5 Miata is not one of those things. It will never disappoint you, and I guarantee you won't regret buying one. So do it. Buy a new Mazda Miata. Come on, what are you waiting for? A sign? This is your sign. I am the prophet. The car gods have sent me to preach their message of salvation, and today I speak unto you, "Go buy a Mazda Miata. You will not regret buying a Mazda Miata."

Now, $32,640 is a lot of money, at least if you're spending it. It probably feels like a lot less if it's your annual income. But even if you make $326,400 a year, it's still not a sum many people will spend on a whim. Divide that MSRP by 60, though, and you get a much more manageable $544. A lot of people have room in their budget to spend $544 if they get creative with it. And if you're one of those people, the best thing you could do with that money would be to spend it on a new Mazda Miata.

Typically, you'll hear that a new car can only fix the problems in your life that your current car is causing. A new truck, for example, can't fix the fact that women find your personality off-putting and weird. Anyone who tells you that is probably right, but they're also wrong. Because while that's true for other cars, there's no problem in your life that a Mazda Miata can't fix.

Do you wish you had more friends? Buy a Miata, and you'll instantly be friends with every Miata owner and car enthusiast that you meet. Do you like talking to old people? Good. Because you're friends with all of them now, and I'm sorry, but they're all yappers. Everywhere you go, you'll find a community of Miata lovers, and because of that, you'll never be lonely again. Because you have a Miata, and no one can possibly be lonely as long as they own a Miata.

Are you trying to make yourself more attractive? I hear tall people are more attractive. And while doing something about your height is incredibly expensive and involves a long recovery, you can easily make your car shorter. By buying a Mazda Miata. How can you possibly look short if you're taller than your car? That's why everyone who drives an Escalade, Tahoe, or Suburban looks so short. They aren't taller than their cars, and it throws off the perspective.

Have other problems? Well, a Mazda Miata once treated my sciatica. And my brother once told me that after the first time he drove a Miata, his IBS was cured. And then there's my friend's cousin, who says his hair regrew even thicker than before, and his ex-wife realized she loved him again. All within a week of buying a Miata. It sounds miraculous, but I swear, it's true. Those are all real things that definitely happened, all because of the magic of the Mazda Miata.

Now, if you buy a new Miata, can I guarantee that you'll look better naked, never have to worry about health problems again, finally receive that apology from your mother, fall deeper in love than you ever thought possible, win a Mega-Millions jackpot, and adopt the one dog that lives as long as you do? Yes. Yes, I can. Because there's nothing the Mazda Miata can't do. And you should buy one. I promise you won't regret buying a new Mazda MX-5 Miata.

#5842. Turtle Wax Is Concerned About Your Love Life, And Claims Messy Cars Impact Dating

Dating today can be "a nightmare of confusion and poor outcomes," according to Psychology Today, but don't worry, Jalopnik and Turtle Wax are on the case. We've already checked in with readers to see which cars to avoid on a first date, along with which ones instantly tell you their owners have good taste. Now, thanks to a survey recently commissioned by the Turtle Wax team, we can go a little deeper: If you really want to make a good impression the next time you take someone out, first take the time to clean up your car.

The survey, conducted by Talker Research on behalf of Turtle Wax, looked at what people thought about potential partners' car-care routines. For instance, out of the 2,000 "actively dating Americans" questioned, 73% said the way people take care of their cars and the way they take care of themselves go hand in hand. Moreover, 67% of those surveyed responded that they can tell if people have messy personal lives by looking at how messy their vehicles are. Coming at it from the other angle, 65% of the survey respondents agreed that a clean, well-ordered vehicle is a sign someone has a clean, well-ordered life.

In fact, 25% of the folks surveyed said they've ended a date early because the driver's car was dirty. The real surprise is that that number wasn't any higher. After all, some of the disgusting items survey-takers had seen in cars included at least one box with a dead cat in it, live roaches and maggots, bottles of urine, moldy milk, and that all-time classic, used condoms.

Driving a messy car can affect more than just your chance for romance. Working our way from the outside in, one welcome benefit of a clean exterior is that it can improve your vehicle's fuel economy — if the car starts out dirty enough, it can see a fuel-efficiency gain approaching 10% once it's been cleaned. And every percentage point counts, given today's fuel prices.

Some road filth can actively damage your car as well. Tree sap and bird poop are prime examples: They can eat right through your vehicle's exterior finish if you don't clean them up promptly. It's a similar story with road salt and other corrosives, which can cause a car to rust if not regularly washed off. Either way, the result can be a cascade of problems. Keep the car, and you could face costly repairs. Try to sell it, and you're likely to get less for a cruddy-looking vehicle than for one that's been freshly detailed. Meanwhile, leaving grime and dust on your windows can turn into a major safety issue if they limit your visibility.

Getting into a car with a disgusting interior can open up its own set of issues. One of the worst is if you drop food or drinks in the cabin and simply leave the spill laying there. Then mold can start forming in your car. Oh, and remember the dead cat in a box mentioned above? Yeah, driving around with dead animals is another bad idea, since they can carry salmonella, hantavirus, e. coli, and more.

#5841. $150 Billion More Paid by Americans for Gas This Year Than Last

Since the beginning of America’s war against Iran, Americans have paid an additional $150+ billion for gas and diesel than they did last year during the same time period, according to a study from Brown University. Global gas prices have risen because supply has been disrupted (Iran having one of the top oil reserves in the world). Americans are paying upwards of $6 a gallon even though American oil companies have not had their own supply chains disrupted the way crude from the Middle East has.

The result? Windfall profits for big oil at the expense of American citizens.

In the leadup to his election in 2024, Donald Trump asked about 20 oil executives at Mar-a-Lago to raise $1 billion for his campaign, promising to reverse dozens of Biden-era regulations if elected. The industry seems not to have raised the full $1 billion, but oil interests still gave tens of millions to Trump-aligned committees. The policies put in place by the Republican party, who has had control of congress and the White House since the last election, have been very pro oil & gas, whether they were anti-EV, anti-solar, anti-energy efficiency, anti-fuel efficiency, anti-wind, or just flat out subsidies to oil and gas.

They ended many of the Biden Administration’s clean energy policies. They ended the Solar For All Program (or tried to). They ended residential and commercial solar tax credits. They signed Executive Order 14315 to terminate wind and solar production and investment tax credits and impose additional restrictions. They made solar development on Federal lands more difficult. On his first day in office, Trump signed a memo withdrawing federal offshore areas from new and renewed wind leasing – but the rule didn’t apply to oil and gas. They ended wind tax credits. They kneecapped the federal standards for vehicle fuel efficiency. They tried to eliminate Energy Star, a program that costs the government $32 million a year and saves Americans $14 billion a year. They weakened Home Energy Rebate programs, SPECIFICALLY stipulating that they could not be used to replace polluting gas furnaces (that increase childhood asthma by more than 15%) with energy efficient electric options. They even tried to make it illegal for future legislators to improve energy efficiency legislation. Then, as if that wasn’t enough, they just gave our tax dollars to the fossil industries, allocating about $190 billion in tax breaks and subsidies over the next decade from the OBBBA.

If this isn’t quid pro quo, I guess I don’t understand what quid pro quo is. But the Iran War took it to a whole new level.

Since it began on February 28, as Americans have spent about $150 billion more on gasoline and diesel, U.S. oil and gas companies earned more than $130 billion in profit from April through September, nearly triple what they earned a year earlier, according to the New York Times.

We can’t say the war was fought for the industry. The president has said his focus is stopping Iran from getting a nuclear weapon. But we can say who is paying at the pump and who is collecting.

Connect the dots however you like, but connect them before you vote. Add it to the roughly $50 billion that US taxpayers have directly spent on the war, and untold more that has percolated through the rest of our economy due to inflationary pressure from rising energy costs, and it’s safe to say it’s well over $200 billion that this war has cost us, alongside the unquantifiable cost in human lives and suffering.

Wars fought for oil are nothing new. But there are things about this conflict that are new.

Oil shocks focus the mind, and this week’s reporting shows how fast the world outside the US is moving to get away from oil dependency. CleanTechnica’s analysis argues that global oil demand is on track to fall by 2–3 million barrels a day this year, the first drop not caused by a recession or pandemic, as high prices push consumers away from fuel. You can see it in China, where gas-car sales fell 49% in September and EVs hit a record 67% share. Europe is also answering, with affordable small EVs like the Smart #2 and an electric Citroën 2 CV at about €15,000. In Australia, a Chinese-built electric semi is already hauling cement for about half the price of its rivals, and Africa’s e-mobility leaders are talking about building vehicles rather than just importing them. Drivers who’ve switched are happy too: 82% of XPeng owners in a 4,000-person European survey said they’d recommend the brand, versus a 53% average.

Every driver, trucker, and country that electrifies becomes less exposed to the next oil shock. The question for the U.S. is how fast it joins in. And this election will go a long way toward answering that question.

Scott Cooney (LinkedIn) is a serial eco-entrepreneur focused on making the world a better place for all its residents. Scott is the founder of CleanTechnica and was just smart enough to hire someone smarter than him to run it. He then started Pono Home, a service that greens homes, which has performed efficiency retrofits on more than 20,000 homes and small businesses, reducing carbon pollution by more than 27 million pounds a year and saving customers more than $6.3 million a year on their utilities. Scott wanted to contribute to native ownership of the clean energy revolution, so he gifted Pono Home to a long tenured employee with native Hawaiian roots for just the liquidation value, turning down a mainland company interested in purchasing the company. In a previous life, Scott was an adjunct professor of Sustainability in the MBA program at the University of Hawai'i, a consultant at Saatchi & Saatchi S, where he worked with a team to educate and empower millions of employees to live healthier and more sustainably. He is the author of Build a Green Small Business: Profitable Ways to Become an Ecopreneur (McGraw-Hill) , and Green Living Ideas. Scott is an occasional investor, currently he has investments in Rivian (RIVN).

#5840. Caterpillar expands production of engines and turbines to meet data center demand

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The heavy machinery and power equipment maker saw its order backlog reach $78 billion during the third quarter, almost double what it was a year ago.

Caterpillar delivered one of its strongest third quarters on record, powered by data center buildouts, as orders across power generation, construction and mining continued to grow, the company said last week.

“Our third quarter results reflect the durability of our end markets, with our highest sales quarter ever, strong order rates and a record backlog,” Caterpillar CEO Joseph Creed said in a Q3 earnings call Thursday morning.

The heavy machinery maker saw its backlog reach $78 billion, nearly double what it was a year ago. Caterpillar has taken steps to expand production of its large reciprocating engines and turbines, while increasing capacity, to meet surging demand for data center customers, Creed said.

“The team is effectively managing the operational challenges that come with this rapid growth,” he said.

Caterpillar’s power and energy division, which manufactures gas-powered engines and generators, led the way for the company during Q3. Sales totaled $8.8 billion, up 18% from the same period last year.

Most of the growth came from power generation sales, which increased 30% year over year and were fueled by data center customers, according to the report. Oil and gas sales also saw double-digit growth, driven by reciprocating engine aftermarket parts and engines used in gas compression applications.

The vast majority of external sales and revenue, approximately 82%, came from North America and the Europe-Middle East-Africa region. Sales declined in the Latin America and Asia-Pacific regions.

The segment turned a profit of $2.1 billion, up 31% from a year ago.

Caterpillar’s construction industries division, which manufactures bulldozers, excavators and other heavy machinery, saw higher sales across all of its geographic market regions during Q3.

Sales totaled nearly $8 billion, up 18% from the same period last year. North America led the way, comprising more than half of the company’s external sales, followed by the EMEA, Asia-Pacific and Latin America regions.

Higher volumes and prices offset unfavorable manufacturing costs, including the impact of higher freight and capacity expansion. Caterpillar last month said it will invest $1 billion to build a facility in Sanford, North Carolina, focused on compact construction equipment for small contractors and growing businesses.

The segment posted a profit of $1.8 billion, up 33% over last year.

Caterpillar’s resource industries division came in below expectations during Q3, in part due to mining shipment and commissioning delays, as well as lower locomotive deliveries, Creed said on the call.

Sales totaled $4.6 billion, up 15% from a year ago. However, Creed said sales to end users or retail customers increased just 4%. The vast majority of Caterpillar’s sales come from dealers and original equipment manufacturers.

The segment turned a profit of $622 million, up 14% over last year. Higher volumes and prices offset unfavorable manufacturing and material costs, including tariffs.

Caterpillar recognized $600 million in tariff costs during Q3, excluding International Emergency Economic Powers Act refunds that it claimed.

This was in line with last year but $200 million lower than what Caterpillar recorded in Q4 2025, CFO Kyle Epley said on the earnings call. Construction industries comprise about 50% of the tariffs total, while power and energy and resource industries each account for 25%.

Caterpillar expects “moderate sales growth” in Q4 over last year, as well as higher operating profit margins, according to Caterpillar’s earnings presentation. For the full year, the company forecasted “mid-to-high teens growth” versus 2025. It also expects full-year tariff costs to be about $2.2 billion, excluding IEEPA refunds, Epley said.

“We delivered another very strong quarter, with year-to-date financial results tracking toward another record year for Caterpillar,” he said. “Our end markets remain healthy with strong order rates and a growing backlog.”

The PJM Interconnection’s plan for curtailing large loads that don’t have matching new generation intrudes on state jurisdiction and appears to be discriminatory, the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission said.

#5839. The industry races into Las Vegas for RE+

The largest tradeshow focused on the U.S. solar, storage and clean energy markets is back in Las Vegas for 2026. RE+ was the 24th largest tradeshow in the United States in 2025 by attendance, and this year’s showing (Nov. 16-19) is expected to be just as large.

The Formula 1 series will race the Las Vegas Grand Prix the weekend after RE+ concludes. Photos courtesy: F1 and the Las Vegas Grand Prix

The U.S. solar and storage industries have gotten so big that it appears only the Las Vegas Convention Center can accommodate a tradeshow bringing together nearly 40,000 clean energy professionals in one place. RE+ will be held at the Las Vegas Convention Center each November for the foreseeable future.

The convention center is tucked away from the main strip of casinos, but there is still plenty in the area to see and do. The site is also easily accessible, with a stop on the Las Vegas Monorail.

Conference education programming begins on Monday and sessions continue throughout the show at various stages on the exhibit hall floor. The exhibition opens on Tuesday.

Exhibitors will be spread across three halls in the convention center — the main entrance is at West Hall which extends via an over-street walkway to North Hall, and then Central Hall is accessed beyond a lobby area. There will also be a few outdoor booths to peruse located near food trucks. Visiting outdoor exhibits in November will be more comfortable, as the average high temperature is just 70°F, unlike the late summer heat of September.

Special events at RE+ include a golf tournament on Monday and the Run with the Sun 5K on Tuesday. The “Energy in Motion” block party will be at the Convention Center on Tuesday starting at 7 p.m. in the West Hall.

Formula 1 will race the Las Vegas Grand Prix the weekend following RE+. The course takes drivers down the Las Vegas Strip and around the Sphere, and road closures to the public will begin on Thursday. The Las Vegas Convention Center is several blocks north of the racing circuit, so the conference shouldn’t experience disruptions. After-conference activities might be affected later in the week, and attendees should plan a little extra time getting to the airport if leaving before the weekend.

Solar Power World editors will be covering all the sights at RE+ this year, so be sure to follow along on LinkedIn and right here on the website. But first, here’s a preview of a few must-see booths on the tradeshow floor.

Visit AWM at RE+ to learn about our Strata for BESS cable management solutions. Our Strata Components and Strata System are tailored for leading energy storage applications and engineered for durability and easy installation. Strata supports reliability across the entire lifecycle of stand-alone storage or hybrid renewable power plants.

Construct solar safer, faster, and cheaper. Meet Built’s AI Piling Solution: a fully autonomous piling system for utility-scale solar. Leading contractors are trusting Built to install production-ready piles anywhere, anytime. Head to Central Hall Booth C6310 to learn how to add physical AI to your next solar project.

Hydrelio aiR Optim is our flexible floating racking and anchoring system. It comes from 15+ years of FPV knowledge and expertise, and the first patented and industrialized floating solar solution. It is the result of continuous R&D involvement, innovation, testing and experience building more than 350 FPV arrays worldwide.

IslandDER Meter Socket Adapter (MSA) streamlines solar + storage installs by integrating a microgrid interconnect device (MID), consumption transformers (CTs), voltage sensing and battery communication protocols into a single, compact device that inserts directly into the meter socket in 15 minutes. This eliminates complex electrical work — such as panel upgrades, circuit relocations and gateways — saving installers days of labor on battery backup jobs and preventing unexpected costs for homeowners. Compatible with battery systems from SolarEdge, FranklinWH, Lunar Energy, EcoFlow and Fox ESS, IslandDER is trusted by leading national installers, offering more versatility and compatibility than any meter collar on the market.

Built on gallium nitride (GaN) technology, Enphase’s ninth-generation microinverter platform delivers industry-leading efficiency and cooler operation for residential and commercial systems. IQ9 Microinverters support the latest high-wattage solar panels across single-phase and three-phase applications and seamlessly integrate with IQ Batteries to create a complete AC-coupled solar and storage system.

Ultra-precision cutting boosts power density and cell efficiency, backed by the company’s FEOC-compliant, TOPCon-licensed manufacturing capabilities and 20 years of cell-to-module expertise.

Intertek CEA will showcase two new advances in solar quality assurance: AePVI, an aerial inspection service that combines drone and ground-based data with electroluminescence (EL) testing to identify defects across utility-scale projects; and EL-AI, software that reviews the EL image of every module before shipment, moving beyond traditional sample-based inspections.

#5838. Trump Wants More Attention For His New Algae Biofuel Showcase

US President Donald Trump has famously transformed the magnificent Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool into a high profile showcase for the algae biofuel industry, and he stands firm in his faith that the tiny green microalgae can produce renewable fuels at scale. In fact, on Monday, November 2, the President held his all-important closing message for the 2026 midterm election cycle right there at the Reflecting Pool. Hey, maybe Trump knows something about algae biofuel…

The Reflecting Pool is a massive body of shallow water that extends from the foot of the Lincoln Memorial in Washington, DC. Situated on filled ground in former marshlands, the Reflecting Pool provides a golden opportunity to showcase the ability of the domestic biofuel industry to take advantage of the built infrastructure, rather than taking up valuable farmland with conventional energy crops like corn or soybeans.

Although algae cultivation was probably not the original intention of the Reflecting Pool, it has been a perennial host to algae ever since its construction in the 1920’s, with the humid environment and temperate climate of DC lending a hand.

In earlier years, the Pool was drained and refilled twice a year to control the growth. By the 1990’s, the structure was clearly sinking into the marshes, sparking calls for a major structural overhaul and the addition of a water circulating system.

The soup-to-nuts rehab was completed in 2012, and yet the algae persisted. Finally, when Trump took office for the second time in January of 2025, he lit upon a brilliant idea of turning lemons into lemonade. The Reflecting Pool would be re-made as a monument to the domestic algae biofuel industry.

By the summer of 2026, the transformation was complete and the algae were thriving, as confirmed by satellite imagery. Others mocked, but the President stood by the plan. After all, biomass is a key element in his “American Energy Dominance” policy, and where better to showcase biofuel than in the nation’s capitol, to be seen and admired by millions of tourists.

And so, that explains why Trump chose the Reflecting Pool as the seemingly incongruous scene of his Election Day eve closing message, as reported somewhat incredulously by The Guardian among many others.

“On the eve of a consequential election day, with polls indicating that most voters feel the US president has grown too focused on building projects in the nation’s capital, he stood in front of the newly opened reflecting pool to discuss – in detail – changes to the pool’s pipe, the grass surrounding the area and granite,” observed The Guardian.

Algae biofuel surfaced regularly on the pages of CleanTechnica in the early 2000’s, with the high oil content and rapid growth cycle of algae being the attractions along with the potential for developing next-generation farming and extraction systems leading to a competitive renewable energy resource (see more algae background here).

Not much progress has been made since then, and stakeholders in the field have weathered much skepticism if not downright mockery. A seal of approval from the President of the United States of America is just what the industry needs to get to the next level.

Indeed, a fresh round of activity has surfaced in recent years, with the UK firm HutanBio being one example. The startup aims to generate profitability by leveraging the carbon-capturing angle of algae farming.

Earlier this year Hutan signed an MOU (Memorandum of Understanding) with the Irish firm Utopia World Investment and the biodiesel refiner Wakud International to scale up its algae farming system, with Oman being the location. “Our unique microalgae strain, Sphaerica, is grown in seawater, on non-arable coastal land, using CO₂ and sunlight creating a genuinely low-carbon biocrude stream that the industry currently lacks and urgently needs,” HutanBio says of itself.

As described by HutanBio, Sphaerica yields up to ten times more oil than typical algae biofuel strains. Hutan expects the high level of productivity to dovetail with Oman’s high level of solar radiation, enabling it to leapfrog over its competitors.

The Oman operation is set for operation at a demonstration scale before the end of 2027, which is just around the corner. Meanwhile, Japanese automaker Mazda is also beginning to crack the algae code.

On October 7, Mazda announced that its microalgae biofuel research program has nailed down support from NEDO, the New Energy and Industrial Technology Development Organization of Japan. The Mazda program is in early stages, with scale-up expected by the 2030’s. If all goes according to plan, Mazda will use its homemade algae biofuel to factory-fill the tanks of every diesel vehicle that rolls off its assembly lines.

Evidently Mazda does not anticipate that drivers can pump out algae biofuel at any corner gas station for their next fill-up. Still, it’s another sign that that years-long research programs are finally leading to commercial application.

For a glimpse into the future, check out newly published research from the Saitama University in Japan. Where conventional systems involve cultivating algae, drying it, and processing it to remove oils, the Saitama team has developed a strain of cyanobacteria (commonly known as blue-green algae) that can express free fatty acids outside of the cell and deposit them directly into a culture medium. Among other benefits, the Saitama system does not require destroying the algae with each harvesting cycle.

As for the US, ExxonMobil was the standard-bearer for early stage algae biofuel research throughout the early 2000’s. By 2017, the company was confident enough to put up millions for an elaborate advertising blitz that showcased its efforts to the world.

Or not, as the case may be. In 2019, Massachusetts State Attorney General Maura Healey filed suit against Exxon, alleging that the biofuel ads were deceptive. “Specifically, the complaint alleges that Exxon systematically and intentionally has misled Massachusetts investors about material climate-driven risks to its business and has deceived consumers about the central role its fossil fuel products play in causing climate change,” AG Healy’s office explained.

Exxon subsequently dropped the program altogether, but the lawsuit persists. Among the details coming to light is a deposition by an Exxon scientist who worked on the algae program. Under questioning by attorneys with the AG’s office, the scientist asserted that the claims in Exxon’s ads were “patently false,” and that he said as much to the company.

It’s too bad Exxon dropped the ball before Trump had a chance to pick up the algae biofuel torch. Oh, well. We’ll always have the Reflecting Pool…

Photo: The Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool has been host to thriving populations of algae ever since its construction in the 1920’s (cropped, courtesy of National Parks Service).

#5837. EV Owners Need To Lend Out Their Cars If They Want Regular People To Go Electric

Electric vehicles will probably never fully replace the internal combustion sports car, but mainstream daily drivers are living on borrowed time. It's not like anyone with no idea which wheels are powered or how many cylinders their car has cares about a missing exhaust note. But while EVs are taking off in the rest of the world, the U.S. lags behind, thanks in large part to pro-oil propaganda. People believe all sorts of myths about EVs, and I'm sorry, but short test drives won't fix it. Which means EV owners need to start acting like they have an obligation to lend friends and family their cars.

A few days in an EV won't change anyone's mind if their lifestyle doesn't match what EVs currently offer, and that's fine. But if they've been tricked by a bunch of pro-oil myths, you may find that a few days with your car is all it takes to convince them that EVs are the best thing ever. Because even an hour-long test drive doesn't show you the benefits of living with a gas station in your garage. It's the kind of thing that probably requires a three-day loan at a minimum, because they have to see how it fits into their regular lives.

Ideally, the automakers would take the lead here, but that's probably not happening anytime soon. I've discussed this idea with employees at several automakers, and so far, I don't think anyone wants to take on the cost and complexity of dealing with dealerships to make longer-term test drives happen. So without the automakers, it falls on the EV owners who want to see EVs become more popular. We need to be the ones pushing our cars on people like they're the supposed free samples that Deputy D. Diggity Dawg Dawson insisted drug dealers would force me to take.

At least anecdotally, I don't know of anything more powerful for changing minds about EVs than time spent with your butt in the seat. Most recently, a friend had to replace their beloved Mazda CX-5 after someone hit them; they asked me about EVs, and almost before I knew it, they were the proud new owners of a Hyundai Ioniq 5. So they were already open to a car that would save them money on gas, but even they quickly realized the things they were worried about weren't actually a big deal.

According to the friend, who I will call Bill, "driving them is super f—ing fun," and he did not think he "would enjoy trying to max out the mi/kWh using the regenerative braking... But [he does]." After only a few days of driving an EV, someone experiencing their first EV was already realizing how strong the oil propaganda is and how much better EVs are at things beyond saving money on gas.

An example of a borrowing experience changing minds is my brother — he borrowed my Fiat for a week while he was in the process of replacing his company truck. With three (soon to be four!) kids, he was never going to buy a two-door hatchback, but he loved being able to charge at home so much that he went out and bought a non-Fiat EV. All it took was a little time living with an electric car that he didn't even have any interest in owning, and it changed his mind about EV ownership before he'd even driven a competitive one.

I know it's scary letting other people borrow your car, and I certainly wouldn't want you handing the keys to someone you know is irresponsible. But without automaker or federal support, we're going to have to do this on our own, and the best way to get people to change their minds about EVs is to get some real time with the cars. And what better way to make that happen is there than trading cars with someone for a few days? Friends and family help each other out, after all.

If you lend your car to someone who decides they probably shouldn't buy an EV, that's fine. No harm, no foul. But just think how happy you'll be if a few days if your car convinces someone they've been wrong about EVs and should actually get one. It's pretty nice, I'm not going to lie, and definitely worth the risk of letting someone you know drive your car. They quite literally sell themselves.

#5836. The Senate permitting bill holds promise but needs amendments

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Targeted changes can minimize the most negative consequences of the permitting bill’s overreaching provisions, writes former FERC Chairman Mark Christie.

Mark Christie is a former chairman of the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission and former chairman of the Virginia State Corporation Commission, and is the director of the William & Mary Law School Center for Energy Law and Policy.

Every true compromise in a polarized legislature contains candy for each side, but also requires each side to swallow some hairballs. In the Senate permitting reform bill (SB 5653), the true permitting reforms hold a welcome potential to get needed infrastructure built faster. The transmission provisions, however — which are not permitting reforms at all — contain some provisions that are indigestible if you care about rising consumer costs and respect for historic state authority over utilities.

The bill’s best transmission provisions seek to ensure that large-load customers pay the full costs they impose on the grid. The worst transmission provisions, however, can be fixed with the amendments described below. Before considering these amendments, it is important to understand that the Federal Power Act for nearly a century has recognized a clear and logical division between state and federal authority over the power grid: The states have authority over the siting and permitting of all physical assets within their borders, including generation and transmission. The Federal Energy Regulatory Commission has authority over the rates for interstate transmission services and wholesale power sales.

The current Senate bill blows up this century-old balance of authority by giving FERC the sweeping power to order a transmission line built in any state, without regard to the state’s own decision on the project or the opposition of the state’s residents who will be directly affected. Congress has enacted so-called “backstop siting authority” before, most recently in the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act in 2021, but Congress strictly limited the geographic reach of FERC’s backstop authority to narrow geographic corridors established by the U.S. Department of Energy and only after DOE made a finding that the national interest required the establishment of each corridor.

The Senate bill contains no geographic limits on FERC’s permitting power. It expands FERC’s authority to override the states to every square inch of the country, a breathtaking expansion of federal power at the expense of a core state function: the permitting and construction of utility assets within their own borders, projects that could have huge impacts on the lives of their residents.

Fortunately, targeted amendments can minimize the most negative consequences of the bill’s overreaching provisions. Here are three recommended amendments:

The bill allows FERC to order the construction of transmission lines based on a nebulous “public interest” standard. Such a standard is a blank check for FERC to approve trillions of dollars’ worth of transmission lines for whatever reason a presidential administration finds is in the “public interest,” as long as FERC adds general findings of some reliability improvements or consumer benefits, which can be claimed for every transmission line ever proposed. In the short term, FERC could use the bill’s grant of new authority to order transmission lines built to serve hyperscale data center development, a Trump administration priority. A future FERC could find that the public interest requires trillions of dollars of new transmission to meet “Net Zero” climate goals.

So my first recommended amendment is to remove the “public interest” and other general criteria, and instead require that FERC must find that the proposed line is the optimal solution to meet a proven reliability need at just and reasonable rates, compared to alternative solutions.

My second amendment addresses the critical role of states in cost allocation. FERC Order 1920 guaranteed to states in a multi-state region the right to reach an agreement on cost allocation for regional transmission projects and have that agreement submitted to FERC. The Senate bill should add an explicit guarantee that for regional transmission projects the states in each region have the right to agree on cost allocation and FERC shall presume such state agreement is just and reasonable.

My third amendment concerns one of the most onerous and frankly insulting provisions in the Senate bill. When we at FERC implemented the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act’s backstop siting authority, we gave each affected state one full year to conduct its permitting proceeding before FERC would start its own backstop proceeding. That allowed the state to develop a factual record and issue a decision within a reasonable period of one year.

The Senate bill allows FERC to start its backstop proceeding simultaneously with the state proceeding. That effectively renders the state proceeding meaningless, and states will perceive it exactly that way. Why should a state spend its time and limited resources on its proceeding when FERC is already doing its own? Any evidentiary record developed in the state proceeding may not even be available to FERC before it overrides the state. This provision in the Senate bill is nakedly punitive and overreaching and should be amended to give the states one full year to act before FERC can begin its own backstop proceeding.

Even if you don’t care about respecting state authority, you should care about the bill’s impact on consumers already struggling to pay their power bills. Over the past decade, transmission has been one of the fastest-growing drivers of power bill increases. The Senate bill as drafted could result in literally trillions of dollars of new transmission being ordered by FERC to serve whatever policy goals a president wants. These costs will flow right into consumers’ monthly bills.

Every compromise offers candy and hairballs. With these amendments, the bill can be made a lot sweeter.

#5835. I Checked Out Tesla’s New Model YL, Standard Y, Model 3 In Person — Here’s What Surprised Me

I finally got to see the new Tesla Model YL, Model Y Standard, and Model 3 Standard in person a few weeks ago and wanted to give my thoughts on the three models. I drove the two Model Ys and just looked at the Model 3 Standard.  I was visiting relatives in Colorado and visited the Tesla dealer in Superior, Colorado (a very progressive part of Colorado). The dealer was very busy, but not quite as busy as I saw when the tax credit was expiring last year.

As I mentioned in this article 4 months ago, I’ve been a fan of the Model YL since it was announced in China, and finally in the US recently. As a parent of 3 kids and an owner of a series of 3 minivans over 30 years, I’m well aware of the criticism that Tesla doesn’t make a car suitable for large families. The Model X was a partial solution to this problem, but had two large flaws in my opinion.

So, I wanted to see if the Model YL solved these two issues with the Model X. The short is answer is that it solved the price issue but not the space issue. The premium launch edition at $61,990, with about $11,000 in free options, is plenty affordable — although, still a bit more than a minivan or a Grand Highlander. In fact, I optioned up a Model Y Premium with similar features (including the 3rd row) and it came out to about $60,000! So, this suggests the Model YL will be available for about $53,000 or approximately a $3,000 upcharge once they stop selling the Launch Edition (similar to the price difference in other global markets). On space, the Model YL is pretty similar to the Model X. Good enough for many families, but not as spacious as minivans, the Grand Highlander, or the Chevy Tahoe. You can see from the screenshot below that the Model YL is sold out for 2026, and the dealer was telling people to not expect them until March 2027.

I thought it would look more different than the regular Y. I found it difficult to tell the difference at a glance.

The second row was very comfortable, with heated and cooled seat and plenty of room and adjustable headrests. A significant upgrade to the second row of the regular Y!

The third row was a mixed bag. The seats are heated, but not cooled. It does have vents for both air conditioning and heat. It’s not as spacious as the first and second rows, but at 6′ 2″, I was comfortable and would be fine on a long drive. I did find it challenging to get to the 3rd row, since the second row didn’t slide forward like it does in many vehicles (including the regular Model Y). You are expected to sneak between the two seats in the second row, which works for most people, but older folks like me don’t like it.

Leg room was adequate (not not generous), but I was more comfortable putting one leg in the middle as shown. Had I moved up the second row, I would have had plenty of room, but then it would have been crowded. I was setting all three rows as best I could for 6-feet-tall people in all 3 rows. This is MUCH better than the 3rd row in the regular Model Y, which is only appropriate for children and small adults.

I was amazed I had an inch or two of space above my head in the 3rd row. I forgot to take any pictures of the first row. It does have an extra thigh support adjustment that is nice, but is mostly just like all of the other Model Y (great, but nothing new). Handling and accelerations were about the same as a Premium Model Y dual motor. The ride was a little softer (the YL has electronically controlled dampers, tuned for comfort) than the 2026 Model Y Premium (frequency dependent passive controlled dampers, tuned for a balance of comfort and handling) and much improved over my 2021 Model Y (conventional passive shock absorbers, great for handling but gives a harsh ride) and the 2026 Model Y Standard (retuned conventional passive shock absorbers, a balance between handling and ride). Full Self Driving (Supervised) was promoted by the sales advisor and was perfect on the short test drive.

The Model Y Standard reminded me most of my daughter’s 2024 Model Y Rear Wheel Drive. It is a little noisier and doesn’t ride as well as the premium model. It doesn’t have the ventilated seats and the premium audio, but for many, the $6,000 price difference makes up for that. For others, the Premium is well worth that cost. I did like the 18″ wheels that improve range and offer more sidewall for surviving large potholes. The Model Y Premium only offers 19″ and 20″ wheels. Full Self Driving (Supervised) worked perfectly on the test drive.

To adjust the seats, you use the screen, since there are not controls on the seats.

I didn’t drive the Model 3 Standard, but I want to make two points about it.

After seeing the Model YL, Model Y Standard, and the Model 3 Standard in person, the takeaway is clear: Tesla has made its most practical vehicles significantly more accessible without abandoning the core strengths that made the brand popular.

The Model YL addresses the long-standing complaint that Tesla lacked a true family hauler. While it still doesn’t match the passenger or cargo space of a minivan or large three-row SUV like the Grand Highlander or Tahoe, it is a meaningful improvement over the regular Model Y’s third row and far more usable for adults or even teenagers. At roughly $53,000 once the Launch Edition incentives end, it finally offers a realistic alternative to the much more expensive Model X or Rivian R1S (~$90,000 for comparable 6 seat layout) for families who want three rows without the premium price.

The Model Y Standard reinforces that Tesla can still deliver strong value at a lower price. It gives up ventilated seats, premium audio, and some refinement, but the $6,000 savings and more practical 18-inch wheels make it a sensible choice for some buyers. Meanwhile, the Model 3 Standard stands out as an even better relative deal than the original 2016 Model 3 that created such intense demand — more range, quicker acceleration, and Full Self-Driving capability at a price that remains extremely competitive in today’s market. I was disappointed when these models were announced a year ago, as I wrote about here, mainly because I felt Tesla promised a smaller car for under $30,000. However, all three of these vehicles have found their market in the US, as evidenced by the long waitlist to get all three and the lack of discounts and aggressive financing deals available.

Taken together, these three models show Tesla broadening its appeal. The company is no longer only competing at the premium end; it is also offering capable, reasonably priced options for both families and efficiency-focused buyers. For those who have been waiting for Tesla to better serve larger households or entry-level shoppers, the current lineup finally delivers more realistic choices.
