#5834. At $7,450, Could This Clean 2001 Audi A4 Avant Quattro Be The One For You?

The Audi cult has come together to keep today's Nice Price or No Dice A4 Avant on the road. There's still work to be done, but the seller advises that it will come with a service manual to ease the burden. Let's see how burdensome we find its price.

When you think of Wrangler, what's the first thing to come to mind? Is it the legendary Jeep that debuted during WWII? Or is it the brand of blue jeans originally designed for rodeo wear and that are just as old? It's unusual for two brands to leverage the same name, even when they serve different categories. The main corporate concern is that one brand's failure might possibly taint the other's fortunes.

That wasn't a primary concern when we considered the 2003 Jeep Wrangler Sahara that came our way on Monday. A bit battered, but not beyond redemption, the Wrangler (Jeep, not jeans) came with a factory hardtop and a $7,500 asking price. You all mostly liked what you saw and weren't too perturbed by the price. That combo earned the Wrangler (Jeep, not jeans) a narrow but ultimately victorious 55% 'Nice Price' nod.

Not everyone knows the difference between four-wheel drive and all-wheel drive. They sound similar and interchangeable, but the terms describe distinct drivetrains, each designed for specific purposes. The Jeep we looked at yesterday was 4WD, a drivetrain that ties all four wheels together when needed through a central locking differential for low-speed maneuvering through mud and over rocks.

By contrast, today's 2001 Audi A4 Quattro Avant is permanently activated AWD and is intended not for off-roading but for more secure handling and power application in inclement weather. For Audi, at least, this drivetrain is time-tested and rally-proven. Their personalities are further differentiated by their looks: the Jeep being brutish and basic while the Audi is one of the most handsome and elegant small estates ever offered for sale.

This one, in Cactus Green with the coal mine black interior that was favored by German carmakers back in the day, comes with a clean title, 175,020 miles under its Cactus Green belt, and an extensive list of refurbishment and renewal that has left it looking like Cinderella before the ball.

Under the A4's hood is a 20-valve version of Volkswagen/Audi's ubiquitous inline four, here in 1.8-liter displacement, denoted as the "AJL". Turbocharged and intercooled, the engine makes 170 horsepower and 166 pound-feet of torque. That's routed through a five-speed automatic with Tiptronic manual controls on both the lever and steering wheel for when the driver is feeling a little spicy. Naturally, as a Quattro, it sends torque to all four wheels via a central viscous diff. We'll get to more on the mechanical situation in a sec, but first, let's address the car's appearance.

Overall, this little Audi looks to be in great condition for its age and not insignificant miles. The paint still shines; there are no significant dings or dents beneath that top coat, and the headlamp lenses show only minor clouding. According to the seller, they addressed minor paint imperfections, including bumper scrapes and hatch scratches. Surprisingly, the interior is in even better shape, looking almost as-new thanks to several replacement parts and general care and cleaning. Even the comically flimsy dashboard cup holder appears to be working as originally intended.

Plenty of other work has gone into this A4's care and feeding. That included replacing several suspension components and wheel bearings, all set at factory ride height. Most importantly, the engine has received a ton of maintenance, starting with a timing belt and water pump replacement (a plus because this is an interference engine). That work continued to the exhaust, which had the catalytic converter, O2 sensor, and mounting hardware replaced. Another major update was converting the A/C from R12 to R134. New rotors and pads, plus chasing down a number of electrical gremlins, round out the major mechanical work.

In addition to all the wrenching, the ad notes that any broken or missing parts—such as the jack and emergency triangle (German car) have all been replaced. There are still a few bugs to be tracked down, including rear door security sensors and the CD player. Fortunately, the seller is throwing in a manual for future owners. Per the ad description, the car now throws no codes and is ready for the next owner "to drive it away with confidence."

For that confident drive away to happen, there's the small matter of the seller's set price of $7,450. That's almost exactly what yesterday's more rough-hewn Jeep asked, making for an interesting contrast. Naturally, these are wildly different vehicles in both design and intent, but they hail from almost the same year and, as noted, fall in the same price range. Which would you rather have?

Wait. Strike that. Let's focus on the Audi for now. We do, after all, have to consider it on its own merits, not in comparison to a truck or a pair of blue jeans, whichever yesterday's Wrangler was.

With that in mind, what's your take on this well-cared-for and rehabilitated A4 Avant at that $7,450 asking price? Does that feel fair given its now turnkey presentation? Or is that too much for a car that's already nickel-and-dimed its present owner significantly?

#5833. Hyundai's XRT Off-Road Division Is On The Right Path But A Step Behind

There's nothing more in vogue right now than automakers soft-roader-ing their crossovers for weekend warrior types who enjoy a bit of camping or a dirt road every once in a while. Honda has TrailSport, Subaru has Wilderness, Toyota has TRD, GMC has AT4, and so on and so forth. Of course one of the largest automakers in the U.S. would want to get in on the action, so Hyundai spun up its own off-road sub-brand a few years back: XRT, which stands for Extreme Rugged Terrain.

What started out as more of a trim and appearance package on vehicles like the first-gen Palisade and fourth-gen Tucson and Santa Fe has evolved into offering styling changes with a bit of added capability when the going gets bumpy. With the introduction of Hyundai's XRT Pro trim on the Palisade and upcoming fifth-gen Tucson, that idea gets taken up another notch. However, after spending a day with both the 2026 Santa Fe XRT and 2026 Palisade XRT Pro off-roading in rural Missouri, I'm not totally sure Hyundai is all the way there yet. That's not to say the Korean automaker isn't on the right path and won't keep improving expeditiously, but as it stands right now, it may be just a step or two behind.

Full Disclosure: Hyundai flew me out to Bentonville, Arkansas, put me up in a very nice hotel and fed me, all so I could sample the 2026 Santa Fe XRT and 2026 Palisade XRT Pro.

It can be sort of tricky to evaluate the capability of a soft-roader like Hyundai's XRT fleet. Technically, both the Palisade XRT Pro and Santa Fe XRT were able to do everything that was thrown at them on this program, but they both lacked a bit of the sure-footedness I just experienced in Honda's Passport TrailSport, and that was on a far more technical and challenging course. That being said, what I did with the XRT siblings is probably 10 times as grueling as what the average Hyundai owner will do with their SUVs. There's also the issue of tires. While the Passport TrailSport is fitted with beefy General Grabber A/T Sport all-terrain rubber, the Santa Fe XRT makes do with less-aggressive Continental TerrainContact A/Ts, which leads to a good bit of wheel slippage.

Still, both XRTs do an admirable job of tackling trails, though rock crawlers they are not. The Palisade XRT Pro I drove was certainly the more capable of the two, thanks to its electronic limited-slip differential in the rear and a 360-degree camera that helped overcome its sheer size, but it never felt particularly at home, even if it did tackle every obstacle without much protest.

I say much protest because, while the car didn't get stuck at any point, the 4,575-pound crossover didn't exactly seem happy, and that especially goes for its Hyundai-built 8-speed transmission. While the naturally aspirated 3.5-liter V6 provided enough power (287 horsepower and 260 pound-feet of torque) to get up just about anything despite a lack of low-end oomph, I could tell that the 8-speed was none too pleased. I couldn't hear it on the road, but when switching to the Sand setting in its Trail drive mode, there was a noticeable whine coming from it on the throttle. A temperature readout in the gauge cluster also served as a constant reminder it was, uh, hot, and when I got out of the car, there was an unmistakable smell of hot transmission wafting through the air. From a functional standpoint, the XRT Pro didn't skip a beat, which is certainly a testament to the rest of the Palisade's drivetrain and its 8.4 inches of ground clearance; it bottomed out far less than I expected it to.

It's a similar story with the Santa Fe XRT. It may lack the e-LSD and all-out grunt of the Palisade, but it does make up for that somewhat with more low-end grunt courtesy of a torquey turbocharged 2.5-liter inline-4 that pumps out 277 hp and 311 lb-ft of torque. This drivetrain lent itself far better to the old-school way of getting yourself unstuck: give 'er some gas. Sure, there are 10 fewer horses than the Palisade's V6, but an extra 51 torques is massive.

Still, you can tell the Santa Fe is a far less luxury-oriented product than the Palisade, and I got bumped around a hell of a lot more than I did in the Palisade. Obviously, there's a price delta to consider, as the Santa Fe XRT starts at $43,940 including destination, and the Palisade XRT Pro has a far more costly $51,820 entrance price.

Currently, there are four XRT models on sale: the two I've been talking about, and then the Santa Cruz XRT and the Ioniq 5 XRT. I've driven the Ioniq and had a pretty good time with it, but came away feeling it was more of a styling exercise than anything else. Sure, it can handle some dirt, but that's just about it. While all these models do come with all-terrain tires, a bit more ground clearance, upped towing capacity, better approach and departure angles, and functional front recovery hooks, they're without a doubt behind industry frontrunners like Honda TrailSport and Subaru Wilderness when it comes to real capability.

I believe that's because XRT, as a brand, is so new. Hyundai's still trying to find its footing in an ever-more crowded world. It promises that its next product, based on the fifth-generation Tucson, will be its best yet, and far more than an appearance package. Even though the Tucson won't have the e-LSD of the Palisade XRT Pro, despite sharing the "Pro" moniker, it will have skid plates, all-terrain tires, 8.3 inches of ground clearance, some sick body cladding, and Hyundai's new off-road software called Smart Terrain Assist.

That program has the ability to analyze the terrain ahead of you, determining traction and slip conditions. From there, it provides visual guidance via green, orange or red "stuck zones," and shows the driver the best route to take. It works in concert with Automatic Terrain Detection (which has auto, snow, mud, sand and rock modes) with intelligent mode switching to remove any guesswork from off-roading. Clearly, Hyundai wants to make it as friendly to first-time off-roaders as possible.

Lest we forget, this same team is working on the body-on-frame Hyundai Boulder SUV, which will likely be powered by a hybrid or EREV drivetrain. It shares underpinnings with a future body-on-frame truck Hyundai is also working on, and it's targeted specifically at the American market.

While I'm not sure Hyundai's XRT division is quite the cream of the crop just yet when it comes to soft-roading — it might be a bit too focused on aesthetics, rather than actual performance — there's little doubt in my mind that it'll get to the front of the pack sooner rather than later. Just look at what this massive company has done with its N performance division, or what it has done with Genesis when it comes to luxury. When Hyundai puts its mind to something, it gets the job done, and I don't expect XRT to be any different.

#5832. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5831. Aluminum–Air Generators for Data Centres Face a Metal Logistics Test

California startup Voya Energy announced a $35 million financing round in August 2026 for generators that use prepared scrap aluminum, air and water to produce electricity without combustion. Its immediate opportunity includes replacing diesel backup equipment at data centres, where exhaust, noise and permitting can be genuine obstacles. The company also describes a larger market for firm power at grid-constrained sites, including systems operating around the clock. The electrochemistry is established, but delivering competitive electricity requires a materials supply chain that the generator specifications do not describe.

An aluminum–air cell consumes aluminum. It does not accept electricity through a charging cable and return to its original chemical state. Aluminum reacts with oxygen and water, producing electrical energy and aluminum hydroxide, sometimes called aluminum trihydrate. The generator needs more metal when the fuel is spent, and the hydroxide has to be collected, transported and either sold into another market or processed further. The aluminum brought to a data centre already embodies substantial electricity used earlier to turn aluminum compounds into metal. That upstream energy belongs in the assessment.

I encountered the same supply-chain issues when I examined Wright Electric’s aluminum–air aircraft proposal in 2021. Prepared aluminum would have needed delivery to airports, followed by collection and processing of spent material. Data centres avoid the aircraft weight constraint, while retaining the need for deliveries of prepared fuel and industrial handling of reaction products.

The operating schedule determines the size of that problem. Voya’s August announcement emphasizes diesel replacement while also describing larger, continuously available power installations. To test the larger proposition, I calculated the materials needed for a hypothetical 100 MW data centre supplied continuously by aluminum–air generation. Using the company’s reported 57% conversion efficiency and aluminum’s theoretical electrochemical energy, the system consumes roughly 520 metric tons of metal each day and produces about 1,500 metric tons of aluminum hydroxide. That becomes approximately 190,000 tons of incoming metal and 550,000 tons of outgoing material each year. The additional byproduct mass comes from oxygen and water incorporated in the reaction. These are scenario quantities, not measured operations at a Voya site.

An intermittent backup generator could consume orders of magnitude less aluminum annually than the continuous 100 MW case, changing the economics of deliveries and residue handling. Voya’s interest in low-grade contaminated scrap deserves examination, since some metal streams are difficult to sort into high-quality recycled products. Yet scrap is not automatically valueless because it is contaminated. Recycling aluminum into new metallic products uses approximately 95% less energy than making primary metal, and improving scrap recovery is itself an important industrial decarbonization opportunity. Buying that scrap as expendable fuel can compete with other recovery routes as their technology and economics improve.

Voya initially expects to sell aluminum hydroxide and proposes metal regeneration as a longer-term option. Those are different uses for the spent material. A 2026 lifecycle assessment modeled electricity-to-electricity round-trip efficiency of 29–35% for aluminum–air storage pathways involving regeneration. That finding is not a test result for Voya’s equipment, but it indicates how much additional electricity and processing could be needed to close the material loop. A company selling the hydroxide cannot also assume it will recover that same material for fuel without buying it back.

Voya’s published metal-price target leaves limited room for other costs. In a 2025 California Energy Commission submission, the company listed targets below $0.30/kWh for delivered electricity and $0.50 per pound for the metal fuel. At 57% conversion efficiency, the latter implies about $0.24/kWh for aluminum alone, before fuel preparation, generator capital, transport, residue handling or financing. Saleable hydroxide could provide an offset where purity, processing costs and buyers support it. The generation target remains a target, rather than a demonstrated commercial tariff.

There may be worthwhile applications for compact, noncombustion backup power where diesel is hard to permit and suitable scrap is readily available. Continuous power for large AI facilities calls for a much larger comparison with grid connections, generation and rechargeable batteries. The chemistry works, but the economics must account for every load of metal delivered, every load of residue removed and the electricity consumed upstream.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment follows the aluminum through the generator, the possible recycling and regeneration routes, Voya’s cost targets and the very different material demands of backup and continuous generation.

#5829. What Recessionmobile Should Automakers Build?

Folks, it certainly feels like there's a recession right around the corner. The AI stocks are tumbling, nothing else in the economy is doing anything, we're teetering on the edge of an auto debt crisis — things are bad. But, no matter how broke we all get, we all still have to get to work. That brings us to the topic of today's question: What recession-mobiles should automakers start building?

A good recessionmobile should be practical, reliable, and most importantly economical. Think the Cruze and Focus of the post-recession era, cars that just get you from point A to poitn B without pretending to do anything more. We've all got places to get to, and we all want to do that for as little cash as possible — especially with fuel prices where they are. What should automakers start designing now, so they're ready to hit the market with new cost-effective products when everything hits the fan?

I think, for this try at a recession, we've got to give it up on the whole idea of four-wheeled transportation. We've tried it, and cars are just too much to move around for the one or two people aboard. Plus, when we're all broke, we won't exactly be making Costco hauls — who needs all the cargo space of a Traverse? The Honda Cub is the vehicle we all need, but other companies should try their hand at it too. Give us a Suzuki, a Triumph, a Toyota version of tiny, efficient transportation.

I think more companies should make Super Cubs for the recession, but what do you think? Leave your responses in the comments below, and I'll pick my favorites out later this week. Bonus points for some real in-depth reasoning as to why you think your pick would be the ideal vehicle for getting us all through the next once-in-a-lifetime financial crisis.

#5828. If The US Department Of Defense Gets Solar Power, Why Can’t We?

In the topsy-turvy world of today’s federal energy policy, solar power is a bad thing. Various members of the President’s cabinet have advised that solar is  “unreliable,” and that solar power plants routinely experience “catastrophic failure” with every sunset. So why does the US Department of Defense continue to pursue solar-enabled microgrids at its facilities? Asking for a friend…

Though some solar projects at military facilities are relatively small, they are stamped with the seal of approval from the Defense Department, and that can have an outsized influence on public perceptions about the reliability, security, and durability of photovoltaic systems.

The history-making White Sands Missile Range in New Mexico is a case in point. In February of 2025 the facility celebrated the launch of a new, 700-kilowatt solar array designed and built by the joint venture HSGS-Ameresco.

At 700 kW, the new array is peanuts compared to the megawatt scale common among modern solar power plants. However, a little can go a long way in some cases, particularly when solar is integrated with other energy resources in a microgrid that can operate independently, without interruption, if the local distribution grid goes down.

“The innovative microgrid system, which integrates a 700kW solar photovoltaic array, a 500kW natural gas generator, and a 500kW lithium-ion battery system, will provide backup power to four groundwater wells on the range,” Ameresco explained in a press statement last year, in which it described the White Sands project.

The four wells are the primary source of potable water to the main post at White Sands. The new microgrid meets a US Army requirement for up to 14 days of independent water and power delivery to the post in case of a grid outage.

“This self-sufficient energy system, designed to serve a specific geographic area, helps ensure that critical missions at WSMR can proceed without interruption,” Ameresco elaborates, adding that the microgrid underscores White Sands’s commitment to building more resilient energy systems into its future construction projects.

Ameresco also indicated that the now-familiar combineation of solar power and BESS (battery energy storage systems) will do most of the heavy lifting, with the gas generator playing a back-up role if necessary.

“The solar array captures energy from the sun during the day, while the natural gas generator provides a dependable backup power source. The lithium-ion battery system efficiently stores excess energy generated by the solar array and releases it when needed, helping to ensure an uninterrupted power supply,” Ameresco emphasized.

The role of a lithium-ion battery in the new microgrid may come as quite a shock to US Interior Secretary Doug Burgum, who has somehow failed to get memo about energy storage systems for solar power. Energy storage is an actual thing that exists in the space-time continuum here on Earth, but apparently not in Burgum’s corner of the mulitverse.

“And of course, when the sun goes down, you have a catastrophic failure called sunset and there’s no solar energy produced, and yet we’re subsidizing these things that are intermittent, unreliable, and expensive,” Burgum told a Fox News audience in July of 2025.

Whether Burgum believes it or not, the HGSG-Ameresco project is living proof that solar power is just as reliable as any other energy resource, with a mature supply chain and knowledge base to back it up.

Take the Florida-based firm Cantsink, for example. A specialist in  piling specialist infrastructure, Cantskin installed the supports for  the solar racking system in the new microgrid.

“Cantsink delivered a foundation system engineered to withstand the demanding conditions of a military testing range,” the company reports in a recap of the project on its website.

“Given the site conditions, pull testing was performed to determine pile requirements, which confirmed that 17’6″ piles were needed to support the racking system,” Cantsink further explains.

Among other details, Cantsink notes that the racking system is supported by 193 8×10 piles measuring 17’6″ long. “Every bolt used had a torque requirement that was inspected by a vendor appointed third party,” Cantsink emphasizes.

Evidently the people in charge of energy systems at White Sands were satisfied with the results of their new microgrid, and the facility is on track to receive another solar-enabled microgrid. On November 3, the Kentucky-based global-facing engineering and infrastructure firm ABM described a new microgrid in the planning stages at White Sands, consisting of a carport-mounted solar power plant, BESS, and dispatchable on-site generators, presumably meaning gas generators for backup power.

The construction contract for the facility was assigned to ABM’s joint venture partner, AACON-RavenVolt. The AACON part of the venture is a general contractor based in Louisville, Kentucky. Ravenvolt is a subsidiary of ABM based in Georgia, having been acquired by the firm in 2022.

The relationship with RavenVolt is significant because ABM has positioned the partnership to zero in on microgrid opportunities throughout the federal government. “The partnership provides federal agencies with dependable, end-to-end energy resilience and infrastructure construction capabilities,” ABM notes.

Keep an eye on ABM and RavenVolt moving forward, as EV stakeholders in the US anticipate an uptick in sales following President Donald Trump’s decision to start a war against Iran last February. That will increase the demand for kilowatts, and RavenVolt is among those advocating for solar-enabled microgrids to avoid grid-related bottlenecks.

“It’s all about the easy integration of on-site renewables, whether that’s solar, battery or ensuring on-site resiliency and expansion capability,” explains RavenVolt co-founder and President of Sales Cody George. The company’s microgrid model is designed for ease of installation, combining whole-of-system standardization while tailoring the switchgear to customer-specific applications.

“We’re not beholden to one technology, so our customers aren’t locked into outdated infrastructure forcing them to completely retool,” George adds.

Circling back around to military applications, it’s not surprising to see solar power resurfacing as a resilient energy resource over at the Defense Department, regardless of last year’s sharp U-turn in federal energy policy. Resiliency was front and center in the early 2000’s, when the Defense Department began pushing the envelope on solar power while the civilian market was still spinning its wheels. Solar panels were still relatively expensive back then, and the civilian market was slow on the uptake. Energy storage systems were more expensive, too.

As one indication that the Defense Department is still carving out its own energy destiny, get a load of Defense Secretary Pete Hesgeth’s new “FORTRESS America” resiliency plan. The goal is to make major Defense Department facilities in the US energy independent for at least 90 days, alongside other emergency stockpiling measures.

Nuclear and fossil fuels are part of the plan, but keep an eye on the solar-plus-storage combo to be first out of the box on a speed-to-power basis.

Photo: White Sands Missile Range celebrated the launch of an on-site, solar-enabled microgrid last year, and the facility has already begun planning for another microgrid with solar power (cropped, courtesy of Cantsink).

#5827. Trump admin ‘pauses’ leases for five offshore wind projects already under construction

The U.S. Dept. of the Interior has immediately paused the leases for all large-scale offshore wind projects under construction in the United States due to “national security risks identified by the Dept. of War in recently completed classified reports.”

“The prime duty of the United States government is to protect the American people,” said Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum. “Today’s action addresses emerging national security risks, including the rapid evolution of the relevant adversary technologies, and the vulnerabilities created by large-scale offshore wind projects with proximity near our East Coast population centers. The Trump administration will always prioritize the security of the American people.”

The Dept. of the Interior claims that the blade movement and reflective towers create “radar interference called ‘clutter.'” Allegedly, this is a national security risk.

Dominion Energy released the following statement in response to the pause issued for the Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project:

“The Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind Project (CVOW) is essential for American national security and meeting Virginia’s dramatically growing energy needs, the fastest growth in America. This growth is driven by the need to provide reliable power to many of America’s most important war fighting installations, the world’s largest warship manufacturer, and the largest concentration of data centers on the planet as well as the leading edge of the AI revolution.

Stopping CVOW for any length of time will threaten grid reliability for some of the nation’s most important war fighting, AI and civilian assets. It will also lead to energy inflation and threaten thousands of jobs.

CVOW is American-owned and benefits all of our Virginia customers. Our customers are paying for the project after a careful review of project costs and benefits by Virginia state regulators in 2022. These same state regulators, along with numerous federal agencies, oversee our cyber and physical security program, which is among the strongest in the energy industry.

The project has been more than 10 years in the works, involved close coordination with the military, and is located 27 to 44 miles offshore, so far offshore it does not raise visual impact concerns. The project’s two pilot turbines have been operating for five years without causing any impacts to national security.

CVOW enjoys bipartisan support and is within months of generating a massive 2,600 MW to support the fastest growing part of America’s energy grid. This growth serves the largest concentration of critical infrastructure in the world.

Virginia’s All-American, All-Of-The-Above-Energy Plan requires a range of power generation assets, including natural gas, advanced nuclear, and renewables. Virginia needs every electron we can get as our demand for electricity doubles. These electrons will power the data centers that will win the AI race, support our war fighters, and build the nuclear warships needed to maintain our maritime supremacy. Virginia’s grid needs addition of electrons, not subtraction.

We stand ready to do what is necessary to get these vital electrons flowing as quickly as possible.”

#5826. Overshoot Demands a New Era of Adaptation, Loss & Damage, and Climate Justice

As the world is on track to exceed 1.5 degrees Celsius of global warming, climate action must double down but also go beyond emissions cuts to prioritize adaptation, respond to loss and damage, and confront growing inequality.

For decades, climate policy has been dominated by a central question: How do we avoid exceeding 1.5 degrees Celsius of global warming above preindustrial levels? An overshoot world—where warming exceeds 1.5 degrees Celsius before potentially declining—means more than higher temperatures; it means escalating risks to the communities, ecosystems, and species already bearing the brunt of climate change, with catastrophic consequences for many coastal regions and small island states.

The United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) report on climate overshoot challenges us to confront a reality that many policymakers have been reluctant to fully acknowledge: The world will exceed 1.5 degrees Celsius. Decades of insufficient climate action have placed us in this predicament.

Aggressive emissions reductions can limit how far and for how long global warming exceeds that threshold. However, a future decline in global temperatures would not erase the losses experienced along the way. That reality should sharpen global climate ambition and broaden our understanding of what climate action requires. It is equally important to prepare for and respond to the escalating impacts that communities and ecosystems will experience in the decades between crossing 1.5 degrees Celsius and hopefully returning to a temperature below it.

One of the UNEP report’s central messages is that mitigation and adaptation are fundamentally intertwined, and both will be essential in navigating an overshoot world. The report also emphasizes the growing need for transformational adaptation: actions that go beyond incremental adjustments to fundamentally reshape systems, institutions, and development pathways in response to escalating climate risks.

Yet there is an alarming disconnect between the scale of adaptation that is needed and the reality on the ground. Many countries are still struggling to finance and implement basic adaptation measures. Adaptation finance remains grossly inadequate, national adaptation plans are underfunded, and frontline communities continue to lack the resources needed to prepare for worsening risks. The Adaptation Gap Report 2025 estimated adaptation finance needs in developing countries at up to US$365 billion per year by 2035, while international adaptation finance flows from developed countries reached just US$35 billion in 2024, only around 10 percent of what is needed.

There is reason for optimism. After decades of advocacy, adaptation is finally receiving greater recognition as a core pillar of climate action. Negotiations on the global goal on adaptation (GGA)—which aims to enhance adaptive capacity, strengthen resilience, and reduce vulnerability—have elevated adaptation within international climate discussions. Initiatives such as the Fostering Investable National Implementation (FINI) are also helping to mobilize public and private investments to scale adaptation efforts.

But overshoot forces us to think beyond closing today’s adaptation gaps.

What happens when livelihoods—particularly those connected to our food system—become untenable because of chronic drought, extreme heat, or repeated disasters? What happens when infrastructure designed for one climate must function in a dramatically different one; when transit and energy hubs become inoperable due to heat? What happens when fundamental changes in land use become unavoidable, and our forests and farmlands can’t keep up with those changes?

“The challenge is not deciding which type of climate action deserves support. We need all three: mitigation, adaptation, and responses to loss and damage.”

These are questions of transformation, requiring us to rethink how societies are organized, how development occurs, and how communities can thrive in a warmer world with greater climate impacts. The challenge is that we are asking societies to prepare for transformational adaptation before many governments have fully integrated adaptation into planning, investment, and governance.

Governments and funders—both public and private—must therefore treat adaptation as an investment in economic stability, public health, food security, water security, and human dignity, not as a secondary environmental issue. Support must be predictable, accessible, timely, and responsive to local priorities. Researchers, practitioners, governments, businesses, and communities must work together to envision, plan for, and implement systemic change, from rethinking infrastructure and land use to reshaping governance, livelihoods, and development pathways.

At COP31, progress on the GGA must be a priority. Countries must move beyond the stalled discussions to reach agreement on how adaptation progress will be measured and how implementation will be accelerated. Discussions on preparing for the next global stocktake must also grapple more explicitly with the implications of overshoot for adaptation and adaptation finance.

One of the most important implications of overshoot is that there are climate impacts that are unpreventable and irreversible. Some impacts exceed the capacity of households, institutions, and ecosystems to adjust, and others occur despite preparation. When communities lose lands to sea level rise, when repeated disasters force permanent displacement, when cultural heritage disappears, or when ecosystems collapse, we are no longer talking only about adaptation. We are talking about loss and damage.

Countries and communities need support for recovery and reconstruction, assistance with displacement and planned relocation, protection of livelihoods and cultural heritage, and solutions for slow-onset impacts such as sea level rise, desertification, and glacier loss. The scale of these needs is already staggering. Estimates find that loss and damage costs in developing countries could reach up to US$671 billion annually by 2030, and those figures only capture economic losses. They do not account for noneconomic losses, such as lives lost, cultural heritage erased, or permanent damage to ecosystems.

Yet climate policy discussions continue to focus primarily on the relationship between mitigation and adaptation. In an overshoot world, that is no longer enough. Responses to loss and damage must become a central component of climate action. The establishment of the Fund for Responding to Loss and Damage (FRLD) was an important step, but its success will ultimately depend on whether it mobilizes finance at the scale required and whether support reaches affected people quickly, directly, and equitably.

Loss and damage are also inseparable from questions of justice. The countries and communities contributing the least to climate change are those facing the greatest risks of irreversible harm. Small Island Developing States, Indigenous Peoples, women, low-income communities, and many countries across the Global South continue to bear disproportionate impacts despite contributing little to the problem.

Climate change has always raised questions about who bears the costs, who receives protection, and who gets left behind. Overshoot makes those questions even more urgent. If we are serious about navigating an overshoot world, loss and damage can no longer remain at the margins of climate policy. It must become a core pillar of climate action.

At COP31, loss and damage must be integrated across the full climate agenda rather than confined to a stand-alone negotiation track. Parties should move beyond procedural discussions and focus on delivering finance through the FRLD and other loss and damage funding arrangements at the scale required, with resources flowing quickly from pledges to actual disbursement. Access to support must become faster, more predictable, and responsive to the needs of affected communities. Discussions under the next global stocktake should also place loss and damage at the center of assessing collective progress, recognizing that overshoot will bring unavoidable and irreversible impacts that demand dedicated responses. Doing so will require rethinking current international approaches to climate finance and accountability, ensuring that support for loss and damage is treated as a core pillar of climate action alongside mitigation and adaptation.

One of the biggest barriers to addressing overshoot is the current approach to competing climate action. Too often, climate discussions become debates about mitigation versus adaptation and adaptation versus loss and damage. Overshoot makes it clear that this is the wrong conversation.

The challenge is not deciding which type of climate action deserves support. We need all three: mitigation, adaptation, and responses to loss and damage. These are mutually reinforcing responsibilities, not competitors. For climate finance, the problem is not only how we divide the pie; the problem is that the pie itself remains sorely inadequate for the challenges ahead of us. An overshoot world requires a dramatic expansion of overall climate finance that must be equitably distributed to mitigation, adaptation, and responses to loss and damage.

The findings from the UNEP report should serve as a blaring alarm—not only because overshoot is a reality, but because it forces us to confront whether our current systems, institutions, financing mechanisms, and political processes are capable of delivering the transformation required. An overshoot world is fundamentally about people: whether societies can adapt, how we respond to loss and damage, and whether the greatest burdens continue to fall on those who contributed the least to the problem.In an era of overshoot, our attention, ambition, and investment must finally match that reality.

By Dr. Adelle Thomas, Senior Director, Climate Adaptation, Environmental Health, NRDC

NRDC is the nation's most effective environmental action group, combining the grassroots power of 1.3 million members and online activists with the courtroom clout and expertise of more than 350 lawyers, scientists, and other professionals.

#5825. Surprise, Surprise: Short-Sighted Automakers Back Trump's Fuel Economy Standards Rollback

Good morning! It's Tuesday, November 3, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift, your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.

In this morning's edition, car companies are all in favor of the Trump administration's rollback of fuel economy rules while they simultaneously urge Congress to pass the permanent ban on Chinese vehicle imports they've been whining about. Also, Europe has all but forgiven Tesla CEO Elon Musk and a chip shortage at Stellantis could hamper Fiat output in Italy.

If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.

The Alliance for Automotive Innovation, a group representing some of the biggest automakers in the world, including General Motors, Toyota, Hyundai, Ford, Stellantis and Volkswagen, said it was backing the Trump administration in a legal challenge to its sharply lower vehicle fuel economy standards that had been filed by California, New York and two dozen other states, cities and counties around the country. I'll tell ya what, I'm not surprised, but I'm sure those automakers will be once those fuel economy rollbacks are changed in the next administration (whenever that comes).

Last month, the U.S. Department of Transportation said it was finalizing a fleet-wide fuel economy average of 34.9 mpg by 2031. That's down from the 50.4 mpg mark that was set by the Biden administration in 2024. The group argued that automakers require "certainty and stability" in standards. From Reuters:

It added that if the standards were set aside, it "would affect automakers' ​business, endangering the capital investment planning, product development, and performance validation for hundreds of ​new vehicle models intended for the US market."

The states said the Trump administration was ignoring the presence ‌of millions ⁠of electric vehicles, "leading to a flawed, dramatically distorted analysis of the 'maximum feasible' fuel economy level." The automaker group said the court should reject those arguments.

The revised Trump administration standards seek to reverse Biden's push toward greater fuel efficiency and electric vehicles. A coalition of environmental ​and consumer groups ​also filed a separate ⁠lawsuit challenging the new rules.

The Transportation Department said the regulation reduces manufacturers' average cost per vehicle to comply by $1,289 but increases fuel ​costs by more than $1,600 over the life of the vehicles.

The department ​said the ⁠rule would increase US gasoline consumption through 2050 by 4.6%, or about 121 billion gallons, compared with the Biden rules but also boost new car sales.

The American Petroleum Institute, an oil ⁠and natural ​gas industry trade group, also filed in support of ​the Trump administration's rules, saying they ensure the government sets standards that "do not force consumers to purchase vehicles that ​consume less fuel than they would otherwise choose."

Automakers can talk all they want about being environmentally sustainable and eco-friendly, but at the end of the day, it's very clear that what it all comes down to is the bottom line. It'll certainly be interesting to see what the fallout will be once a new, more eco-conscious administration enters the White House in a few years' time.

That same group, the Alliance for Automotive Innovation, is currently on a heater. Not only are they going after fuel economy standards. They're also continuing their push to get Congress to ban the import of Chinese EVs to the U.S. Automakers have been whining about this for years at this point, and now they'd like the bill to pass when lawmakers return next week for a quick session following today's midterm elections.

Back in September, the Senate sponsors of a bipartisan bill to effectively ban Chinese-made vehicles from entering the U.S. agreed to delay the effort to fast-track the bill until November, despite the fact it had overwhelming support from other congresspeople who apparently aren't a fan of a free market or freedom of choice for the consumer. There are some officials who believe that if Dems win control of Congress, there may not be enough time to approve the ban before the session ends in December. Whatever will these OEMs do??? From Reuters:

"We need to ​protect the US market from a flood of Chinese imports that carry ​surveillance risk and disadvantage current manufacturers that have made generational investments in ⁠American communities," said John Bozzella, who heads the Alliance for Automotive Innovation.

"Given the ​obvious national security risk and China's pattern of unfair behavior, Congress should protect ​our defense and advanced manufacturing base and stop Chinese automakers from doing here what they've gotten away ‌with ⁠in Europe," Bozzella said.

Bozzella said in the first eight months of 2026, the EU imported more than 1.1 million vehicles from China — more than all of 2025.

A Senate committee passed the bill, which has significant momentum, in July and a House version now tops 100 co-sponsors. ​Automakers and legislative aides ​hope to win full ⁠passage this year.

A regulation imposed by the former ​Biden administration ⁠in early 2025 effectively banned Chinese automakers from selling or building passenger vehicles in the US because they could send sensitive driver data to China. Washington also maintains more ⁠than 100% ​tariffs on Chinese electric vehicles.

The Chinese foreign ministry ​has previously urged the United States "to respect the laws of the market economy and principles of fair competition," ​criticizing previous proposals against Chinese companies as "unreasonable suppression."

President Trump, wildcard that he is, sparked a hell of a lot of worry among key stakeholders when he said he would accept Chinese automakers building cars in the U.S., but so far no one has taken him up on the offer.

Of course, the whole "national security angle of this story is just steeped in irony. After all, I'm writing this article on my Chinese-made laptop, and you're reading it on your Chinese-made phone.

I guess time really does heal all wounds. Well, that, and staying out of the spotlight as best as Elon really can.

#5824. Stratos Data Center Will Emit Air Pollution That’s Linked to Premature Deaths and Health Economic Damages for Utah & States Downwind

A new report models emissions from different on-site methane generation power scenarios to show potential impacts on the people of Utah and residents of multiple downwind states.

Canadian businessman and television personality “Mr. Wonderful” (aka Kevin O’Leary) and his businesses have proposed the Stratos data center in Box Elder County, Utah, near Salt Lake City, that  will be one of the largest data centers in the world, requiring as much as 9 gigawatts (GW) of on-site power from burning methane gas. The Box Elder County Commission approved the project in May—under highly contentious circumstances and under a highly unusual arrangement within a project area created by Utah’s Military Installation Development Authority.

A new report prepared by EmPower Analytics Group for NRDC estimates that the air pollution, health harms, and economic damages from the proposed Stratos data center would be substantial: “[a]t the potential 9 GW full build-out, air pollution from Stratos’s methane-gas power generation is estimated to cause approximately 5–45 additional premature deaths and $75–$690 million in health-related economic damages every year.” The report also discusses additional health harms that extend beyond premature deaths and include new asthma cases, nonfatal heart attacks, hospital visits, and lost work and school days.

These ranges reflect scenarios that model the health harms and economic damage caused by air pollution from two possible methane-gas primary power generating technologies: modern combined-cycle combustion turbines and methane gas–fired reciprocating internal combustion engines (RICE). The primary harmful air pollution consists of tiny soot particles—particulate matter of 2.5 microns or less (PM2.5)—about 30 times smaller than the width of an average human hair.

The report further finds that over a 30-year period, at the potential 9 GW full build-out, the health and economic harms from this PM2.5 air pollution “could accumulate to approximately 856–1,350 premature deaths and $13.5–$20.7 billion under gas-fired RICE, compared with 145–253 premature deaths and $2.3–$3.8 billion under combined-cycle gas.”

Of the two methane-gas primary power generation technologies evaluated in the report, one uses a smaller number of large gas combustion turbines, while the RICE scenario reflected electricity generated by many individual engines operating in parallel. Indeed, the RICE scenario is patterned on a methane gas–powered data center with 69 RICE generators that the Utah Division of Air Quality permitted in Millard County in early 2026. RICE generators produce higher emissions than combined-cycle gas at the same generation capacity. Accordingly, the report finds that “[t]he technology used to power Stratos could make a five- to sixfold difference in its estimated health harms.”

Importantly, the modeled combined-cycle gas scenario does not estimate the emissions from the use of on-site diesel generators for backup power because those plans by Stratos remain unknown. (The report modeled diesel backup generators for the RICE scenario, scaled from the Millard County permit example.) Diesel generators are significantly dirtier than comparably sized methane gas generators, emitting 95 percent more smog-forming nitrogen oxides than an equivalent gas generator as well as 98 percent more non-methane hydrocarbons and 59 percent more carbon monoxide. Harmful PM2.5 emissions will be even higher from any data center employing diesel generators (the scale of that additional pollution would depend on whether they were truly used for “emergency conditions” versus being run more frequently; state permitting parameters for backup diesels is a critical issue for all data center developments).

The highest concentrations of harmful air pollution from the Stratos data center will occur near the facility in Box Elder County, neighboring areas of northern Utah (including Salt Lake City), and southern Idaho. But the health harms and economic damage from Stratos would extend well beyond these areas: The report finds that more than 80 percent of health harms and health-related economic damages occur outside the state, reaching downwind populations in Colorado, Wyoming, and as far away as Illinois. The report appendix identifies the full list of potentially impacted states.

The Stratos developers have maintained from the start that the massive data center will be powered by methane gas. “That’s never going to happen,” Utah Governor Spencer Cox told the Salt Lake Tribune in May. “The very first phase will be natural gas, but the other phases should not be. They should be nuclear, and they should be geothermal, and solar and other technology.” But the most recent filing by the Stratos developers with the state still show plans for the full build-out of the data center to be powered by methane gas. This filing shows that the developers now say that they plan a 7.5 GW data center using the dirtier and more harmful RICE generators that burn methane gas. None of these filings commit the developers to doing or not doing something different from what they are saying now, a reality that has characterized this data center project—and many other proposed data center projects nationally.

Data centers in Utah or anywhere else, of course, need not be powered by highly polluting, harmful fossil fuels like methane gas. Other data center developers have been doing better jobs showing how, in stark contrast to the Stratos proposal, it is possible to power these facilities with cleaner energy—such as a robust portfolio of wind, solar, geothermal, and batteries—and maxing out demand side resources.

NRDC commissioned this important EmPower Analytics report to raise awareness and concerns about the harmful health impacts and associated economic damages from dangerous air pollution caused by data centers like the proposed Stratos Project in Utah and by the proliferation of data centers across the United States. These harms arise especially from hyperscaler data centers powered by fossil fuels, like Stratos. While the dangers and costs of excessive water use by data centers are more well known, the deadly toll of air pollution—a silent killer—deserves greater attention and concern.

Harmful air pollution should be met with the best air pollution control technologies available. Data centers should be powered by new, additional, deliverable clean energy that does not release air pollution, along with batteries, storage, and other smart solutions. If someone nicknamed Mr. Wonderful wants to earn that name, he should live up to it, starting with the Stratos data center.

By John Walke, Director, Federal Clean Air & Senior Attorney, Environmental Health, NRDC

#5823. Participation in Sunrun’s Puerto Rico VPP program doubled in 2026

Sunrun announced that participation in its Puerto Rico distributed power plant has doubled to more than 60,000 customers, representing nearly 70,000 home batteries. The distributed power plant frequently and reliably delivers 45 MW of dispatchable power for four hours at a time, providing critical support to Puerto Rico’s electric grid.

Since April, Sunrun has dispatched its power plant more than 60 times to weather severe energy shortfalls and prevent rolling blackouts. During an extended period of unseasonably hot temperatures this fall, Sunrun dispatched its home batteries to the grid for 18 consecutive days beginning October 13.

Sunrun’s Puerto Rico distributed power plant has scaled rapidly since 2024, when approximately 4,000 customers were enrolled. Enrollment surged to more than 30,000 customers in 2025. Sunrun is the largest aggregator in Puerto Rico’s Customer Battery Energy Sharing program.

Puerto Rico’s electric utility operator, LUMA, said in June and again in July that energy from home batteries helped “maintain system stability and prevent outages that would have affected thousands of customers.”

“What began in Puerto Rico out of necessity for family resilience has become a vital blueprint for the U.S. mainland, where severe weather and the proliferation of AI data centers are demanding new energy solutions,” said Sunrun CEO Mary Powell. “Sunrun is uniquely positioned to provide these solutions, whether to support a grid at its breaking point or to provide capacity for hyperscalers looking to come online.”

Sunrun’s increased dispatching capacity in Puerto Rico comes after residents on the island experienced an average of 36 hours of power outages in 2025 that were not caused by major adverse weather, which is 19% more than the previous year, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration. When severe weather is factored in, residents experienced an additional 23 hours of power interruptions.

Customers who participate in Sunrun’s Puerto Rico distributed power plant choose how much energy to share, with most contributing a small portion of their total battery capacity. This approach ensures they retain reserve power for personal backup power in the event of grid outages. Participants are financially compensated for the power they choose to share, while Sunrun generates recurring revenue by operating the distributed power plant.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5822. These Are The Cars You'd Lease But Never Buy

#5821. Smarter Cooling, Stronger Reliability: Advancing Wind Turbine Gearbox Performance

As the wind energy industry matures, operators and service providers are increasingly focused on maximizing turbine availability while controlling long-term maintenance costs. Although major drivetrain components often receive the spotlight, one overlooked system can have an outsized impact on turbine reliability and performance: gearbox oil cooling.

Heat exchangers play a critical role in maintaining proper oil temperature and viscosity within wind turbine gearboxes. When cooling systems become clogged with airborne debris, dust, or organic material, oil temperatures rise, lubrication performance declines, and component stress increases. Over time, these conditions can contribute to reduced gearbox efficiency, elevated maintenance demands, and avoidable downtime.

“Wind operators today are balancing increasing performance expectations with the realities of aging fleets and rising maintenance costs. Technologies that improve cooling efficiency and reduce service exposure can have a meaningful impact on long-term turbine reliability and operational profitability,” according to Mike Erickson, HYDAC’s Market Manager for the Wind Industry.

Traditional “panel cut” heat exchanger designs have long been susceptible to clogging due to their tightly packed fin structures. In many wind environments — particularly agricultural, dusty, or high-pollen regions — debris accumulation can severely restrict airflow. Oil leaks can further compound the issue by trapping contaminants within the exchanger core, making cleaning more difficult and labor intensive.

This operational challenge has driven the need for more resilient cooling technologies designed specifically for real-world wind farm conditions.

HYDAC’s new “Square Wave” heat exchanger design represents a significant advancement in addressing these reliability concerns. Engineered to allow debris to pass more freely through the exchanger fins, the design minimizes clogging while improving cooling efficiency under demanding operating conditions.

The importance of effective cooling cannot be overstated. Wind turbine gearbox oil typically operates within an optimal temperature range of approximately 45°C to 65°C. Even modest temperature increases can significantly impact oil viscosity. According to field data, a 12°C rise in oil temperature can reduce viscosity by nearly 28 percent — affecting lubrication quality and accelerating wear on critical drivetrain components.

Field trials have demonstrated measurable operational benefits from the Square Wave design. During a one-year trial on GE 1.x turbines, the upgraded exchanger consistently reduced clogging and lowered maintenance requirements compared to traditional panel cut cores. Operators reported reduced up-tower cleaning time and fewer temperature-related cooling faults.

Field validation across multiple turbine platforms has further reinforced the operational advantages of the Square Wave design. In one case study involving legacy 1 MW-class turbines operating in the Pacific Northwest, a major renewable energy operator installed pilot sets of Square Wave heat exchangers as part of a cooling performance evaluation. Installation teams reported the upgraded exchangers integrated seamlessly into the existing platform with no retrofit complications.

After initial operating data demonstrated improved cooling consistency, the operator expanded the evaluation program with additional units. Long-term SCADA analysis later showed turbines equipped with Square Wave technology operate approximately 20 to 25 percent cooler than comparable turbines using conventional heat exchanger designs under similar operating conditions. The data also highlighted another important advantage: the Square Wave cores continued to maintain airflow and cooling efficiency in debris-heavy environments where traditional exchanger designs became increasingly restricted over time.

The successful deployment also generated interest from additional wind service organizations seeking retrofit-ready cooling upgrades that could improve turbine reliability while reducing maintenance burdens across aging fleets.

“What operators are increasingly recognizing is that small improvements in thermal management can create meaningful gains in turbine reliability, maintenance efficiency, and long-term asset performance,” said Erickson. “The field data we’re seeing continues to validate the value of designing cooling systems specifically for real-world wind operating environments.”

More recently, long-term SCADA data from Mitsubishi platforms further validated the technology’s performance. After two years of operation, turbines equipped with Square Wave heat exchangers operated approximately 19 to 25 percent cooler than reference turbines using standard exchanger designs under comparable operating conditions.

For wind asset owners and service providers, the implications are substantial: Improved thermal management supports healthier oil viscosity, more stable gearbox operation, and potentially longer component life. At the same time, reduced cleaning frequency and fewer cooling-related service events translate directly into lower operational expenditures and increased turbine availability.

As the industry continues to prioritize lifecycle optimization and energy production efficiency, innovative subsystem upgrades such as advanced heat exchanger technology are becoming increasingly valuable. Rather than waiting for costly failures or performance degradation, operators are seeking practical retrofit solutions that improve reliability without requiring major system redesigns.

“Effective thermal management is critical to protecting gearbox health and maintaining oil performance over time. By addressing one of the most common causes of cooling degradation — debris accumulation — operators can improve uptime while reducing unnecessary maintenance interventions,” said HYDAC’s Erickson.

For wind stakeholders evaluating ways to improve gearbox reliability, reduce maintenance exposure, and enhance turbine performance, cooling system optimization deserves greater attention. Proven retrofit technologies like advanced heat exchanger designs can offer a practical path toward improved uptime and operational efficiency without requiring major system overhauls.

As fleets age and performance expectations continue to rise, collaboration between operators, service providers, and technology partners will be essential to building more resilient wind assets for the future. Now is the time to evaluate whether existing cooling systems are supporting — or limiting — long-term turbine reliability goals.

“HYDAC’s goal is to help wind owners and service providers solve practical operational challenges in ways that create long-term value across the entire asset lifecycle. Innovations like the Square Wave heat exchanger are designed to improve reliability, simplify maintenance, and support greater energy production over time.”

Don’t let cooling system limitations impact turbine performance.

Proactive cooling optimization can help operators reduce maintenance exposure, protect critical drivetrain components, and maximize energy production across the asset lifecycle. To learn how HYDAC’s Square Wave heat exchanger technology is helping wind operators improve reliability and lower operating costs, connect with our wind energy specialists today or visit: https://www.hydac.com/en-us/industry-solutions/renewable-energy/wind-power/

#5820. The Jaguar Type 01's U.S.-Spec Bumper Is Fantastically Well Integrated

We may be long past the days of requiring extraordinarily ugly impact bumpers be affixed to cars sold in the U.S., with those laws extending to supercars like the Lamborghini Countach, cars that are driving around on American roads still aren't quite as smooth as they may be in other markets. Whether for insurance reasons like the Bugatti Chiron or to meet regulatory crash safety regulations, U.S.-spec versions of some cars (typically wild-looking, high-end ones) sometimes get bumpers that protrude slightly further out, or have bumperettes or bumper guards added on.

The latest victim of this is the Jaguar Type 01, except its little U.S.-spec bumperette solution is actually extremely well done, perfectly integrated with its outrageous styling. I got to see the Type 01 in person for the first time at the Las Vegas Concours over the weekend, and I love it even more in the metal than I already did in photos. Luckily our American-market modifications haven't ruined that.

At the bottom of the body-color strakes in the upright rear end, the Type 01 has a black bar stretching across the same width that houses the parking sensors, with the license plate holder sitting just below it along with a fang-like diffuser design. On the U.S.-spec Type 01, that black bar juts outward a little more dramatically, with angled sections at the ends instead of it being flat all the way across. The rest of the rear end design is exactly the same, and the protruding bumper flows so perfectly with the car's design, you probably wouldn't even notice it if the two cars were side by side.

There is one other difference between Euro- and U.S.-spec Type 01s you may have noticed. Here in America our cars need to have side marker lights and reflectors at front and the rear; usually automakers will integrated these as subtly as possible, maybe along the curve of the wheel arches, following the design of the bumper, or even putting them within the headlights and taillights themselves. Jaguar, though, decided to make them a more prominent part of the car's design.

The Type 01's side markers are squares, positioned low down in the corners of the front and rear bumpers where their lines are aligned with other lines of the car. Yeah, they stand out, and some people will find them to be an eyesore, but I actually like this placement. At least nothing else about the Type 01's lighting design had to be changed for the U.S. market — the headlight arrays are still ultra-thin, and the pair of full-width taillights are still hidden between those strakes.

Other than those two little details, the Type 01's design is identical across markets, which is a good thing. Love it or hate it, you've got to respect Jaguar really swinging for the fences here and creating production car with pure concept-y looks, like it traveled back in time to show off how wild cars will look in the future.
