#5819. China Truck Sales Now Majority Electric. What’s Next?

EV heavy trucks hit 50.3% market share in China for September, with sales up 34% from a year ago. Long-haul big rig market share hit 64.5%. China is on track to have over 40% EV market share for the entire year, reaching their 2030 goal in a matter of months. This is not the first time China’s heavy truck market share topped 50%, but that previous blip in December last year was prior to subsidies ending, followed by a dip in January. Current sales rates are not bolstered by the subsidy and are now on a much more stable upward trajectory.

The speed of EV adoption for heavy trucks in China has recently grown much faster than for cars, having risen from just 13.6% in 2024. When I visited Guangzhou in November of last year and again in April of this year, I noticed a lot more green plates on trucks, indicating that they were electric. It seems that my perception is backed by sales data.

With the addition of LNG trucks, diesel’s share of heavy trucks has dropped to roughly 40%. While LNG sales have also declined with the rate of EV adoption, diesel trucks could soon make up less than 1/3 of sales and decline further from there. China could feasibly double its 2030 goal and hit 80% EV penetration, by which point many diesel models are likely to be discontinued.

With these levels of market penetration, in addition to retirements, China’s diesel demand is on track to fall by about 230,000 barrels per day this year, despite increased freight movement. Every additional electric truck that replaces a diesel truck then builds on that reduction. Due to their duty cycle, each electric truck sold has a much greater impact on fuel consumption than each car sold. And the price of fuel combined with the duty cycle has a much greater impact on overall truck economics.

To be clear, these improving results are not due to subsidies, as they appear in the year after subsidies were cut. They are also not due to mandates, as the 2030 goals were met before the ink was dry on their announcement. EV trucks are now more affordable to own and operate than diesel trucks in China. The increase in diesel prices has only increased the cost gap.

Last year, roughly 90% of all electric truck sales globally were in China. That percentage is set to expand this year, despite rising sales in some other countries. Those sales are simply not rising fast enough outside of China to keep pace with China. In comparison, the EU is looking at 2.5% EV market share this year. China is on pace to average roughly as many electric trucks sold per week as Europe sells per year. The US EV truck market looks like a rounding error in comparison at less than one percent.

In addition, China has rapidly been building out heavy truck charging infrastructure as part of the 28 million charging facilities planned to be in operation by the end of 2027, up from 21 million last March. This not only focuses on the major cities but also extends into more rural areas that have been slower to adopt electrification. In addition, several companies are building out battery swap stations. While we have yet to see if battery swap or MW+ charging wins in the truck market, expect the competition between the two approaches to be fierce.

Within Tier 1 cities, I could see a potential phase out of diesel trucks in the near future. Hainan province is already planning to end ICE passenger car sales by 2030, and I expect that to extend to diesel trucks as well. Although rural areas have been slower to adopt, they also tend to be less wealthy and thus more impacted by the changing economics. If diesel prices stay elevated while charging becomes convenient, many rural businesses will not be able to afford to buy new diesel trucks. With the end of diesel truck sales, strict emissions standards and a potential return of scrappage incentives could take remaining diesel vehicles off the road at an accelerated rate.

With fewer diesel trucks on the road in the largest market globally, diesel consumption is set to decline. In addition, the supply chain for diesel parts will also start to decline. As protectionism has targeted electric vehicles, rather than internal combustion  engine (ICE) vehicles, a decline in Chinese (ICE) parts has a potentially significant impact on global markets.

Of course, as EVs take over the majority of heavy truck sales in the fiercely competitive Chinese market, Chinese manufacturers will start looking outside of the country for growth.

The traditional thought would be to look at wealthy Western countries that have substantial buying power. However, many Chinese companies may be a bit reluctant to focus on those markets after the protectionist efforts against electric passenger vehicles. Many European markets, like Germany, also have very high electricity prices, which makes the financial proposition less attractive for commercial vehicles that are heavily motivated by costs. Chinese companies may start focusing elsewhere.

The Middle Corridor could become a focus. This essentially rebuilds the Silk Road land route from China, across the Caspian Sea, on to Turkey. Many of the countries along this route  once had prosperous trade, only to fall into decline when ocean trade routes were established. However, land transport is much easier to electrify than long ocean voyages. China is investing heavily with countries like Kazakhstan along the route with renewable electricity infrastructure. Electrified rail is expanding by 26-fold in Turkey. Electrified trucking is the next logical step, connecting the rail lines to more local production along the route.

Other Belt and Road Initiative (BRI) countries could also benefit. In countries like Ethiopia, which has expensive diesel but electricity prices to businesses of just $0.017/kWh, electric trucks could make perfect sense to transport between new factories and new ports. Trading partners like Vietnam and Thailand could also see an influx.

All this is not to say that local companies will not benefit. Unlike passenger cars, heavy equipment tends to be more modular. Due to the bulk and mass of the vehicles, final assembly is more likely to be localized, with greater use of local suppliers.

Overall, we could see much of the “Rest of the World” (ROW) leapfrog legacy economies in EV truck adoption. The purchase price is no longer prohibitive in China, and the operating costs are much lower. With infrastructure buildout and increasing renewable installed capacity, electric trucking is poised to take over rapidly.

That is not to say that the West will be completely left out. Countries with less prohibitive trade policies toward EVs will likely see increased adoption. While EVs have taken over passenger car sales in Norway, their trucking industry still has a way to go at just 14.4%. And the geographically most eastern country typically lumped in with the “West,” Australia, is likely to see EV truck sales follow a similar pattern to EV car sales. If Canada continues to open up, they could also benefit. Of course, as the majority of the economic benefit from electric trucks comes from their use, inflated trucking costs hurt every company that moves products by truck, and those inflated costs then lead to inflation throughout the economy. That impacts overall affordability. As such, there is a chance that protectionists do not put as much effort into undermining electric truck imports as they put into passenger EVs.

And the EV heavy truck global supply chain will become increasingly well established. This could mean more parts availability for brands outside of China to build competitive vehicles, if those companies have access to global supply chains. Competitive markets will also drive innovation, moving the technology forward at an accelerating rate. Meanwhile the supply chain for diesel trucks will shrink.

While the US still seems to be figuring out how to make a safe EV Semi, battery-powered heavy trucks are becoming increasingly mature products elsewhere. As electric trucks move from majority share to complete market dominance in the largest market, other markets will follow, and the transition will become inevitable. Hopefully, the largest oil producing country in the world will not be left too far behind.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5818. I Only Want The Gorgeous Mid-Engine BMW Sports Car Because I Care About Saving The Planet, Sir, I Promise

As we all do occasionally, I found myself browsing BMW i8 listings over the weekend. A clean i8 passed me on the road, and it looked so good, I had to check prices. I mean, we're talking about a mid-engine sports car with butterfly doors and a sweet carbon-fiber chassis, so they can't be that cheap. But the engine's essentially the same three-cylinder that's found in a Mini, and there's no manual, so they can't be that expensive.

The cheapest BMW i8 I could find with a clean title is listed for $33,299, or what those of us in the business call "the danger zone." That's juuuust cheap enough that a normal person could convince themselves it's a good idea to buy a depreciated luxury car, only to get hit with huge bills. Obviously, a normal person should never buy a heavily depreciated, low-volume German car that's incredibly complicated and already more than a decade old. Me, though? I think I might replace my Fiat 500e with an i8 when the lease is up.

Obviously, since the Fiat is a coupe, and I'm never having kids, it makes sense that I'd replace one coupe with another, but you're probably assuming I want the BMW i8 because it's a gorgeous mid-engine sports car that I can buy at an affordable price. That's incorrect. You see, I actually want the beautiful BMW with the carbon-fiber chassis because I'm a good person who cares about the environment. Yes, it offers great balance and agile handling in a comfortable, daily drivable package that looks like an exotic, but I need you to believe I only want the BMW i8 so I can help save the planet.

You can buy all sorts of cars for about $33,000, but as far as practical rides go, it's hard to beat the Honda Civic Sport Touring Hybrid. It's comfortable, offers pretty much all the bells and whistles, and its hybrid powertrain is rated at 49 mpg combined. That's the kind of good, responsible car you buy, and oh, isn't it nice it also has 200 horsepower and 232 pound-feet of torque? A hybrid Civic is the car someone my age probably should buy, but I just care about not giving kids asthma too much to get the reasonable Honda.

Remember, the BMW i8 is a hybrid, too. In fact, it's a plug-in hybrid. And because of that, despite making enough power for Car and Driver to hit 60 mph in 4.0 seconds flat, it's also incredibly fuel efficient. Keep that battery topped off, and it's rated at 76 MPGe, which is more than 49 mpg. The i8 also has 15 miles of electric-only while the Civic has none. You see, I can't buy the Honda because I can't afford to. I need the BMW and its sweet billionaire doors because it's so much more efficient.

Could you imagine driving a car that got less than 50 mpg? I certainly couldn't. I need to get at least 75 mpg to feel good about all the tire particles my car feeds the salmon, and the only car I can think of that meets my needs is the BMW i8. So if you really think about it, the two-door German and its laser headlights is just the more practical choice. That's not a slight at the Civic, either. I'm sure it's a very nice car if you like modern features and refinement. It's just not the car I need because it's not the BMW i8. And I need the BMW i8 because I care about saving the planet. You believe me, right? I need you to promise you believe me here.

#5817. How Does Oahu Get to an Economically Resilient, 100% Clean Energy Future?

Oahu is a unique place. It’s the home of one of the largest clean tech incubators in the world, Elemental Impact, for a reason. Exorbitantly high electricity rates combined with ample natural resources make it an incredible laboratory to bring clean tech solutions to market. Rapid investment for WWII created a grid that was dependent on foreign oil. Path dependency (the idea that “what you know” becomes what you continue to do) hampered innovation and resulted in the creation of infrastructure (multiple power plants across multiple islands) that still, today, burns diesel fuel as large generators, to produce electricity for the island. Throw in a dependence on imported fuels that must come at least 3,000 miles to arrive, being the strategic hub for the entire Pacific theater of the U.S. military, one of the wildest weather years and hurricane seasons the island chain has ever seen AND the state’s economic dependency on tourism dollars, high cost of living, and low wages, and and wow – you’ve got yourself a puzzle and a half to solve in order to get the place to a sustainable future.

Analyst Mike Barnard loves a challenge, so he set forth to put together a roadmap to help Oahu get to 100% clean energy, which helps recirculate money in the state, lower costs, create jobs and foster economic resiliency.

Recently I gave a talk at Hawaii Tech Week in which I summarized this report, added in my own experience running an energy efficiency service that has now greened more than 20,000 homes and is saving Hawaii residents over $10 million a year on their utility bills, and then compiled the new and coming technologies that can help the island get off fossil fuels.

Mike’s report did something very important. The state’s own reporting and strategy looks at the big picture, a very difficult mountain to climb. Mike looked at that mountain and removed some of the false summits and unachievable viewpoints, resulting in a much more achievable and strategic pathway. What he did was eliminate the things we simply cannot fix right now – long distance flights, maritime bunkering, and military fuels – and tackled the problem of Oahu’s actual energy consumption for its residents. The result was that it was achievable, and there are technologies right now today that can be deployed in what he refers to as a “no regret stack” first and foremost.

I combined that with my approach, which was to showcase the economic resilience it creates. Case in point, an LED bulb saves so much money on Oahu that it pays for itself in 18 days. Every day after that, it is like change plinking in a piggy bank for the person who put it in. That builds economic resiliency for those who need it most. From LEDs to e-bikes to heat pump water heaters to balcony solar – it all adds up – fast payback periods, and financial independence.

Mike comes back to the things we simply can’t fix right now and argues that the tech will come – but for the duration of the 2020s and into the 2030s, we should be installing parking lot solar canopies, agrivoltaics, heat pump water heaters, and more.

Scott Cooney (LinkedIn) is a serial eco-entrepreneur focused on making the world a better place for all its residents. Scott is the founder of CleanTechnica and was just smart enough to hire someone smarter than him to run it. He then started Pono Home, a service that greens homes, which has performed efficiency retrofits on more than 20,000 homes and small businesses, reducing carbon pollution by more than 27 million pounds a year and saving customers more than $6.3 million a year on their utilities. Scott wanted to contribute to native ownership of the clean energy revolution, so he gifted Pono Home to a long tenured employee with native Hawaiian roots for just the liquidation value, turning down a mainland company interested in purchasing the company. In a previous life, Scott was an adjunct professor of Sustainability in the MBA program at the University of Hawai'i, a consultant at Saatchi & Saatchi S, where he worked with a team to educate and empower millions of employees to live healthier and more sustainably. He is the author of Build a Green Small Business: Profitable Ways to Become an Ecopreneur (McGraw-Hill) , and Green Living Ideas. Scott is an occasional investor, currently he has investments in Rivian (RIVN).

#5816. This Mid-90s Austrian Car Company Cobbled Together A Lineup With A Lightly Rebadged Ford, Mercury, Saturn And Dodge

Sauber isn't a strange way to say Saab, also a defunct auto brand. Nor is it in any way related to the Sauber racing team that finally closed shop in 2025, giving way for Audi to take a seat at the Formula 1 table. The Sauber we are talking about today was an extremely short-lived Austrian car brand that existed from 1993-1995, solely to import American cars to Austria to sell them under the Sauber name. Well, it was a little more complicated than that. Its relative obscurity sent YouTuber corsicar on one of the most amusing missions of discovering what exactly the brand was, and more importantly, "why?"

Prior to corsicar's deep dive into this mysterious car company, knowledge on Sauber had been little to none. It seemed to have made it into the curious car chasm in 2025 via TheTruthAboutCars, and an Autopost article done shortly after. One simply cannot internet search Google themselves into a hole for hours taking in information on Sauber otherwise. Coriscar, however, found a newspaper ad for a vehicle called a Sauber Villager. It looks strangely similar to Mercury's Villager. Almost identical, because it is the same vehicle. This gets the whole search rolling.

The Sauber brand was established by a married couple in Austria in 1993. After getting things situated in Austria, the owners founded another company in the U.S., in Florida, Sauber Automobiles USA, Inc. This was likely to get around import laws and accruing unwanted taxes.

According to a review Coriscar found of the Sauber Villager in Auto Revue magazine, the Florida office was used to purchase American vehicles that would then be registered in the U.S. From there, it could export those vehicles to Austria, where they'd get a light facelift to make changes to meet Austrian regulations, and with a little rebadging, they'd officially be "Sauber" cars, as demonstrated by the ads found and shared in the video.

The company essentially tried to make a complete lineup of in-demand or soon-to-be in-demand vehicles using different American market models. There was the family hauler, minivan or MPV Sauber Villager, which was the Mercury Villager, a Ford product, which was also a Nissan product. Sauber's mid-size sedan, coupe and or wagon, the Messager, was General Motors' Saturn S-class sedan, 2-door coupe, and wagon. The entry-level econo-car was the unfortunately named Sauber Teenager, or a Ford Aspire. And the sporty car was the Sauber S1, which was a Dodge Stealth or Mitsubishi 3000GT.

Sauber models went on sale in 1994 with 12 existing dealers. The hope was to expand to 30 by mid-year. At those dealers, interested buyers could consider the cheaper, poorly named Teenager at around $14,500 (in 1994), the Messager at $22,000, the base Villager at $41,600, its top-of-the-line First Class trim level cost around $54,700, and the S1 offered at $76,100. Just for kicks, to give you an idea of how much that costs today with inflation, the Teenager would start at $33,000 and the top of the line, sporty S1 would be just shy of $175,000.

To get the attention of potential buyers, Sauber really played with the Austrian sense of humor. One ad shown in the video reads (in Austrian) "In life you have to try everything at least once. A test drive in a Sauber doesn't hurt either." Pictured below the text was a Sauber Villager (or basically edited Mercury Villager), and above the text, a pair of boxing gloves, cigars, and a leather crotch harness for you know exactly what.

It seems the ads were not funny enough because in May 1995 documents show the Commercial Court of Vienna rejected the car company's intent to declare bankruptcy, and instead officially declared the company dissolved. Sauber was so broke, it had nothing to help pay off debtors in a bankruptcy. What ruined it isn't clear. Importing cars from another country is an expensive endeavor. Buying a car to then make costly adjustments to them to meet regulations likely didn't help either. The whole model doesn't feel very viable. It also happens that a new tax introduced in 1994 — the Austrian Value-Added Tax Act — would have subjected those imports to heavier taxation.

Also, spending $100,000 (today) on a minivan is outrageous. I can't imagine the pricing did them any favors when there were plenty of vehicles in Europe, as well as imported for a price that was much more palatable for European families.

The whole thing almost doesn't seem real, but coriscar prudently shared every bit of archived paperwork, magazines, and ads he finds to verify that Sauber was indeed real, including a 1994 review of the Villager in Austria's Auto Revue. He actually bought almost all of the Auto Revues he could find just to find this one real-life review.

Corsicar was also able to find out through extensive research that the brand had maybe sold 28 cars in that time. In reality, it's still more real than Tesla's lastest Roadster might ever be.

If you do have any bit of a soul, the last part of corsicar's video will tug at your heartstrings. The YouTuber looked into how many of these nearly extinct relics had stayed on the road by looking at Sauber registrations in Austria. The last one on the road was in 2018. By 2019, not a single Sauber remained in motion — at least in Austria. If one has survived, we'll only know if you post a photo of it in the comments.

#5815. PowerBank completes 3-MW New York community solar project

Renewable energy developer PowerBank has completed the 3.26-MW Camillus community solar project in Camillus, New York, and has received permission to operate. Solar Advocate Development, LLC, the project’s owner and EPC, built the array on a capped landfill, which it acquired from PowerBank in spring 2025 in a $7.3 million transaction.

“A closed landfill is land that most people would write off,” said Andrew van Doorn, president and COO of PowerBank. “At Camillus, our engineers and construction partners turned it into a 3.26-MW source of clean power for local households. Reaching permission to operate on a site like this reflects decades of hands-on experience, and it’s exactly the kind of project where that experience matters most.”

The project will soon accept community solar subscriptions. Solar Simplified will manage customer acquisition and subscription services for this community solar project.

#5814. Flock Won't Take No For An Answer, Keeps Cameras In Service After Communities Cancel Contracts

Cities across the country have been dropping their Flock camera contracts left and right as people object to the surveillance state they enable. Canceling these contracts isn't always a straightforward process, however. The Verge reports that Flock is not only delaying the removal of their equipment from communities that have requested it, but also reactivating it when officials take matters into their own hands.

One of these cities is Plymouth, Minnesota, where Joel Feder of The Drive was wrongfully accused of driving a stolen car based on a bad Flock hit. The city council voted to cancel its contract with Flock on September 22, and officially notified Flock the following day. From The Drive:

[Plymouth Police Chief Eric] Fadden said, "We did not hear any reply from Flock Safety Corporate or Legal, other than some communication with our local representative." Flock's silence and refusal to comply with the canceled contract forced the Police Chief to take matters into his own hands. "After hearing nothing for more than a week, I directed our staff to disconnect the power cords from the cameras in order to be sure that they are not functioning or recording plates," Fadden said.

It's rather ironic that the chief of police ended up instructing his officers to do exactly the same thing some vandals have done to disable the cameras. With Flock not responding to multiple attempts to cancel the service, this appeared to be the only way to be sure the cameras were off, as the city council's vote required.

Other cities have taken this process a step further, removing the cameras and storing them until Flock wants them back. From KARE 11:

"Once we made this decision, I personally went to the locations and removed the cameras and poles myself," Crystal police chief Pete Underthun told KARE 11 in a statement. "We are still working with the company to close out our contract and for them to pick the cameras up from our garage."

I guess what would otherwise qualify as vandalism is fine if the police do it themselves. Dayton, Minnesota, also physically removed its cameras, but even that didn't shut down the service. As the city explained in a Facebook post:

On September 17, 2026, the Police Department was made aware of a maintenance work order produced by Flock. The City notified Flock on September 18, 2026, that the program was on hold, and no work orders were to be completed. Unfortunately, the City's direction was not followed, and the camera was placed back into service without authorization. Following the report, City staff reviewed locations throughout the community to determine whether any additional cameras had been reinstalled, and one additional location was identified. The Police Department has been notified, and both cameras will be removed promptly.

Like so many things these days, Flock is a subscription service. Communities pay for the service, but Flock owns all the hardware and is responsible for installing and maintaining it, as well as removing it when requested. Technically, by the book, the police shouldn't be disabling or removing cameras, either. But we're not going to complain when they do, especially when Flock seems to be dragging its feet on the matter.

Other communities have resorted to covering cameras with trash bags while waiting for Flock to do its part. Even this strategy hasn't always been effective, though. The Verge also reports that Elgin Community College found the bags over its Flock cameras removed, presumably by Flock contractors sent out to "fix" the cameras they had covered while trying to cancel the service. This happened several times between December 2025 and April 2026. Only when the original contract expired did Flock arrange to remove the unwanted cameras.

Flock's only comment on this matter to KARE 11 was: "We are proud of the impact our technology had in Plymouth and Dayton. We respect the cities' decisions and are working with the customers to have the cameras removed." It gave no status of the cancellation requests or timeline for camera removal. Zach Doud, Dayton city administrator, told KARE 11 that Flock told him it would take 60 days to remove the equipment. It's hard to tell whether Flock is deliberately dragging this out, or simply overwhelmed by the large number of cancelation requests it's getting.

Either way, I wonder if whoever wrote Flock's cancellation policy was listening to "Hotel California" by the Eagles at the time. It seems like you can check out any time you like, but you can never leave.

#5813. Jeep Wrangler Scrambler SRT Concept Is Your Inner-Child's Dream Jeep With A Hemi V8, Reservable Jump Seats And Independent Front Suspension

Jeep has just done something with the Wrangler that has never been done before: given it independent front suspension, and somehow, that's not even the wildest part of its latest concept vehicle. The automaker just pulled the covers off the Wrangler Scrambler SRT Concept at the 2026 SEMA Show in Las Vegas, and right away you can tell this thing is no ordinary Jeep truck.

Right above that independent front suspension sits a naturally aspirated 6.4-liter HEMI V8 with 470 horsepower and 470 pound-feet of torque on tap. Out back, you'll find a Trophy Truck-style rear axle. The whole truck is finished in what Jeep calls Punk'n orange, and its bed is actually pretty functional while also serving as a nod to Jeep's own Scrambler history. It has reversible jump seats that let rear occupants face either forward or backward toward the rear of the truck — a layout built for desert chase trucks and spotters. Is it a gimmick? Yeah, but it's also sick as hell.

Equally sick as hell are the looks — especially up front where designers clearly took inspiration from the CJ-8 Scrambler of the early 1980s. However, it's a damn slight more aggressive than that car with its shark nose front end and massively flared fenders. The rear, while much shorter than your typical Jeep Gladiator, looks fairly similar. The body itself is far shorter as well, which isn't surprising when you consider it only has two doors. All in all, it doesn't look too dissimilar from the Jeep Sandstorm Concept we saw back in 2018, but they're certainly different vehicles.

Since the dawn of man, Wranglers have had solid front axles, but that changes for the Scrambler SRT. Developed with off-road racer Casey Currie, the new independent front suspension delivers 14 inches of travel up front and 15 inches out back. Jeep says the goal is better high-speed stability, more precise steering (impossible for it to be less precise) and better wheel control when blasting through the desert. This is a Jeep aimed at desert running, not the rock-crawling crowd that usually flocks to Wranglers.

As I mentioned earlier, the Trophy Truck-inspired rear suspension is its own feat of engineering. It's built to soak up high-speed impacts rather than slowly articulate over rocks. When paired with beefy 37-inch tires, it's clear that the Wrangler Scrambler SRT Concept is designed for off-road speed first and foremost. While I do love rockcrawling, there's nothing quite like prerunning to really get the heart pounding.

Of course, none of this is really, well, real. The Wrangler Scrambler SRT Concept is very much a concept, though I'll admit, I won't be too shocked if the Wrangler ends up getting independent front suspension at some point in the future. The Ford Bronco has it, and the Earth has yet to collapse in on itself despite that.

While this concept is still unmistakably a Jeep, Tim Kuniskis, head of American Brands, SRT Performance and Direct Connections at Stellantis North America, said the project was a dare to the brand's own playbook, which is probably where the use of an IFS comes in. Sure, it's going to tick some Jeep loyalists off, but some departures are good departures.

#5812. GTA VI Will Take Up To 212 GB On Your Console's Hard Drive

"Grand Theft Auto VI" is set to be the biggest video game of the decade, if not one of the biggest games ever made. That distinction isn't just figurative either because it's set to be absolutely huge in the literal, bits and bytes sense, too. Developer Rockstar Games announced Tuesday that the highly anticipated title will be available for digital pre-load starting November 10, and that the full game file will take up to 212 GB, "depending on your device," even telling gamers to "make sure you've got ample space to begin pre-loading as soon as possible."

To put that number into context, the standard PlayStation 5 comes with an advertised 1 TB or 825 GB of storage depending on whether you get the disc or digital version, and about 158 GB of that is taken up by the operating system. In other words, assuming the standard PS5 version of the game is indeed 212 GB and you've got a digital console without any external drives, just GTA VI will take up more than 25% of all your available space.

On the Xbox side, a Series X comes with either 1 TB or 2 TB while the entry-level Series S is offered in 1 TB or 512 GB drives, and of course, a good chunk of that is always reserved for the OS. Big thoughts and prayers for owners of the 512-gig Series S.

Given how much content this next Grand Theft Auto game is set to come with, a massive download isn't entirely surprising. The map is said to be double that of "GTA V," three times bigger than "Red Dead Redemption 2," and will apparently include "hundreds" of accessible building interiors. There are also hundreds of cars to account for, super detailed character models, and, y'know, the underlying logic and systems that make it all a playable game.

All that said, it's entirely possible that the quoted 212 GB size is only for the higher-fidelity PS5 Pro version of the game, and that console comes with 2 TB out of the box. However, the fact that Rockstar is quoting that number only as a blanket sort of thing leads us to believe that the standard version most people will download is indeed just as big, or not meaningfully far off.

Grand Theft Auto VI will release (or, for those who pre-load, become playable) on November 19 on PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X and S consoles. For the real keeners, pre-load starts on November 10 — midnight local time on PS5, midnight GMT for Xbox.

#5811. Here's How Many Patients Fudge the Facts to Get Antibiotic Scripts

— Caregivers also reported misrepresenting kids' symptoms for antibiotics

More than one in four adults said they misrepresented symptoms to try to get antibiotics for themselves or their children, survey results showed.

Among 959 adults, 27.9% said they had misrepresented symptoms to their clinicians to get antibiotics for themselves, with 26% reporting that they had exaggerated their existing symptoms and 16.7% reporting that they had made up symptoms that didn't exist.

Notably, 76.9% of those who misrepresented symptoms at least once said they succeeded in getting antibiotics, noted Alistair Thorpe, PhD, of the University of Utah in Salt Lake City, and colleagues in JAMA Network Open.

Among 236 caregiver respondents, 26.7% reported that they had misrepresented a child's symptoms to get clinicians to give them an antibiotic: 24.2% said they had exaggerated a child's symptoms, 23.6% said they had fabricated symptoms, and 19.1% reported doing both. Of note, 87.3% said they'd successfully received antibiotics, reported Thorpe and colleagues in a separate research letter in JAMA Network Open.

"Together, these findings suggest concerning patient-clinician dynamics characterized by patients not feeling taken seriously, unclear and inconsistent messaging about antibiotics and antimicrobial resistance, conspiracy-like beliefs about antimicrobial resistance, and symptom misrepresentation to obtain antibiotics," Thorpe and team wrote.

These results "signal a significant issue for health professionals making antibiotic prescribing decisions and for the fields of antimicrobial stewardship and public health," they concluded.

Blame for unnecessary antibiotic prescriptions extends beyond dishonest patients, cautioned Jeffrey Linder, MD, MPH, of Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine in Chicago, in an accompanying commentary.

"It does not track that approximately 80% of respondents who reported misrepresenting their symptoms obtained a prescription unless prescribers were being willfully deceived or, more likely, just getting worn down with what they perceive as patient demand, regardless of actual clinical appropriateness," Linder wrote.

For diagnoses for which antibiotics aren't appropriate, "prescribing antibiotics is medically incorrect and could itself be considered a form of dishonesty or lying mainly to assuage the patient," he noted. Beyond the potential danger to patients and the rise of antimicrobial resistance, "the more concerning negative externality of inappropriate antibiotic prescribing is, like lying, an erosion of trust."

Prescribing antibiotics that clinicians know "are more likely to hurt than help ... is an affront to clinicians' professional responsibility to our patients," Linder concluded. "Clinicians' obligation is to act in patients' best interest, even if patients are hoping we do something different, even if patients do not like it, or even if patients lie about their symptoms."

Conducted in early 2026, the online survey asked U.S. adults if they had ever exaggerated or fabricated symptoms to obtain antibiotics. Respondents were also surveyed about their reasons for their decisions.

Among adults who misrepresented symptoms for themselves, cold-like symptoms were most often exaggerated or faked (65.7% of respondents), followed by stomach symptoms (49.3%), joint or muscle pain (46.3%), and nothing specific (36.9%). Of the eight symptom categories, only one -- urinary symptoms -- "clearly justified antibiotic prescribing," Linder noted.

Among adults, reasons for misrepresenting their own symptoms included:

Factors associated with higher odds of reporting symptom misrepresentation to obtain antibiotics included often feeling that clinicians do not take their symptoms seriously (OR 2.19, 95% CI 1.49-3.22), believing that antibiotic resistance is a hoax (OR 1.98, 95% CI 1.34-2.93), reporting more comorbidities (OR 1.16, 95% CI 1.05-1.28), having higher lying acceptability scores (OR 1.49, 95% CI 1.23-1.80), and needing help reading health materials (OR 2.40, 95% CI 1.62-3.54). In contrast, dislike of taking antibiotics (OR 0.89, 95% CI 0.81-0.99) and higher antibiotic knowledge (OR 0.73, 95% CI 0.58-0.91) were associated with lower odds of misrepresentation.

Few patients had regrets about bending the truth to get antibiotics. Among adults, fewer than 30% said they regretted their action, and those who did were more likely to do so because of adverse effects or ineffectiveness than concerns about dishonesty.

Among caregivers who misrepresented a child's symptoms, 63.5% who reported that they lied about symptoms said they did so because they felt clinicians didn't take their child's symptoms seriously. That group was significantly more likely to misrepresent symptoms (adjusted OR 3.34, 95% CI 1.44-7.99), as were caregivers whose child had comorbidities (aOR 1.24, 95% CI 1.02-1.54), those with higher lying acceptability scores (aOR 2.06, 95% CI 1.33-3.31), and those with lower health literacy (aOR 2.50, 95% CI 1.06-6.05).

Caregivers with higher antibiotic knowledge scores had lower odds of reporting symptom misrepresentation (aOR 0.55, 95% CI 0.31-0.93).

Study limitations included the small number of survey respondents, who may not represent the U.S. population. Inaccurate reporting also may have led to underreporting.

"Everyone said it was impossible to get back in one year. He stated last weekend that he will be available to race elsewhere regardless of manufacturer. Keselowski won Dodge's most recent Cup Series title with Penske in 2012. #5808. AI, Digital Pathology in Early Breast Cancer Among Highlights at SABCS

— Virginia Kaklamani also previews studies on antibody-drug conjugates, endocrine therapy, and more

The use of artificial intelligence (AI) and digital pathology in early breast cancer will be a major theme at the upcoming San Antonio Breast Cancer Symposium (SABCS), with new research exploring whether image-based tools can help determine which patients need chemotherapy or extended endocrine therapy.

In this exclusive MedPage Today video, Virginia Kaklamani, MD, DSc, of the University of Texas at San Antonio Health Science Center, who is co-director of SABCS, previews some of the early breast cancer research to watch at this year's meeting, including studies on novel antibody-drug conjugates and endocrine therapy, genomic studies, and a debate on the use of trastuzumab deruxtecan (T-DXd; Enhertu) in the neoadjuvant versus adjuvant setting for HER2-positive disease.

Following is a transcript of her remarks:

Hello, my name is Virginia Kaklamani from UT Health San Antonio, and I'm one of the co-directors of the San Antonio Breast Cancer Symposium.

So for this year's symposium, our 49th year, we're going to have a lot of really interesting abstracts, especially in the early-stage setting, looking at AI. So looking at digital pathology and how this digital pathology can actually help us determine who needs chemotherapy and who doesn't need chemotherapy, who needs extended endocrine therapy, and so forth. This is really a new era where we have a lot of tools available so that instead of taking a specimen, sending it to a laboratory thousands of miles away, and analyzing it, now with digital pathology, all we need to do is just take a picture and send that picture over and then that picture gets analyzed, and really the information that we get may actually be as good, if not better, than the information we get from doing genomic assays on specific tumor specimens.

So this is going to help, I think, in the future because it'll allow us to do these tests quicker and also cheaper. And we can do them from anywhere in the world, whereas right now these laboratories sometimes in certain countries have specific regulations.

So I think this is kind of the biggest news out of SABCS this year, but there's so many other things. We have new novel antibody-drug conjugates. We're going to be looking at endocrine therapy in the early-stage setting and so forth, and a lot of other genomic presentations that'll really help us treat our patients better.

This year we expect more than 11,000 people to come to San Antonio. We always have many medical oncologists, surgical oncologists, radiation oncologists, advocates. I think the strength of SABCS is the presence of our patient advocates and the inclusion of our patient advocates through the program and the different sessions that we have.

We have several special sessions that are looking at lobular carcinoma, looking at obesity and breast cancer, GLP-1 agonists, really the talk of the past -- what, 5, 10 years -- and how GLP-1s can help improve health in our patients with breast cancer, potentially also improve outcomes in our breast cancer patients.

So a lot of things happening in the symposium, from educational sessions, state-of-the-art sessions, debate. The big debate this year is going to be whether to give T-DXd in the neoadjuvant or adjuvant setting in patients with early-stage HER2-positive disease. So the 5 days that the meeting is going on is really going to be crazy, but it's going to be a lot of fun data to discuss.

#5807. Ford Settles Transit Trail Class Action Lawsuit By Paying Owners Less Than The Package Cost

The current trend of automakers "off-roadifying" their road cars has created a new breed of hiked up, black plastic-clad soft roaders that buyers can't seem to get enough of. Ford gave its Transit full-size van the treatment back in 2022 with the all-wheel-drive Transit Trail, but recalled all of them in early 2024 and replaced their 30.5-inch all-terrain tires that rubbed against the wheel wells with the same 28.5-inch tires on standard Transits, and owners were not pleased.

They were so displeased, in fact, that they filed a class action lawsuit that we first reported on last year. Ford finally reached a settlement with the plaintiffs last week, offering Transit Trail owners a $6,000 payout for the diminished off-road abilities of their vans. This is a pretty healthy payout from a class action lawsuit settlement, but it's still less than half of the original price of the Trail package that cost $12,500 when new.

According to classaction.org, "Eligible for the settlement are all individuals who, as the original owner or lessee, bought or leased a model year 2023 or 2024 Ford Transit Trail before June 1, 2024 for personal, family or household use and who have not already received any compensation from Ford, including any settlement payment or repurchase, related to a covered vehicle or to any alleged defect of, or economic loss relating to, the vehicle." More fine print about how to prove membership in the settlement class is available on the Class Action site.

The Ford Transit Trail was marketed by Ford as having an "adventure-ready chassis is re-tuned and tested to meet the same BUILT FORD TOUGH durability standards as the rest of the Transit lineup," complete with 6.7-inches of ground clearance, upgraded underbody protection, and the 30.5-inch Goodyear Wrangler Workhorse tires that led to the recall. The 28.5-inch tires that Ford replaced the Wranglers with reduced the ground clearance of Transit Trails down to 5.5-inches, or the same as the standard Transit. Nearly 2,000 vehicles were included in the recall, but it's not immediately clear how many owners are eligible for this class action lawsuit settlement.

#5805. Your blood could reveal inflammatory bowel disease 10 years before diagnosis

Scientists have identified hidden changes in the immune system that can appear up to 10 years before a person is diagnosed with inflammatory bowel disease (IBD). The findings, presented at UEG Week 2026, suggest that certain antibody patterns in the blood could eventually help doctors identify people at risk of developing the condition and even predict how severe their disease might become.

Inflammatory bowel disease refers to a group of long-term disorders, including Crohn's disease (CD) and ulcerative colitis (UC), that cause persistent inflammation in the digestive tract. These conditions develop when the immune system responds abnormally, contributing to inflammation and damage in the intestines.

Scientists increasingly believe that IBD begins developing years before it is officially diagnosed. However, the immune system changes that take place during this early, largely hidden period have remained poorly understood.

Tracking Immune Changes a Decade Before Diagnosis

To investigate what happens before IBD is diagnosed, researchers examined 2,000 blood serum samples collected from 500 people. The participants included 200 who eventually developed Crohn's disease, 200 who developed ulcerative colitis, and 100 healthy individuals of comparable age and sex.

Blood samples were available from the time of diagnosis and from approximately two, four, and 10 years earlier. This allowed the researchers to look backward through a decade of immune activity and identify changes that emerged before the diseases were recognized.

The team analyzed antibody reactions to approximately 357,000 antigens associated with bacteria, viruses, food, and the immune system itself. Antigens are substances that the immune system can recognize and respond to, while antibodies are proteins produced by the immune system that help identify specific targets.

Across all 2,000 samples, the researchers detected 123,980 unique antibodies.

A striking pattern emerged. People who later developed Crohn's disease or ulcerative colitis had significantly less antibody diversity than healthy participants, meaning their immune systems showed a narrower range of antibody responses. Distinctive changes could be detected in both diseases as far back as 10 years before diagnosis.

Different Blood Signals Reveal Crohn's Disease and Ulcerative Colitis

The researchers also found that Crohn's disease and ulcerative colitis were associated with different immune signatures.

Among people who later developed Crohn's disease, antibody responses to herpesviruses and bacterial flagellin were elevated. Flagellin is a protein that forms part of the tail-like structures some bacteria use to move.

These elevated responses were especially noticeable in individuals who eventually developed complicated Crohn's disease or disease affecting the ileum, the final section of the small intestine.

At the same time, antibody responses to encapsulated bacteria gradually decreased as diagnosis approached. These bacteria are surrounded by protective outer coatings that can help them evade immune defenses.

People who later developed ulcerative colitis showed a somewhat different pattern. Their antibody responses to herpesviruses and autoantigens increased, while responses to encapsulated pathogens declined. Autoantigens are components of the body's own tissues that can mistakenly become targets of an immune response.

Together, these findings suggest that the immune system undergoes measurable changes many years before inflammatory bowel disease is diagnosed.

AI Identifies Hidden Disease Risk Years in Advance

The researchers then used machine learning, a form of artificial intelligence that identifies patterns in complex data, to determine whether these antibody signatures could distinguish people who would eventually develop IBD from healthy individuals.

Using blood antibody patterns from up to 10 years before diagnosis, the models distinguished future Crohn's disease cases from healthy controls with an area under the curve (AUC) of 0.90. For ulcerative colitis, the corresponding value was 0.84.

AUC is a measure of how well a model distinguishes between two groups. A score of 0.5 indicates performance equivalent to chance, while 1.0 represents perfect discrimination.

The researchers also discovered that antibody patterns could help distinguish people who would develop complicated Crohn's disease from those whose disease would remain less complicated. This distinction was possible approximately 10 years before diagnosis, with an AUC of 0.85.

These findings raise the possibility that blood antibody testing could eventually help identify not only who is at increased risk of IBD, but also which patients may need more intensive treatment.

Commenting on the findings, lead author Dr. Arno Bourgonje said: "It is fascinating to see that complicated CD at diagnosis is associated with a distinct antibody profile years before the first clinical symptoms and is not simply the consequence of disease progression after diagnosis. Finding these patterns so early points to the potential of antibody profiling to support earlier risk detection, distinguish between disease subtypes and help identify patients who may benefit from more intensive therapy as early as possible."

Could a Blood Test Detect Gut Disease 10 Years Early?

Although the results offer compelling evidence that IBD has a detectable early immune signature, the researchers emphasize that the findings are not yet ready for use in routine medical screening.

The study establishes an important proof of principle, but additional research is necessary to determine whether these antibody patterns can reliably predict disease in broader populations.

Researchers must also establish whether antibody testing provides meaningful benefits beyond existing genetic information, clinical assessments, and other blood-based measurements. Any potential screening test would need to be accurate, affordable, reproducible, and ultimately shown to improve patient outcomes.

"The next priority is to validate these signatures in independent, more diverse and prospectively followed populations," concluded Dr. Bourgonje. "We also need to determine their added value alongside genetic, clinical and other blood-based markers, translate the findings into a reproducible and affordable targeted assay and, ultimately, demonstrate that using such a test actually leads to better clinical outcomes."

If future studies confirm these results, blood-based immune profiling could offer doctors a new way to recognize inflammatory bowel disease risk years before diagnosis, distinguish between disease subtypes, and identify patients who might benefit from earlier intervention.

#5804. Another Surgical Treatment for Back Pain Fails in Rigorous Analysis

— Norwegian registry data show no benefit from spine fusion as part of decompression surgery

Spinal fusion added to decompression surgery in patients with spinal stenosis did not provide any added benefit versus decompression alone, Norwegian national registry data indicated.

With outcomes compared for 1,446 patients undergoing decompression alone and 806 having spinal fusion as well, no significant difference in Oswestry Disability Index (ODI) scores was seen a year later, according to James H. McAuley, PhD, of Neuroscience Research Australia in Randwick, and colleagues.

This is not the first study to question the utility of spinal fusion for patients with degenerative stenosis, the group noted in their report appearing in Annals of Internal Medicine. Earlier, prospective trials in Sweden and Norway found no improvement in disability when compared with decompression surgery alone, as did a smaller U.S.-based study (although the latter did show better quality of life with fusion). Moreover, these trials all showed a higher burden of complications such as bleeding with spinal fusion, along with prolonged operative time and increased costs.

Nevertheless, McAuley and colleagues observed, including fusion in decompression procedures is now common practice in some countries, particularly the U.S. and Australia. One analysis in 2023 found that fully 90% of U.S. surgeries for degenerative spondylolisthesis in 2019 involved spine fusion -- a 23-point increase from just 3 years previously. McAuley's group had no explanation for this explosion in popularity, while the authors of the 2023 analysis pointed to "financial incentives, lack of guidelines and/or national regulations, and delayed evidence dissemination through medical education or professional societies."

Degenerative spondylolisthesis is a common feature of spinal stenosis; it involves a forward shift in a vertebra relative to those below it. Some surgeons have viewed this as indicative of instability that should be rectified via fusion, which seems plausible on its face but, as noted above, rigorous evidence for actual benefit is weak at best.

The current study sought to extend the findings from that Norwegian trial, called NORDSTEN-DS, by examining spinal stenosis patients' outcomes as recorded in the country's national health registry. They identified a total of some 3,900 patients undergoing surgery for degenerative spondylolisthesis from 2007 to 2021. NORDSTEN-DS participants' data were included in the registry and analyzed anew in the current study, which used a "target trial emulation" approach to compare patients undergoing versus not having fusion as part of decompression surgery. After excluding patients who, for example, were very elderly or had previous surgeries in the same spinal region, the investigators had 2,252 patients for detailed analysis, including 236 who were in NORDSTEN-DS.

Mean patient age was about 65 and roughly 70% were women. ODI scores at the time of surgery averaged 38.9 for those having decompression alone and 41.4 for the group undergoing fusion (higher scores indicate worse symptoms, and the maximum is 50).

At month 12 after surgery, mean ODI scores declined to 20.0 for the fusion group and 22.3 among those undergoing decompression alone. The mean difference of 2.2 points fell short of statistical significance (95% CI -1.6 to 6.1).

McAuley and colleagues also performed some subgroup analyses to see whether patients who would be more likely to benefit from fusion could be identified from baseline characteristics, such as age, sex, body mass index, or symptom severity. This effort came up empty, as no significant differences in ODI improvement emerged. However, it did show that men with obesity (BMI ≥30) had significantly worse outcomes versus men with lower BMI values.

And, as with earlier studies, fusion led to longer operative time (mean 85 extra minutes) and longer hospital stays (mean 1.9 extra days) compared with decompression alone. Although no particular complication stood out as more common with fusion, the risk for any type of complication was doubled overall. Risk for needing additional surgery because of complications was increased even more.

"This study calls into question the highly prevalent management of pain from lumbar degenerative spondylolisthesis with fusion," McAuley and colleagues wrote. "Our findings add to current evidence and should prompt reevaluation of current guidelines," which, they noted, are sparse and "offer conflicting recommendations."

Limitations to the study included the reliance on administrative records, the potential for confounding by unmeasured variables, and the 12-month window for examining outcomes, leaving open the possibility that fusion could have longer-term benefits.

#5803. Mid-Air Helicopter Collision Near Everest Base Camp Kills Tourist, Injures 2 Others

Although an entire tourism industry has grown around climbing Mount Everest, summiting the world's tallest mountain remains a tremendously dangerous endeavor. Two helicopters collided in mid-air while attempting to land at the same spot below the Everest base camp. Helicopter company Fishtail Air stated that the crash killed an Indian tourist, and the chopper's captain and another passenger were injured. The other helicopter's pilot somehow escaped without injury. The second chopper was only carrying cargo.

The footage is harrowing, to say the least. A red helicopter is hovering, gradually descending and rotating to a landing spot at Pheriche. The Nepali village sits 13,780 feet above sea level. Then, a second helicopter comes into frame, seemingly appearing out of nowhere. Both pilots take evasive action far too late. The second chopper's main blades smash into the other rotorcraft, slicing off the tail boom. Both helicopters immediately plummet right into the snowy terrain as people waiting on the ground scramble away. Miraculously, none of the bystanders were struck by flying debris or injured. There's never a good outcome when two helicopters hit each other.

It's pretty baffling how two helicopters could collide in completely clear conditions. Maybe the pilots didn't realize they were trying to land in the same spot. Helicopter flights aren't rare in this part of the world, either. According to the BBC, helicopters are the only way to get trekkers and guides down from Everest when they are sick or exhausted in inhospitable conditions. It's also the tail end of the mountain's autumn climbing season.

The tourists who spend thousands of dollars to climb Everest do so with the understanding that it's inherently dangerous. Those dangers are typically low oxygen levels and freezing temperatures at absurdly high altitudes. Just getting to the mountain can be just as dangerous. Most journeys to Everest base camp begin at Tenzing-Hillary Airport, named after the first mountaineers to climb the mountain. The facility is often called the world's most dangerous airport because of its short 1,729-foot runway with a 12% gradient and the mountain wall at one end. Not to mention, the terrain doesn't allow pilots to go around despite the rapidly shifting weather conditions. Just flying in would be enough of an Everest adventure for me.

#5802. Could lowering high blood pressure be as simple as stimulating your ear?

A small device that delivers gentle electrical pulses through the ear could offer a new approach to treating stubborn high blood pressure. In a clinical trial led by researchers at Queen Mary University of London, the technology proved safe, well tolerated, and practical for patients to use at home.

Although the treatment did not significantly lower blood pressure compared with a placebo device across all participants, researchers observed encouraging results in patients with more severe hypertension. The findings suggest that stimulating nerves in the ear could eventually help certain people whose blood pressure remains elevated despite taking medication.

The results come from the SCRATCH-HTN trial, published in Hypertension, a journal of the American Heart Association. Researchers emphasize that larger studies are needed to determine whether the approach can provide meaningful benefits and which patients might respond best.

How Ear Nerve Stimulation Could Lower Blood Pressure

The experimental treatment uses a technique called transcutaneous autonomic neuromodulation (tAN), which aims to influence the autonomic nervous system. This network of nerves automatically controls essential bodily functions, including heart rate, digestion, and blood pressure.

By delivering mild electrical signals to nerves in the ear, researchers hope to influence the body's natural mechanisms for regulating blood pressure without requiring an invasive procedure.

The handheld device, developed by Afferent Medical Solutions, uses two small clips positioned on the tragus, the small flap of cartilage located just in front of the ear canal. These clips deliver gentle electrical stimulation, allowing patients to administer the treatment themselves without visiting a clinic.

Clinical Trial Tests the Device in 63 Patients

The clinical trial, funded by the National Institute for Health and Care Research (NIHR), enrolled 63 people with uncontrolled hypertension. All participants were already taking at least one medication to lower their blood pressure, but their readings remained above recommended levels.

Researchers randomly divided the participants into two groups. One received active nerve stimulation, while the other used a sham device designed to serve as a comparison. This approach helped investigators assess whether the electrical stimulation produced benefits beyond those associated with using the device itself.

Participants used their assigned devices for 30 minutes daily during the first two weeks. For the following 10 weeks, they reduced their sessions to once per week.

The results showed that the treatment was safe and generally comfortable for participants. Most also followed the prescribed schedule, suggesting that the technology could be practical for regular use at home.

However, when researchers compared blood pressure changes across the entire study population, they found no statistically significant difference between patients receiving active stimulation and those using the sham device.

Promising Results in Patients With Severe Hypertension

Despite the lack of a significant overall benefit, the researchers identified a potentially important finding among participants who entered the trial with more severe hypertension.

A prespecified subgroup analysis, meaning the researchers had planned this comparison before examining the results, revealed a greater reduction in blood pressure among these patients when they received active nerve stimulation rather than the sham treatment.

This finding raises the possibility that the technique may work better for people with particularly difficult-to-control blood pressure. However, the trial was relatively small, and the subgroup results require confirmation in larger studies before any conclusions can be drawn about its effectiveness.

Dr. Ajay Gupta, Chief Investigator, Clinical Reader at Queen Mary University of London and Consultant Physician at Barts Health NHS Trust, said:

"This is an exciting, highly patient-acceptable technology that could ultimately provide a cheap, scalable solution for millions. Although the trial did not achieve significance across the broad unselected group, those with more severe hypertension exhibited a clear blood pressure reduction in the active group compared to sham.

"As the first and most thorough study of its kind, this points towards important new research directions that could help us understand whether non-invasive neuromodulation can benefit particular groups of patients with uncontrolled hypertension."

A Potential New Option for Hard-to-Control Blood Pressure

High blood pressure, also known as hypertension, is a major contributor to cardiovascular disease and increases the risk of heart attacks and strokes. It places additional strain on blood vessels and the heart, potentially causing serious damage over time.

Although medications help many patients manage their condition, a substantial number continue to have blood pressure readings above recommended targets even while receiving treatment.

This ongoing challenge has encouraged scientists to explore additional approaches that could complement existing therapies.

Ear nerve stimulation is one such possibility. Because the device is small, does not require surgery, and can be used at home, it could offer a convenient option if future research establishes that it reliably lowers blood pressure in certain patients.

For now, the findings represent an early step rather than proof of an effective new treatment. The researchers say larger clinical trials will be essential to confirm the encouraging results in patients with severe hypertension and identify which groups, if any, could benefit most from this approach.

#5801. Parents' Autoimmune Disease Linked to Kids' Type 1 Diabetes Risk

— Large Swedish study highlighted shared familial susceptibility

Parental autoimmune diseases were associated with an increased risk of type 1 diabetes in offspring, according to a Swedish registry cohort study.

In an analysis of nearly 1.5 million individuals, children born to mothers with autoimmune disease during pregnancy had a 57% higher risk of developing type 1 diabetes (adjusted HR 1.57, 95% CI 1.46-1.68), according to Xingbo Mo, PhD, of Karolinska Institutet in Stockholm, and colleagues. Paternal autoimmune disease before childbirth had a comparable association (adjusted HR 1.51, 95% CI 1.37-1.67).

When parents had type 1 diabetes, the magnitude of risk was higher. Maternal type 1 diabetes was linked with a more than fourfold risk increase (HR 4.51, 95% CI 3.72-5.48), whereas paternal type 1 diabetes was associated with an even higher hazard (HR 5.79, 95% CI 4.70-7.13), they reported in JAMA Pediatrics.

"A clear gradient in type 1 diabetes risk was observed according to parental type 1 diabetes status, with the highest risk among offspring with both parents affected," the authors wrote. "The largely comparable maternal and paternal associations suggest that shared familial susceptibility may play an important role in the intergenerational transmission of type 1 diabetes risk."

While maternal and paternal estimates showed no overall difference for composite autoimmune disease, researchers identified variations across specific disease categories. Paternal associations were stronger for endocrine conditions -- most notably autoimmune thyroiditis -- whereas maternal associations were stronger for ankylosing spondylitis and gastrointestinal autoimmune disorders.

"Stronger associations for endocrine autoimmune diseases, particularly autoimmune thyroiditis, are consistent with the established clustering of endocrine autoimmune disorders and their shared immune-genetic susceptibility," the researchers noted. Yet the "disease-specific heterogeneity suggests that selected conditions may also involve parent-specific or potential intrauterine influences," they added, which "support a multifactorial model of intergenerational autoimmune transmission."

In an accompanying editorial, Mikael Knip, MD, PhD, of Biomedicum Helsinki in Finland, emphasized that an "obvious" practical takeaway is that offspring of parents with autoimmune disease warrant monitoring for type 1 diabetes.

"Consensus guidelines have been published for screening and monitoring of individuals at risk for type 1 diabetes," Knip wrote, noting that while screening efforts "may be variable based on resources available," children with a familial history "represent a definite risk group for progression to clinical type 1 diabetes."

Benjamin Nwosu, MD, chief of endocrinology at Northwell Health Cohen Children's Medical Center in New York, who was not involved with the study, underscored the necessity of early detection.

"Currently, in our practice, we screen children with type 1 diabetes for autoimmune thyroid conditions and celiac disease, because it's been published that children with type 1 diabetes have higher risk for the development of celiac and autoimmune thyroid conditions," Nwosu said in a statement.

He pointed out that the authors "did not mention that 40 to 50% of the risk for type 1 diabetes is carried on what's called the major histocompatibility complex class 2 genes or loci."

"Once you know that, you know a parent who has type 1 diabetes has some possibility of passing this condition to the offspring, not in a Mendelian autosomal dominant fashion, because type 1 is caused by what's called a polygenic aggregation of genes. But the risk is higher than in unaffected parents," he said.

The researchers evaluated 1,455,645 children born in Sweden between 2001 and 2014, with follow-up extending through 2023 (median age was 15.3 years, and 51.4% were male). Overall, 6.1% of children were exposed to maternal autoimmune diseases before delivery.

After adjusting for unmeasured shared familial factors using cousin-comparison models, the maternal association remained statistically significant (adjusted HR 1.27, 95% CI 1.08-1.50), with higher risks specifically noted for maternal endocrine autoimmune disorders and maternal type 1 diabetes.

The study team noted several limitations, including the lack of register data regarding clinical disease severity, autoantibody levels, disease activity during pregnancy, or medication use. "Such factors may modify fetal immune programming and could not be evaluated in the present study," they acknowledged.

Nwosu also cautioned that the results might not fully translate globally, noting Sweden "is located in the northern latitudes, where type 1 diabetes is almost endemic."

#5800. Oil Companies Will Love That The 2027 Chevrolet Silverado's New V8s Get Worse Gas Mileage Than The Old Ones

Due to a litany of misguided decisions on behalf of the Trump administration, gas is ridiculously expensive right now, corporate fuel economy requirements are worryingly low, and we're in an affordability crisis. In totally unrelated news, the oil and gas companies that spent millions to elect Trump are raking in record profits that are more than double what they were at this time last year. Those oil companies are doing fine, but they will still likely be stoked to learn that GM announced the fuel economy numbers for V8-powered examples of the freshly redesigned 2027 Chevrolet Silverado today, and they all get worse gas mileage than the outgoing Silverado's V8s. These numbers should all also apply to the 2027 GMC Sierra, too.

According to documents posted by GM Authority, the fuel economy of all Silverados equipped with either the new 5.7-liter V8 or the new 6.6-liter V8 has dropped between 1 and 2 mpg across city ratings, highway ratings, and combined ratings. While these drops aren't especially significant, it's rare to see redesigned versions of cars debut with worse efficiency than their predecessors, even when we're talking about big trucks.

2027 Silverados fitted with the 5.7-liter V8 are rated at 16 mpg city, 19 mpg highway, and 17 combined in two-wheel-drive form; opting for four-wheel drive drops both the highway and combined ratings by an additional mpg. Those city and combined figures are each down one mpg, and the highway rating is down two mpg compared to the 2026 Silverado.

Trucks with the 6.6-liter V8 are only shown in four-wheel-drive, and are rated at 14 mpg in the city, 18 mpg on the highway, and 15 mpg combined. If you go for the off-road-oriented Silverado ZR2, it drops the city and combined figures by 1 mpg and the highway figure by 3 mpg. (The same should go for the Sierra's AT4X equivalent.) All of those numbers are down by either 1 or 2 mpg when compared to their fuel economy ratings in 2026.

Even opting for the 3.0-liter inline-six turbodiesel powertrain option won't save as much fuel as it did last year. Most diesel-powered 2027 Silverados return between 1 and 2 mpg worse than last year, except for city mileage in some four-wheel-drive trims. City and highway mileage for the diesel ZR2 remains the same. Mileage ratings for the turbocharged 2.7-liter four-cylinder powertrain have not been published yet.

Considering that the Chevrolet Silverado almost always lands in the second-place spot on the list of best-selling vehicles in the U.S. behind the Ford F-Series, and the GMC Sierra isn't too far behind, the impact of small changes in efficiency get magnified. So far through the third quarter of 2026, GMC sold 252,639 Sierras and Chevrolet sold 413,383 Silverados, and production of the new-gen trucks only just started in October. Though not all of these trucks will be fitted with one of the new V8s, certainly a ton of them will, and decreasing even just V8 gas mileage by a single mpg will still result in a massive increase in collective fuel usage.

#5798. CTE in NFL Players: What a New Study Tells Us -- And What It Doesn't

— Does the new research mean 3 in 4 football players are at risk?

A statistical model that extrapolated data from deceased National Football League (NFL) brain donors to include all former NFL players who died from 2008 through 2023 estimated that nearly three in four had chronic traumatic encephalopathy (CTE).

Yorghos Tripodis, PhD, of the Boston University School of Public Health, and colleagues developed the model by quantifying cognitive symptoms of NFL brain donors who had CTE assessments. CTE can be identified only through autopsy.

After adjusting for bias associated with brain donation, the model estimated the prevalence of CTE at death was 74.5% (95% CI 69.6-78.3) for all former NFL players who died from 2008 through 2023, Tripodis and colleagues reported in JAMA Network Open.

The analysis incorporated a selection ratio to estimate the prevalence of CTE. The ratio assumed that brain donation was driven primarily by family members observing cognitive symptoms in former NFL players.

"This is an interesting attempt to statistically account for the problem of selective donation into a brain bank so that they can estimate the prevalence of CTE among all NFL players who died during the time interval they were looking at when they only could analyze a subset of them," observed Marc Weisskopf, PhD, ScD, of the Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health in Boston, who wasn't involved with the study.

The numbers don't necessarily apply to all NFL players, he pointed out. "The deaths they are working with are about 10% of all that could have occurred in that period, and there are reasons to suspect CTE would be less common among the still-living players when they eventually die," Weisskopf told MedPage Today.

"The statistical approach is sound, although it's important to realize that it is very dependent on some assumptions -- notably that brain donation is entirely driven by cognitive symptoms and that those are captured by dementia on the death certificate and their adjustment for additional mild cognitive impairment," he said.

CTE has four distinct stages, with higher CTE stages correlating with more severe cognitive symptoms. "Even if we were able to determine an unbiased estimate of the proportion of living and deceased players with CTE, we still don't have information on their probable CTE stage, which has the most meaning for their everyday lives and health," noted Rachel Grashow, PhD, MS, also of Harvard, who also wasn't involved with the study.

The study findings do not apply to other groups, observed Julie Stamm, PhD, of the University of Wisconsin-Madison, who wasn't part of the study. "It does not provide an estimate of CTE among athletes in other sports or at lower levels of play," Stamm told MedPage Today.

"Greater lifetime repetitive head impact exposure has been linked to an increased risk of developing CTE, so it is likely that the prevalence would be higher among NFL players than those who played at lower levels," she added. "However, we cannot estimate the prevalence among players at lower levels at this time."

Tripodis and colleagues used UNITE brain bank data and national death index records to evaluate former NFL players who died from 2008 through 2023. The mean duration of play was 5.1 years and 41.1% of deceased players were ages 80 or older when they died.

Of 2,362 deceased former NFL players, 396 were brain donors and 1,966 were nondonors. Of 387 donors, 362 (93.5%) had CTE at autopsy. The estimated prevalence of all-cause dementia was 27.2% (95% CI 22.6-31.6).

The median selection ratio was 4.97 (95% CI 4.72-5.26), meaning that former players with cognitive symptoms were five times more likely to have their brains donated by their family than those without these symptoms.

"The findings of this cross-sectional study suggest that selection bias materially inflates donor-based prevalence estimates but is unlikely to fully explain the high burden of CTE neuropathology among this population," Tripodis and co-authors stated.

Studies like this can refocus attention to the "20,000 living former NFL players, some of whom are suffering with cognitive symptoms or have been diagnosed with dementia," Grashow told MedPage Today. The Football Players Health Study at Harvard has more than 5,000 former players in its cohort, she noted.

"In our conversations with and ongoing study of these NFL players, we've found that head injury increases risk for treatable conditions that -- if left untreated -- increases risk for cognitive issues and dementia. These include sleep apnea, high blood pressure, low testosterone, depression, anxiety, tinnitus, and more," she added.

"So, if we are thinking about how these recent findings relate to the many thousands of former living players, the story shifts to one of surveilling and treating former players for treatable conditions so we can reduce their dementia and heart disease risk," Grashow said. "This, we believe, is one of the best ways we can tackle the potential symptoms, risks, and challenges of CTE right now -- before we turn to estimating prevalence, which is at the end of life."
