#5797. Scientists discover how pancreatic cancer rewrites its DNA to escape treatment

Scientists have uncovered new details about how pancreatic cancer constantly changes its genetic makeup, allowing it to become more aggressive, spread throughout the body, and resist treatment. By examining nearly half a million individual cancer cells, researchers identified genetic changes that could reveal new ways to fight one of the deadliest forms of cancer.

The study, conducted by scientists at NYU Langone Health and its Perlmutter Cancer Center, focused on pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma, a particularly difficult cancer to treat. Nearly 85 percent of patients die within five years of being diagnosed.

Although researchers have already linked more than half a dozen genes to the disease, much remains unknown about how pancreatic cancer develops and adapts. By examining tumor cells individually rather than studying them in large groups, the team gained a clearer understanding of how different populations of cancer cells change their DNA as the disease progresses.

How Pancreatic Cancer Evolves To Escape Treatment

The researchers discovered that pancreatic tumors contain a variety of distinct cell populations known as clones. Each clone consists of cancer cells with similar genetic characteristics, but their behavior can differ dramatically.

Some clones develop changes that help them withstand cancer treatments, while others become better equipped to invade surrounding tissues and spread to other parts of the body. These findings suggest that the cancer's ability to continually reorganize its genetic material plays a central role in both treatment resistance and metastasis.

Previous studies generally divided pancreatic cancer cells into two main categories: classical (less aggressive) and basal (more aggressive). However, because those studies examined large collections of cells together, scientists could not fully explain how cancer cells move from one category to the other.

The latest findings help explain this process. Genetic changes occurring within individual tumors allow cancer cells to shift between these two states, potentially making the disease more dangerous as it develops.

Scientists Identify a Gene That Could Make Pancreatic Cancer Less Aggressive

Published on October 13 in Nature Genetics, the study identified several genetic changes associated with pancreatic cancer progression. One particularly important finding involved MYC, a gene that showed heightened activity in some of the most aggressive cancer cell populations.

In additional laboratory experiments, the researchers blocked MYC activity in aggressive basal pancreatic cancer cells. Remarkably, the cells shifted back toward the less aggressive classical state.

The change also made the cancer cells more exposed to immune cells, suggesting they could become easier for the immune system to recognize and attack. Although further research is needed, the results point to MYC as a promising target for future pancreatic cancer treatments.

"Our study provides the most granular view to date of pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma, revealing how ongoing genetic reshaping drives tumor evolution over the course of disease," said study lead investigator Despoina Kalfakakou, PhD, a postdoctoral research fellow at NYU Grossman School of Medicine and Perlmutter Cancer Center. "These findings suggest that targeting the mechanisms underlying tumor evolution, including MYC, may help restrain the cancer's rapid progression."

"Our research also suggests that single-cell clonal analysis offers a better approach to understanding complex cancers, like pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma, as it develops over time, by mapping how tumors evolve and adapt at each stage of disease progression," said study co-senior investigator Aristotelis Tsirigos, PhD, a professor in the Departments of Medicine and Pathology at NYU Grossman School of Medicine and a member of Perlmutter Cancer Center.

"More broadly, our study results highlight the critical role of clonal diversity, genetic plasticity, and interactions with the tumor's microenvironment in shaping human pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma biology," said Dr. Tsirigos, who is also co-director of the Division of Precision Medicine and director of NYU Langone's Applied Bioinformatics Laboratories.

Why Pancreatic Cancer Can Evade the Immune System

The investigation also provided important clues about pancreatic cancer's ability to hide from the immune system, a characteristic that may help explain why the disease often responds poorly to immunotherapy.

Researchers discovered that certain cancer cell clones showed increased activity of the TIGIT gene, which is associated with proteins that help tumors escape immune detection.

At the same time, these clones exhibited reduced activity of the PD1 gene. This combination may help explain why immunotherapies designed to target PD1 have shown limited effectiveness against pancreatic cancer.

Understanding these differences between cancer cell populations could help scientists develop treatments that interfere with the specific mechanisms tumors use to avoid immune attack.

According to Dr. Tsirigos, clinical trials investigating drugs that inhibit MYC or TIGIT are already underway for pancreatic ductal carcinoma and other cancers. He said the latest findings underscore the need for larger studies examining the different states cancer cells can adopt and how treatments might be tailored to target them.

Nearly Half a Million Cancer Cells Reveal Hidden Tumor Diversity

To investigate how pancreatic cancer changes over time, the scientists examined gene activity (using transcriptomics) in 443,451 individual cells collected from 62 tumors belonging to 56 men and women diagnosed with pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma.

Six of these patients also provided tumor samples both before and after chemotherapy, allowing researchers to examine changes associated with treatment.

Unlike traditional approaches that measure changes in gene activity across large groups of cells, the new analysis examined individual cells. This provided a much more detailed picture of the different cancer cell populations living within the same tumor.

The results showed that individual tumors contained approximately two to 11 distinct clones, consisting of varying combinations of classical and basal cancer cells.

By tracing the genetic origins of these clones and comparing patterns of gene activity, the researchers connected differences in DNA copy numbers with the types of cells present in each tumor.

This approach allowed them to identify which genes, including MYC and TIGIT, were particularly active at different points during cancer progression.

Together, the findings provide a more detailed understanding of how pancreatic tumors evolve, resist treatment, and spread. They also suggest that interfering with the genetic mechanisms driving these changes could offer new opportunities to combat a disease that remains exceptionally difficult to treat.

The research was supported by the Sky Foundation and National Institutes of Health grants P30CA016087 and R01CA245005.

In addition to Drs. Kalfakakou and Tsirigos, the NYU Langone research team included Daniel Cameron, MS; Emily Kawaler, PhD; Ziaohong Jing, PhD; Cristina H. Hajdu, MD; Dylan Tamayo, MS; Yoona Shim; Amanda Ackermann, MD, PhD; Daniel Weissinger; Rosmel Hernandez, PhD; Matthew Beier, MD; Dacia Dimartino, PhD; Peter Meyn; Kalina Rice, MS; Shanmugapriya Selvaraj, MS; Cynthia A. Loomis, MD, PhD; Adriana Heguy, PhD; Amanda W. Lund, PhD; and Igor Dolgalev, PhD.

Additional contributors from Oregon Health and Science University were co-investigators Motoyuki Tsuda, MD, PhD; Hayley Zimny; and Rosalie Sears, PhD.

Researchers from the University of California San Diego included Lidong Wang, PhD; Qi Su, PhD; Sungsik Kim, PhD; Hannah Carter, PhD; Theodore Welling, MD; and co-senior study investigator Diane Simeone, MD.

#5796. Washington State Resident Dies From Bird Flu Following Exposure to Infected Cattle

— Two other U.S. deaths have been tied to bird flu since 2024

A person in Washington state has died from complications of the bird flu, the first such U.S. death in a year.

The Yakima County resident, who had previous health conditions, was exposed to avian influenza at a dairy with infected cattle and had been hospitalized. The person died Sunday, county health officials said.

Two other U.S. deaths have been tied to bird flu since it became a national public health concern in 2024. The first, announced in January 2025, was an elderly person in Louisiana who had a version of bird flu known as H5N1. The other, in Washington state, died in November 2025 from a rarer version called H5N5.

Yakima County officials did not release any additional details about the person who recently died. The CDC said the person was infected with H5N1.

County officials have reported that 33 dairy herds recently have tested positive for H5N1.

They say three human infections have been linked to exposure at a dairy, and that the Yakima Health District is working with the state health department to find people who were in close contact with those people and monitor them for symptoms.

The H5N1 bird flu has been spreading widely among wild birds, poultry, cows, and other animals for several years. Health concerns escalated in early 2024 when infections spread among U.S. livestock. Its presence in the environment increases the chances that people will be exposed, and potentially catch it, officials have said.

More than 70 human infections have been reported in the U.S. since the beginning of 2024, according to the CDC. But there is no known person-to-person spread and the risk to the general public is low, the agency says.

The agency analyzed the genetic sequence of the virus from the person who died in Washington and found no changes that would make the virus spread more easily or cause more severe illness, a CDC spokesperson said Monday.

Officials continue to urge people who have contact with sick or dead birds to take precautions, including wearing respiratory and eye protection and gloves when handling poultry. People who work on farms are considered to be at higher risk.

#5794. Late-Stage Sickle Cell Disease Study Splits on Main Goals

— Mitapivat improved hemoglobin response, but no significant benefit for pain crises

The thalassemia drug mitapivat (Aqvesme) significantly improved hemoglobin response in patients with sickle cell disease but failed to top placebo when it came to reducing pain crises in a phase III trial that aims to support an expanded approval.

In the RISE UP trial, 41% of patients assigned to the oral pyruvate kinase activator had a hemoglobin response, as compared with 3% of placebo recipients (P<0.0001). But that didn't translate into a reduction in the annualized rate of sickle cell pain crises (2.6 for mitapivat vs 3.1 for placebo, P=0.1213), reported researchers led by Biree Andemariam, MD, of the New England Sickle Cell Institute at University of Connecticut Health in Farmington.

Beyond those two primary endpoints, treatment with mitapivat was associated with improved markers of hemolysis, reduced transfusion burden, and additional clinical benefits in the subset of patients with a hemoglobin response, but not with improvements in patient-reported fatigue, the study authors wrote in The Lancet.

These mitapivat data, which are currently under review with the FDA, arrive amid turbulence in the drug market for sickle cell disease. Crizanlizumab (Adakveo), approved in 2019, lost its marketing authorization in Europe after randomized trial data showed the drug failed to top placebo when it came to vaso-occlusive crises, though it's still available in the U.S.

And voxelotor (Oxbryta), which was granted accelerated approval, also in 2019, was pulled from global markets after trial data turned up an imbalance in deaths and acute vaso-occlusive crises for patients on the hemoglobin S polymerization inhibitor.

Mitapivat is currently approved as a disease-modifying treatment for anemia in adults with alpha- or beta-thalassemia, and an approval decision in sickle cell disease is expected by February of next year.

Labeling for mitapivat includes a boxed warning due to the risk of liver injury, which also warranted a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) program. The FDA has requested an analysis on whether a REMS would be needed in sickle cell disease, but drugmaker Agios Pharmaceuticals said the data so far have shown no cases of liver injury that follow the pattern or severity seen in the thalassemia trials.

The RISE UP trial was conducted across 69 sites in 15 countries and randomized 207 patients older than 16 years with anemia and recurrent sickle cell pain crises in a 2:1 ratio to twice-daily mitapivat (100 mg) or placebo.

At baseline, participants had a median age of 25 years, 57.5% were female, mean hemoglobin was about 8.5 g/dL, and they averaged 3.4 sickle cell pain crises per year.

The primary endpoint of hemoglobin response was defined as a 1 g/dL or greater increase in average hemoglobin concentration from week 24 to week 52 compared with the baseline.

In a commentary accompanying the study, Jennifer Knight-Madden, MBBS, PhD, and Zachary Ramsay, MBBS, both of the University of the West Indies in Kingston, Jamaica, noted that despite the mixed results on the primary endpoints, "there could be clinical benefit" among the mitapivat responders.

The findings should be interpreted with caution, they noted, but post-hoc analyses suggested the mitapivat-treated participants "who achieved a hemoglobin response experienced lower annualized rates of sickle cell pain crises, fewer related hospitalizations and emergency department visits, longer times to first and second sickle cell pain crises, and lower fatigue scores compared with non-responders."

"The findings provide evidence that a clinically meaningful benefit can exist within a subset of patients," they wrote. "As acknowledged by the authors, this study was not designed to identify patients likely to achieve a hemoglobin response, and differences in hemoglobin response could be due to genetic and epigenetic modifiers, red cell metabolic differences, and co-inherited red cell variants."

As for safety data, mitapivat was associated with lower rates of grade ≥3 adverse events (AEs), at 33% versus 41% with placebo, and lower rates of serious AEs (20% vs 29%), Andemariam and colleagues reported. One of the serious AEs in a mitapivat-treated patient was considered treatment-related, however.

Overall, AEs leading to treatment discontinuation occurred in 4% of patients in the mitapivat group and in 3% of the placebo group. Despite no signal of liver injury in the study, some hepatic-related AEs did lead to mitapivat discontinuation, including one patient with increases in aspartate aminotransferase (grade 3) and bilirubin (grade 2), another patient with hepatic enzyme increases (grade 4) deemed unrelated to treatment, a patient with hepatic cytolysis (grade 2) and other AEs, and one patient with transaminase increases (grade 2) considered secondary to an infection.

Deaths occurred in 2% of the mitapivat arm and 3% of the placebo arm, with none deemed treatment-related.

#5793. At $18,000, Could This 1997 Bentley Brooklands Let You See How The Other Half Lives?

Based on today's Nice Price or No Dice Bentley's price, you don't have to be a one-percent-er to live life large. Let's see if this stately and imposing saloon makes us want to live it up.

On their third album, "Learning to Crawl," Chrissie Hynde and The Pretenders warned us of "Time the Avenger." A song about bad decisions, regret, and the inability to turn back the clock, it fit the album's tone perfectly, following the untimely deaths of two band members.

The fact is, time keeps marching forward, and we can never slow its pace or fully reverse its effects. That makes looking at cars like the 2001 Audi A4 Quattro Avant we considered yesterday such a scary proposition. While the seller promised that many of the car's wear-and-tear items had been addressed, with any old Audi, there's always some new surprise to darken a day and lighten a wallet. That expectation of future failures on this 25-year-old Audi gave pause, calling into question its $7,450 asking price. In the end, that was just too much to risk, and we saw an overwhelming 73% 'No Dice' loss as a result.

Okay, if you all had a hard time justifying the potential future costs of an old mid-level Audi, let's see how you do with today's far fancier-pantsed 1997 Bentley Brooklands saloon. Bentley offered the Brooklands for a short five years, from 1993 to 1998, though the model was merely an evolution of the earlier Mulsanne, introduced all the way back in 1980. That makes the model a grand dame of British luxury comportment.

Following the marque's fancy of naming its models after race tracks or sections thereof, the Brooklands takes its name from the Brooklands circuit outside of Surrey, England. It was the world's first banked oval raceway, inspiring the Indianapolis Motor Speedway when it opened in 1907 and hosting its last race in 1939. In the years in between, Bentleys raced on the track, reinforcing the model's legacy. In another unique connection, the British aircraft manufacturer Vickers located a factory at the Brooklands Aerodrome, right next to the track, and later, when this Brooklands was built, that company was the majority owner of Rolls-Royce and Bentley.

With that bit of history out of the way, let's get to just what this car is. When it was built, Bentley and Rolls-Royce were kissing cousins (I know, eww, that phrase), with both marques sharing production in Crewe on the same platform. That means that, save for its rounded grille, some suspension tuning, and a whole herd of Flying B badges, this model is essentially the same as the contemporary Rolls-Royce Silver Spirit. That means it also shares the Rolls-Royce 6.75-liter OHV V8 engine.

When it debuted in 1993, the Brooklands was meant to be a less expensive (but still very expensive) model slotted under the Turbo R. As such, it was initially available only with a naturally aspirated version of that Rolls engine. That changed in 1996, when the Brooklands got a light-pressure turbo edition, producing 300 horsepower and an impressive 442 pound-feet of torque. That's a good thing, considering this luxury barge is fitted with every comfort and convenience feature the late '90s could offer, bringing the car's weight to over 5,300 pounds. That means reasonable performance and jaw-dropping thirst for premium fuel.

This Brooklands, which the ad claims to be painted in Black Emerald Mica over a parchment Connolly leather interior with spruce trim on the dash and doors, looks to be in fabulous condition. The paint, which appears dark green in the pictures, is accented by elegant but not overwhelming chrome trim and a handsome set of factory alloy wheels.

One notable feature of the Brooklands' cabin is the placement of the four-speed automatic shifter. It sits on the center console, a first for the model line, which traditionally used a column shifter.

The interior, swathed in two-tone leather and elegant wood, cossets passengers like they're in an English drawing room. The only thing missing is the sound of a fire crackling in a hearth. In fact, the ad says nothing is missing from this car, stating categorically that it "needs nothing." In addition to being mechanically and visually sound, the seller notes that the 67,402-mile car comes with a clean title, a passing smog test grade, and a Carfax report offering the kind of dull reading you want on any used car.

Of course, this is a Bentley. And it's a Bentley of a certain age (even older than yesterday's Audi), which means this Brooklands will likely need something in the future. That's to be feared, not eagerly awaited, because parts for these cars are incredibly expensive, and certain maintenance and repair work often requires special tools. Do you possess those? I certainly don't.

That's why anyone interested in a car like this needs to choose the best one they can find and afford. The thing is, these cars are getting very affordable to buy, even in good shape, for a variety of reasons. This one asks $18,000, which I think we can all admit seems like a lot of car for the money. But will it cost far more in the long run? Magic 8-Ball is inconclusive.

What's your take on this Bentley at an $18,000 asking price? Is that a safe bet to slip into the lap of luxury, at least until something major breaks and sullies the experience? Or is even that amount too much to fall into this Brooklands' honey trap?

#5792. Popular cannabis sleep gummies may have a hidden dark side

Cannabinol (CBN) has become a popular ingredient in sleep supplements, often promoted as a natural, gentle way to relax and get a better night's rest. However, new research from the University of Connecticut suggests that this widely available cannabis compound may have risks that consumers should know about.

Scientists found that repeated exposure to CBN caused mice to develop signs of physical dependence. The findings, published in the November issue of the journal Drug and Alcohol Dependence, raise questions about the safety of using CBN regularly, particularly over long periods.

"CBN is everywhere right now. Gummies, tinctures, capsules, and it's marketed as a safe, herbal sleep supplement," says Steven Kinsey, professor in UConn's Elisabeth DeLuca School of Nursing. "Our findings suggest consumers should be aware repeated use may carry risks."

Popular Sleep Aid Shows Effects Similar to THC

CBN is a naturally occurring compound in cannabis, along with THC (tetrahydrocannabinol) and CBD (cannabidiol). Although CBN is generally considered less psychoactive than THC, the researchers discovered that it affects some of the same biological systems and can produce several key signs associated with drug dependence.

In the experiments, mice received CBN twice a day for three consecutive days. As treatment continued, the animals became less responsive to the compound, indicating that they had developed tolerance. When CBN was withdrawn, the mice also displayed physical symptoms resembling withdrawal, including head twitching and paw fluttering. These movements are comparable to the shaking that people can experience during drug withdrawal.

Another experiment revealed a surprising connection between CBN and THC. Mice previously trained to recognize and respond to THC's psychoactive effects reacted similarly when given CBN. This suggests that the two compounds produce overlapping subjective effects, at least in mice.

Together, the results indicate that CBN, despite its reputation as a milder cannabis compound, interacts with some of the same receptor systems that contribute to THC dependence.

Scientists Raise Concerns About CBN Sleep Supplements

The findings are particularly relevant because CBN products have become increasingly common, even though scientists still have limited information about their long-term effects.

The lead author of the study, Olivia Vanegas '26 Ph.D., notes "The marketing of minor cannabinoids is pretty wild right now. There are so many therapeutic claims about these drugs, but we really don't know much about their potential risks, especially with repeated use."

CBN is now widely promoted as a sleep aid, yet important questions remain unanswered. Researchers do not fully understand how CBN affects sleep quality or whether taking it repeatedly at doses commonly found in supplements could lead to addiction in humans.

There is also a historical reason for caution. Over the past century, several medications initially promoted as helpful sleep treatments were eventually found to carry serious risks of dependence and habit formation.

Potential Benefits Come With Unanswered Safety Questions

The researchers also identified potentially useful properties of CBN. The compound demonstrated anti-inflammatory effects, including at lower doses that did not produce psychoactive effects.

However, the experiments revealed another possible concern. During a simulated bacterial challenge, CBN reduced the body's ability to defend itself. This weakening of immune protection could make it easier for an infection to develop, similar to how bacteria can enter through a cut on the foot and cause illness.

These findings suggest that CBN may have medical applications, but scientists need a clearer understanding of its benefits, potential side effects, and appropriate doses before determining how it can be used safely.

Importantly, the experiments were conducted in mice, so the findings do not establish that CBN causes dependence or withdrawal in people. Additional studies are needed to understand how the compound affects humans, especially when taken frequently, at higher doses, or over extended periods.

"CBN may still have therapeutic value," he says. "But until we know more about dosing and long-term effects, the message for consumers is simple: buyer beware."

#5791. Study Strengthens Case for Stent-Free PCI in De Novo Coronary Lesions

— Magic Touch drug-coated balloon was noninferior to drug-eluting stent

For de novo coronary lesions, drug-coated balloons (DCBs) were just as effective as standard drug-eluting stents, the TRANSFORM II trial showed.

Among patients with native coronary lesions undergoing percutaneous coronary intervention (PCI), the incidence of target lesion failure at 12 months -- including cardiac death, target vessel myocardial infarction (MI), and ischemia-driven target lesion revascularization -- was 2.1% with the Magic Touch sirolimus DCB and 2.4% with standard everolimus drug-eluting stents (DES; HR 0.86, 95% CI 0.47-1.59).

The DCB thus met prespecified criteria for noninferiority (P=0.004), reported Bernardo Cortese, MD, MSc, of Fondazione Ricerca e Innovazione Cardiovascolare in Milan, at the Transcatheter Cardiovascular Therapeutics annual conference hosted by the Cardiovascular Research Foundation.

"All of this put into the context of the available literature and the SELUTION De Novo clinical trial, I can conclude that the DCB approach is a safe and effective alternative to stents in a selected population of de novo lesions, but bear in mind that there is no class effect for drug-coated balloons," Cortese said.

The Magic Touch is a DCB designed to release sirolimus and have it stay in the arterial wall for 8 to 12 weeks. The appeal of a DCB is that, unlike DES, the standard of care for native coronary artery disease, no permanent metal is left behind during PCI.

DCBs are already established as a treatment for coronary in-stent restenosis, an indication for which paclitaxel- (the Agent DCB) and sirolimus-eluting DCB models (the Selution SLR) are available on the market. The evidence is more shaky for DCBs in native coronary artery disease, especially following the negative REC-CAGEFREE I trial, though in last year's SELUTION De Novo trial, the Selution DCB was shown to be noninferior to DES for rates of target lesion failure.

Of note, though rates of clinically driven target lesion revascularization were comparable between groups in TRANSFORM II, rates of any target lesion revascularization were higher in the DCB group (2.5% vs 1.1%, HR 2.31, 95% CI 1.10-4.85).

One theory, noted Cortese, is that physicians in this open-label trial may have tended to re-treat patients if they had subpar angiographic results after DCB therapy, whereas they may have been more conservative if they saw stents in the picture. "But the numbers were very low, so this is very, very speculative," he cautioned.

Besides the concern for target lesion revascularization, there is also the question of costs.

Session co-chair Ajay Kirtane, MD, of NewYork-Presbyterian/Columbia University Irving Medical Center in New York City, questioned if cath labs will be willing to switch to DCBs if the listing prices are about $6,000, compared with the $300 to $400 for DES.

"Financial reimbursements are still a big issue for centers, and I think it will continue to be, and particularly as we get farther along, and all the Medicare mandates end. So I think we'll have to wait and see," said panelist Jennifer Tremmel, MD, of Stanford University Medical Center in Palo Alto, California. "We're going to have to decide who gets DCB, who gets DES, when do they get it, and the financials are going to make a difference. So I'm curious to see how things go over the long term, and ultimately which patients we decide to use this in first."

TRANSFORM II was an open-label multicenter trial conducted in Europe, Asia, and Brazil. Participants had to have native coronary lesions with reference vessel diameters measuring 2.0 to 3.5 mm and lesion lengths no greater than 50 mm.

Following successful vessel preparation, patients were randomized 1:1 to the Magic Touch sirolimus DCB or the everolimus DES. Out of 2,699 people screened, 2,223 met criteria for entry, and 1,842 had successful lesion preparation and were ultimately randomized.

Mean age was about 63 years, and most patients were men. Initial presentation was roughly split between acute coronary syndrome and chronic coronary syndrome. Over 35% had diabetes, and about one in three had a prior MI. Approximately 30% had a high bleeding risk.

Lesions were type B2/C in about two in three cases. Bailout stenting was required in 7.6% of the DCB group.

Cortese reported that the minimum lumen diameter at baseline was about 0.92 mm, and in-device acute lumen gain averaged 0.76 mm with DCB and 1.30 mm with DES. On quantitative coronary angiography, the percent diameter stenosis at baseline was about 59%, and this dropped to 31% after lesion preparation. After PCI, the in-device percent diameter stenosis was 26.8% with DCB and 12.3% with DES. The proportion of patients with in-device residual stenosis <30% was 61.2% with DCB and 97.9% with DES.

Regarding the primary endpoint, intention-to-treat results were consistent with the per-protocol analysis, according to Cortese. Individual components of target lesion failure were also generally favorable with DCB:

A secondary endpoint, net adverse clinical events (all-cause mortality, any MI, ischemic stroke, or major bleeding), was not significantly different between the DCB and DES arms (4.1% vs 5.3%, HR 0.77, 95% CI 0.50-1.17).

Cortese stressed that these results do not apply to lesions with unsuccessful lesion preparation and excluded groups (patients with severe calcification, highly tortuous vessels, and left main or vein graft lesions; ST-segment elevation MI within 48 hours; previous PCI in the target vessel; complex bifurcation lesions; and ejection fractions below 30%, among others).

Among other study limitations, event rates were much lower than expected, and the results may have limited generalizability given that the operators were all from experienced DCB centers.

As for the question of whether DCBs might eventually reach superiority over DES for native coronary lesions, Cortese said this will require long-term data, and his group does plan to continue patient follow-up out to 5 years.

"We are expecting to see fewer events starting from year 3, like we have seen with paclitaxel-coated balloons in a patient-level meta-analysis," he said.

Quality Score is calculated based on three primary components: click-through rate (CTR), ad relevance, and landing page experience.

That said, keep in mind that a high-Quality Score is not an end in itself. It's a sign that your ads are relevant, and your landing pages provide a good user experience, but it's still just one factor in the overall success of your Google Ads campaigns.

#5789. This mysterious mushroom makes people see hundreds of tiny humans

People living thousands of miles apart have reported an extraordinarily strange experience after eating certain wild mushrooms. They begin seeing tiny human figures that appear to move through the real world, walking around objects, climbing over surfaces, and interacting with their surroundings.

These visions are known as Lilliputian hallucinations, named after the miniature inhabitants of "Gulliver's Travels," who stand just 6 inches tall. For decades, researchers suspected that cultural beliefs and folklore might explain why people in different regions described such similar experiences.

Now, a new study provides evidence that the phenomenon has a biological explanation.

Researchers at the University of Utah (U) used DNA sequencing to identify Lanmaoa asiatica as the mushroom responsible for these unusual hallucinations in Southwest China and the northern Philippines. Surprisingly, their investigation found no evidence of any known psychoactive mushroom compounds, including psilocybin.

The findings suggest that the mushroom may produce an entirely unfamiliar hallucinogenic substance. Identifying that chemical could help scientists better understand neurological disorders, unusual visual experiences, and the processes through which the brain creates our perception of reality.

The research was conducted by Colin Domnauer, a doctoral student at the U, and Bryn Dentinger, a mycologist affiliated with the university and the Natural History Museum of Utah. Their findings were published in the journal Mycologia.

In an interview with @theU, Domnauer explained how researchers traced the mushroom's identity, what makes its effects so unusual, and why the discovery could have implications far beyond the study of fungi.

A Century-Old Mystery of Tiny Human Hallucinations

The scientific mystery dates back to 1934, when Western researchers learned that people in Papua New Guinea sometimes experienced vivid visions of miniature humans after eating wild mushrooms. The bizarre effect became known as "mushroom madness."

During the 1950s and 1960s, anthropologists and mushroom specialists traveled to Papua New Guinea to investigate. Their work pointed toward several possible species belonging to the Boletaceae (bolete) family.

However, chemical testing failed to reveal any substances that could explain the hallucinations. Samples were even examined by Albert Hofmann, the scientist who first isolated psilocybin and discovered LSD. He, too, was unable to identify an active ingredient.

With no chemical explanation available, some researchers began questioning whether the mushrooms were responsible at all. They suggested that the visions might instead arise from cultural expectations or traditional beliefs. By the end of the 1960s, scientific investigation of the phenomenon had largely stopped.

The mystery resurfaced during the 1990s, when people in Yunnan, Southwest China, reported similar hallucinations after eating bolete mushrooms. Although the precise species remained uncertain, researchers suspected a recently described mushroom called Lanmaoa asiatica.

Scientists Identify the Mushroom Behind the Strange Visions

Another important clue appeared in 2024, when Domnauer learned of comparable experiences among an Indigenous community in the remote Northern Cordillera region of the Philippines.

Community members described a wild edible mushroom called "Sedesdem" that could sometimes cause people to see miniature human figures. Similar mushrooms were known locally as "Nonda" in Papua New Guinea and "Jian shou qing" in Yunnan.

These mushrooms have an important place in local food traditions, but consuming them without thoroughly cooking them can trigger the unusual hallucinations.

The Philippine accounts represented a third independent report of wild mushrooms causing remarkably similar visions of tiny people.

To investigate, Domnauer traveled to Yunnan and the Philippines, interviewed local residents, and collected specimens of L. asiatica along with other mushrooms belonging to the Lanmaoa genus.

The fieldwork included the first scientific survey of fungi in the northern Philippines. Researchers then analyzed DNA from the collected mushrooms to establish their identities and evolutionary relationships.

The results revealed that the mushrooms called Jian shou qing in China and Sedesdem in the Philippines were both L. asiatica.

This was an unexpected finding because scientists had previously believed the species occurred only in China.

Dentinger also visited Papua New Guinea in hopes of collecting specimens from the location where the mystery originally began. Unfortunately, unusually dry weather made it impossible to obtain samples for genetic comparison.

Although the Papua New Guinea mushroom remains unverified, the evidence from China and the Philippines provides a compelling connection. Three geographically separated cultures have described the same highly specific hallucinations, and DNA testing has linked two of those cases to a single mushroom species.

That consistency strongly suggests a shared chemical and neurological explanation rather than unrelated cultural influences.

Hallucinations of Tiny Humans That Seem Remarkably Real

What makes these hallucinations especially unusual is their striking similarity across different accounts.

People typically describe seeing dozens or even hundreds of miniature figures, generally measuring between 3 and 30 centimeters tall. The figures appear with extraordinary clarity, often seeming as solid and convincing as actual people.

According to Domnauer, approximately 90% of those experiencing the visions describe figures resembling elves, clowns, or other fairy-like characters, often wearing brightly colored clothing.

Even more surprising is the way these imaginary figures seem to behave.

Rather than floating through walls or ignoring their surroundings, they appear to follow familiar physical rules. They walk around obstacles, move across ordinary objects, and sometimes tumble from the edges of tables.

Domnauer recalled one particularly unusual account involving someone eating soup. The person reported seeing tiny humans leap from a spoon into the bowl, where they began swimming. When the person took another spoonful, the miniature figures reportedly remained visible inside their mouth.

Such detailed interactions distinguish these visions from many other forms of drug-induced hallucination.

A Mysterious Psychedelic Compound Unlike Any Known Drug

Scientists also investigated whether L. asiatica contains any of the chemicals already known to cause psychedelic experiences.

Using both chemical testing and genetic analysis, the researchers searched for established psychoactive compounds found in other mushroom species.

The absence of familiar hallucinogens was particularly intriguing because the mushroom's effects differ so dramatically from those associated with known psychedelic drugs.

Researchers now suspect that L. asiatica produces an unidentified psychoactive substance responsible for the tiny human hallucinations.

The compound has not yet been isolated or chemically identified, so its exact nature and mechanism remain unknown.

Discovering it could open an entirely new avenue of research into how certain chemicals alter visual perception.

What the Mushroom Could Reveal About the Human Brain

Although L. asiatica provides an unusually consistent trigger for Lilliputian hallucinations, the phenomenon is not limited to mushroom consumption.

Stories involving miniature humanlike beings appear throughout folklore around the world. Similar hallucinations have also been documented in people experiencing alcohol withdrawal, dementia, macular degeneration, and other neurological conditions.

These connections suggest that the visions may arise from a fundamental feature of the way the human brain processes visual information.

However, scientists still do not fully understand the neurological mechanisms responsible for these experiences or how best to treat them when they occur in medical settings.

This is where the mysterious mushroom could become especially valuable.

Unlike the neurological and medical conditions in which Lilliputian hallucinations occur relatively rarely, L. asiatica can produce the phenomenon in a more consistent and recognizable way.

If researchers can isolate the chemical responsible, they may gain a useful tool for investigating the brain circuits and processes that create these unusually detailed visual experiences.

Such research could eventually improve scientific understanding of hallucinations associated with neurological diseases.

The research team's next major objective is to isolate and identify the suspected psychedelic compound.

DNA Sequencing Reveals Four Previously Unknown Mushroom Species

The investigation also produced important discoveries about fungal biodiversity.

Rather than studying L. asiatica alone, Domnauer and Dentinger conducted the first comprehensive genomic analysis of the Lanmaoa genus.

They examined 53 mushroom specimens collected from natural habitats and preserved in fungarium collections around the world.

Genetic sequencing revealed 17 species within the group, including four species previously unknown to science.

Remarkably, two of those newly identified species had already been preserved in existing collections in the United States. Their distinct identities had gone unrecognized until researchers examined their DNA.

The analysis also indicated that L. asiatica is the only species in the genus currently known to produce psychoactive effects.

Scientists are now working to reconstruct the group's evolutionary history. They hope to determine how the unusual chemical trait developed and whether closely related mushrooms might produce comparable substances.

The findings also demonstrate the scientific value of museum collections. Specimens preserved for years can contain biological discoveries that become apparent only when newer analytical techniques are applied.

For Domnauer, the hidden diversity found in these collections is a reminder that many species and potentially important biological characteristics remain undiscovered, even among organisms that scientists have already collected.

Exploring Fungi Could Lead to New Medicines and Discoveries

The broader implications extend well beyond unusual hallucinations.

Fungi remain one of the least thoroughly explored groups of organisms, and researchers believe their chemical diversity could provide valuable clues about biology, medicine, and the nervous system.

One of the most famous examples is penicillin, the antibiotic originally obtained from a fungus that transformed the treatment of bacterial infections and became one of the most consequential medical discoveries of the last century.

Scientists hope continued exploration of fungal biodiversity could reveal other medically useful compounds and previously unknown biological processes.

The mysterious effects of L. asiatica illustrate just how much remains to be learned about the fungal kingdom. A mushroom long consumed by communities in Asia may contain a substance capable of revealing new information about the human brain and how it creates conscious experience.

The research received funding from the National Science Foundation (award #DEB-2114785).

Materials provided by University of Utah. Original written by Lisa Potter. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#5788. Novel Therapy Lands Speedy Approval for Wart-Like Airway Disease

— Plasmid DNA-based immunotherapy reduced surgery need in recurrent respiratory papillomatosis

The FDA granted accelerated approval to doruxapapogene ralaplasmid and lalikinogene sifuplasmid (Voceryq) to reduce the frequency of surgical papilloma excisions in adults with recurrent respiratory papillomatosis (RRP), the agency announced late on Friday.

As the first FDA-approved recombinant plasmid DNA-based immunotherapy, doruxapapogene ralaplasmid-lalikinogene sifuplasmid targets the wart-like growths that characterize RRP. The growths are caused by human papillomavirus (HPV) and develop in the airways, particularly around the larynx. Severe cases of the rare disease can spread into the respiratory tract and make breathing difficult. There is no cure, but repeated surgeries can remove growths if they return.

FDA based its decision on efficacy data from RRP-001, a phase I/II clinical trial. The open-label trial included 32 patients who'd undergone at least two surgeries in the prior year to remove RRP growths. Among 26 patients who had at least three surgeries in the 12 months before treatment, 27% had a complete response to the plasmid therapy, defined as requiring no surgical excisions during 12 months of follow-up.

"The approval of Voceryq is an important step in improving the quality of life for RRP patients as every surgery carries the risk of laryngeal injury," said investigator Simon Best, MD, of Johns Hopkins University in Baltimore, in a statement from manufacturer Inovio Pharmaceuticals. "I believe it is in the best interest of RRP patients that the standard of care quickly transition away from surgical interventions to immunotherapy after RRP is diagnosed."

Doruxapapogene ralaplasmid-lalikinogene sifuplasmid includes two plasmids that contain genetic coding for HPV proteins and interleukin 12. Patients' own healthy cells transcribe that coding to stimulate immune system attacks against cells infected with the HPV types 6 and 11 that cause RRP.

The therapy's treatment regimen begins with surgical debulking of visible papilloma, followed by an intramuscular dose of the plasmid therapy every 3 weeks for a total of four doses. Label warnings include potential adverse events (AEs) such as muscle contractions, transient bleeding, and vasovagal reactions. The most common AEs in trials included injection site reactions, headache, fatigue, hypertension, nausea, and dizziness.

The new treatment was approved in combination with a proprietary DNA medicine delivery device (CELLECTRA 5PSP). Postmarketing trials will be required to verify and describe the therapy's clinical benefit.

The FDA approved the immunotherapy zopapogene imadenovec (Papzimeos) for RRP in 2025, the first approved medical therapy for the condition.

#5787. Industry Voices—The other AI divide is already opening in healthcare

Industry Voices—The other AI divide is already opening in healthcare" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The debate over AI and inequality has largely focused on people—who will have access to the technology, who will benefit from it and who risks being left behind. But inside healthcare, another AI divide is already taking shape. This one is between the hospitals that have the money, talent and infrastructure to put AI to work and those that don't.

The numbers are striking. According to federal data on hospital AI adoption, 71% of U.S. hospitals used predictive AI integrated with their electronic health records in 2024. But adoption reached 96% among large hospitals compared with just 59% among small hospitals. Among hospitals belonging to multi-hospital systems, 86% used predictive AI. Among independent hospitals, it was only 37%.

That gap matters because AI is quickly becoming part of how hospitals survive, not simply an innovation project. The same federal data show some of the fastest-growing hospital AI applications are decidedly practical, including billing and scheduling. These are exactly the kinds of labor-intensive functions where financially stressed hospitals need to find efficiencies.

Yet those hospitals may be the least equipped to make the investment.

Hospitals remain extraordinarily dependent on people to operate. According to the American Hospital Association, workforce spending accounted for about 60% of hospital expenses in 2025, exceeding $1 trillion. Total hospital expenses grew 7.5% that year, more than twice the growth in hospital prices.

This creates a dangerous catch-22. The hospitals with the greatest need to use technology to redesign labor-intensive operations frequently have the least capacity to invest in doing so.

A large health system can afford an AI pilot that fails. It can hire an innovation team, absorb a lengthy implementation, build governance and compliance infrastructure, and try again if the economics don't work. A safety-net, community or independent hospital often has far less room for error. A multi-year software contract that might eventually produce savings still requires money today.

And buying the technology is only the beginning.

Successful AI deployment requires workflow redesign, change management, integration, measurement and governance. Even among hospitals already using predictive AI, the federal data show meaningful variation in how comprehensively models are evaluated for accuracy, bias and performance after implementation. Smaller, rural and independent hospitals also continue to lag their larger counterparts in adoption.

If we leave the market to work this out one hospital at a time, we should expect that divide to compound. Systems that successfully automate expensive workflows can reinvest those savings into their next technology investment. They build institutional knowledge. Their employees get better at implementation. They become more attractive partners to technology companies looking for sophisticated customers and proving grounds.

The hospitals that wait don't simply remain where they are. They lose ground. The answer cannot be telling every hospital to develop a better AI strategy. We need to change the ownership and financing model for healthcare AI.

Hospitals already know how to collaborate when acting collectively creates an advantage. Group purchasing organizations allow them to aggregate purchasing power rather than negotiate for every product independently. Clinical research networks allow institutions to pool patients, expertise and infrastructure to answer questions no single organization could address as effectively alone.

We should apply the same instinct to AI. Instead of requiring every health system to independently buy, implement and govern variations of the same technology, health systems should increasingly pool their purchasing power, operational expertise and capital to build common AI capabilities. And smaller, independent and safety-net organizations need a path into those collaborations rather than simply another vendor contract.

The venture community has a responsibility here as well. We need financing structures that acknowledge the economic reality of the organizations that really need to adopt technologies. That could mean performance-based models where payment is tied to verified savings rather than large upfront commitments. It could mean consortium models in which multiple health systems share implementation resources and governance.

And increasingly, it should mean giving health systems ownership in the companies and infrastructure they help create. Ownership matters because the economics of AI are changing quickly. As the technology itself becomes increasingly commoditized, enduring value will come from distribution, proprietary workflows, real-world implementation knowledge and access to the environments where healthcare actually happens. Health systems contribute enormously to each of those things. Under the traditional vendor model, however, they can provide the workflows, clinical expertise and proving ground and then pay someone else an annual fee for the resulting product.

That bargain deserves to be reconsidered. Collective ownership can help address the trust problem, too. AI adoption isn't only a technology decision. It requires decisions about validation, monitoring, clinical safety and who is accountable when something goes wrong. Federal researchers found that hospitals increasingly rely on multiple parties to evaluate AI, with 74% reporting more than one entity accountable for evaluation. Smaller hospitals should not each have to recreate that infrastructure from scratch.

Shared infrastructure could include common validation standards, clinical oversight, monitoring and implementation expertise. That would give more hospitals access not only to AI, but to the capabilities required to deploy it responsibly.

None of this means every hospital needs to own every AI company, or that traditional vendors disappear. It means we should stop assuming that the only scalable model for healthcare innovation is one technology company selling essentially the same capability to thousands of hospitals one contract at a time.

The AI divide between hospitals is already here. If adoption continues to depend primarily on the size of an organization's balance sheet, the institutions best positioned today will keep accumulating advantages while those with the least room for error fall further behind.

AI gives hospitals a real opportunity to break their dependence on increasingly expensive, labor-intensive ways of operating. But the organizations that need that opportunity most cannot be the last ones able to afford it. Preventing that outcome will require more than making AI cheaper. It will require giving more health systems the ability to build, govern and share in the technology's value, transforming them.

AI should become something hospitals build together, not another advantage reserved for those that could already afford to move first.

Amanda DeMano is SVP of venture creation at Abundant Venture Partners.

Editor's Note: Abundant Venture Partners is a strategic investor in the makers of certain AI technologies, including SpendRule and Playback Health.

#5786. What does a blue Texas sweep mean for Tesla, SpaceX & Elon Musk?

After a historic election defeat in one of the most expensive elections in US election history, the state of Texas has turned blue. Along with this come potential implications for Elon Musk and his companies.

The US midterm election was yesterday, and ended up in a stunning result, with Democrats taking control of both houses of Congress by wide margins, and flipping seats that had been considered safe around the nation.

Perhaps most notably, many of these seats were in Texas – a republican stronghold which saw a clean sweep, with the Democrats winning every statewide contest. Republicans maintained some state legislative power and won the State Senate, but as of this writing Democrats are currently ahead in the count for the State House.

The highest profile contest was for Texas Senate, where James Talarico won by a 55-43 margin. This race set the record for the most expensive Senate race in the nation’s history, with over half a billion dollars spent on this one seat alone.

About half of the total money spent on the race came courtesy of republican PACs, a method by which companies and the ultra-wealthy can launder money into bribes to buy elections. And most of the PAC money in the race came from one man, Elon Musk, who spent $212 million to support the enormously unpopular and scandal-ridden outgoing Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton

That’s nearly half of the $474 million dollars that Musk put into this election cycle, suggesting that he saw this race of particular importance. And yet, his candidate lost miserably.

But the rout did not stop with Paxton. Paxton’s old job as AG is now going to be filled by Democrat Nathan Johnson. And Greg Abbott, the outgoing Governor, was defeated by Gina Hinojosa. Even the less high-profile races for court seats were swept by Democrats – three seats on the 15th Court of Appeals and four seats on the Texas Supreme Court.

And those results, even beyond the big-spending Senate race, could all matter quite a lot to Elon Musk.

In 2021, Musk announced that Tesla would move its headquarters to Texas, after Musk got in a spat with California government because he didn’t want to follow COVID restrictions for worker safety.

But the move wasn’t only about COVID restrictions – it was also about Musk’s vision of having more control over the business environment. And, by making allies with Ken Paxton and Greg Abbott, he has had quite a bit of control in Texas.

The two have rolled out the red carpet for Musk, giving him carte blanche in his adopted state.

Autonomous vehicle regulations have been changed, allowing driverless Robotaxis to operate where other states with more advanced regulations and transparency requirements would not. And many other laws benefitting Tesla and SpaceX have been passed. Paxton even sued Sam Altman, Musk’s chief AI rival, just weeks ago.

Perhaps more notably for Musk personally, Texas has promised to allow Musk to get away with more financial shenanigans than states with more established business regulations might.

With Tesla both headquartered and incorporated in Texas, it can now use special “business courts” instituted by Abbott at Musk’s own urging. If cases from these courts are appealed, they go to the 15th Court of Appeals, a court created by Abbott and filled with 3 hand-picked judges, intended to reduce the chance that a Democratic district court (say, one in Austin, whereTesla is headquartered) gets final say on any issue.

But, the 15th Court of Appeals just had elections last night – and its three seats were flipped to Democratic control.

The 15th Court of Appeals also, just last week, stopped an inquiry into thousands of pages of emails between Abbott and Musk. The emails had been released, but are heavily redacted, with Abbott referring to the redacted portions as having “intimate” information in them.

And who made that request for inquiry into the emails? Gina Hinojosa, a name you might recognize, as she is the next Governor of Texas, as of last night.

As of yet, those emails have not all been released, so we don’t actually know exactly how much influence Musk was able to exercise over the state’s outgoing Governor. But given the changes we know about, the money Musk spent in Texas, and the thousands of pages of emails that have been redacted, it seems likely that there is something more to be found in them.

So what does this all mean for Tesla, our main area of coverage here at Electrek?

On a Texas state level, probably not all that much. There’s no reason that Democrats in the state would interfere with Tesla’s production of electric vehicles – in fact, they’d probably encourage it more than republicans do. Talarico has stated that he supports Tesla – it is a large employer in the state, after all.

And certainly, the national wins by Democrats could be a boon for EVs, given how hostile republicans have been to them (and Musk has been the single largest supporter of that hostility towards EVs).

The same likely goes for SpaceX – perhaps a blue Texas will be more restrictive of the company’s improper usage of protected habitats, or building of data centers, or whatever the hell is going on on twitter. But the core businesses of space launches and Starlink don’t seem likely to go anywhere.

For Elon Musk, though, things could be different. Given his “intimate” relationship with Abbott, the extreme amounts of money he spent on Paxton, and the highly personal nature of his truly ridiculous attacks against Talarico, it seems unlikely that he will have a similar level of allyship with Texas government actors as he did in prior years.

And given his high levels of spending on a party that is outright hostile electric vehicles, it seems clear that his personal fortunes are far more important to him than the well-being of his companies.

This is all important to note, because Musk has been working towards a scheme to merge SpaceX and Tesla, a deal which would likely be a detriment to both companies and all of their shareholders. Musk wants the deal so he can exercise more control over, and extract more personal wealth from, the combined entity.

But that scheme could rely on a favorable legal environment in Texas courts, an environment that just got less favorable.

And, given Musk’s penchant for childish lashings-out anytime he deems people insufficiently obsequious to his imagined greatness, could we see another situation where Musk encounters the tiniest amount of pushback from state government and then picks up his toys and leaves, as he already did in both California and Delaware? Time will have to tell. Keep your popcorn buckets primed and ready.

#5785. Low-dose aspirin linked to 70% lower dementia risk, but there's a catch

A new analysis from Monash University suggests that low-dose aspirin may be linked to a dramatically lower risk of dementia in older adults with certain genetic characteristics. Researchers found that people with a specific genetic profile who took aspirin had a 70 percent lower relative risk of developing dementia, raising the possibility that genetic testing could eventually help identify people who might benefit from preventive treatments.

The findings, published in Alzheimer's & Dementia: The Journal of the Alzheimer's Association, were based on genetic data from more than 13,500 participants in the landmark ASPREE Trial (Aspirin in Reducing Events in the Elderly). Scientists examined whether differences in participants' genes could help explain why aspirin might affect dementia risk differently from one person to another.

Aspirin Linked to 70% Lower Dementia Risk in Certain People

The most striking results involved genes associated with platelet count. Platelets are small components of the blood that help form clots, and researchers wanted to determine whether genetic variations related to platelet levels could influence aspirin's potential effects on dementia.

After assigning participants genetic scores associated with platelet count, the team compared dementia rates among those who received aspirin and those given a placebo.

Among people in the highest 20 percent of platelet count genetic scores, just 1 percent of aspirin users developed dementia, compared with 3.3 percent of participants taking a placebo. That difference translates to approximately a 70 percent lower relative risk of dementia among those receiving aspirin.

However, the apparent benefit was accompanied by a significant safety concern. In the same genetic group, 4.4 percent of aspirin users experienced major bleeding, compared with 2.1 percent of those receiving a placebo.

A Genetic Clue Could Change Dementia Prevention

Lead author Dr. Peter Fransquet, a Research Fellow at Monash's School of Public Health and Preventive Medicine, said the results suggest that a person's genetic makeup could eventually play an important role in determining which dementia prevention strategies are most appropriate.

"Previous trials found aspirin didn't prevent dementia when everyone was considered together," Dr. Fransquet said.

"Our findings suggest there may be more to the story.

"In people with this particular genetic profile, we saw substantially fewer cases of dementia among those taking aspirin.

"It raises the possibility that genetics could one day help us identify who may benefit from a preventive treatment, rather than taking a one-size-fits-all approach."

The possibility of more targeted prevention is especially important as dementia becomes increasingly common. Dementia Australia estimates that approximately 446,500 people in Australia are living with the condition in 2026. That number is expected to exceed one million by 2065.

Blood Platelets Reveal an Unexpected Dementia Connection

Previous research has connected dementia with platelet activation and aggregation, the processes through which platelets become active and stick together. However, scientists have had a less complete understanding of how a person's overall platelet count might relate to dementia.

The new findings point toward platelet biology as a potentially important area of investigation. Notably, the genetic association identified by researchers did not involve the well-established genetic risk factors typically linked to Alzheimer's disease and other forms of dementia.

"What's particularly interesting is that the signal wasn't linked to the established genetic risk factors for Alzheimer's disease and dementia," Dr. Fransquet said.

"Instead, it's pointing us towards platelet biology and gives us a new avenue to investigate."

Why More Research Is Needed Before Taking Aspirin

Although the findings suggest that aspirin could have different effects depending on a person's genes, researchers emphasize that the results must be confirmed before they can guide medical recommendations.

The next step is to investigate whether the association appears in other populations. Scientists would then need to conduct a clinical trial specifically involving people with the genetic profile identified in the analysis.

"We now need to confirm the finding in other studies and ultimately test it in a trial designed specifically for people with this genetic profile.

"People shouldn't start taking aspirin to prevent dementia on the basis of this study without consulting with their doctor, particularly given the increased risk of serious bleeding.

"If it holds up, the fact that aspirin is already cheap and widely available could make personalized dementia prevention a real possibility."

Materials provided by Monash University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
